Lanma should be brief and pointed., Their object must be to impart or seek information, not abuse.

SEVERAL LETTERS received very late in the week are held over till next Saturday.

SPIRITISM AND ROMANISH. , Sir,—Father Buggins seems annoyed, at my .- introducing the Maid of Orleans; but let me ! point out that she had already been introduced, • , and I simply entered the correspondence to ;. place on record a protest against the views of both parties to the argument. The Rev. Father surely will admit that, 0 whether the persecutors of the Maid of Orleans . " were English or French, they bore titles con- ferred upon them'by the 'Roman Church, and were high up in that Church's service. The Cardinal of Winchester, the Bishop of Beauvais, and Jean Lemaitre, vicar-general of the In- quisition, were all active official members of the Roman Church; therefore- whatever infamy attaches to the execution of the Maid of Orleans lies at the door of that Church. In regard to Mdlle. de la Merliere's masquerad- ing as the Virgin at La Salette and elsewhere, I rather prefer my authority than that of the Rev. Father; I must refer him to chap. x. of Francis Ilitchman's "Biography of Pius IX." There is a larger subject that Father Buggins develops in his letter—" The Infallible Church." The only Infallible Church that I know of is the Church Triumphant—the Church of the Blessed around the Throne of God. The Church on earth or the Church Militant is -not infal- lible, but is mortal and sinful. The only per- fect guide to man is the Word of God, which ought to be open to all to read, study and obey. Had the Church to which Father Buggins claims to belong been an Infallible Church, and its head an Infallible Guide, we should have seen through the past -ages a glorious lustre of learning and divinity that would have shone through all time, but it has displayed the re- verse of this. The Roman Church soon began to sow her tares among the goodly Christian wheat of the Gospel. The Word of God gave place to the traditions of man, and things went on until the States began to rebel, and we find in a speech delivered by Duke George of Saxony before the assembled princes at the Diet of Worms, and preserved in the archives of Weimar, a demand for reform and a graphic description of the state of the Rev. Father's Infallible Church at an interesting epoch of its history.

QUAKERISM AND NATIONAL DEFENCE

Sir,—W illyou permit me to thank Mr. Ash- worth for his very courteous reply, and also to make one or two further observations?

My object in writing was not to oppose the proposals of the N.S.L., or to question the sin- cerity of its adherents, but rather to take ex- ception to the methods of trying to justify the League's existence by the utterances of men living in an age which, from the point of view of militarism, required a totally different policy from that needed to-day. If the experience of men like Lord Roberts and other-leading lights of the N.S.L. leaas them to conclude that the best way to avoid war is to be prepared for it, and that the N.S.L. method of training is the best that can, at present, be devised, then I suggest that every Leaguer should make that the basis for argument. Again, if, owing ,to the aggressive policy of Germany, we are reluctantly compelled to re- sort to compulsory training, why does not the N.S.L. boldly state the fact that Christianity has failed in its attempt to enthrone the Prince of Peace in the hearts of men, and discourage the practice of quoting irrelevant passages of Scripture? But for the seriousness of the situation one could smile. Think of it! On the coming Sab- bath the representatives of the meek and lowly Jesus will be exhorting the people of Germany and England to "forgive them that trespass against us," but, alas! for the remainder of the week we shall be deeply engrossed in studying the best methods of keeping the other Christian brother at bay! This is in very truth hipo- - kriteco. • JAS. HAMPSON., 92, Oldham-street.

Sir,—Either muddled thought or wilful per- version characterise Mr. T. R. Ashworth's letter on this subject. I certainly never stated "that Isaac Penington and George Fox were ready to stand up to defend their ovtn country in their day." Isaac Penington's pamphlet was wholly to explain his own attitude and that of his friends in finding such a course contrary to the, spirit of Christ, and George Fox refused a commission in the Parliainentary Army because he had come to lead people qut of the spirit that caused war. At the same time Isaac Pen- ington admitted, and I • also admit, • that ad- vanced teaching of this kind could not be adopted by the GoVernment to-morrow. That is all.

Mr. Townroe also says that I Omitted that many of the original Quakers serVed in Crom- well's army. He-thus perverts the wEole point. Many of them had served in that army before they were convinced of Quakerism. Did Mr. Townroe ever suppose that we were against all strife? Strife is the salt and the spice of life, and there must be few good men in the country who have not much evil to strive against. Only utter confusion of thought can muddle noble with ignoble strife. But I am not after all the only combative person. Does not Mr. Townroe feel it to be risky for the pot to call the kettle black? Let everyone remember my. principal contention, that the National Service League is ziot for the strengthening of home defence at all, but for a New Home Army, enabling us to liberate the Regulars for Continental warfare. It is only one of the many forms of military ex- penditure and political reaction which are now besetting the democracy, largely in the inter- ests of the armament firms, whose shareholders -are strongly represented among the leaders of the National Service League. Sir Andrew Noble, chairman of Armstrong, Whitworth and Co.; Sir Hallowell Rogers, chairman of the Birmingham Small Arms Co.; Mr. Neville Chamberlain, shareholder in Kynocles, Ltd. • Major-Gen-eral Nicholson, chairman of the i Guncotiim Powder Co.; Lord Glenconner, chairman, of the Tharsis Sulphur Co.; Mr. Sam Roberts, M.P., director of Cam- mell, Laird and Co.; General Brackenbury, director of Hadfield Steel Foundry, Ltd.; Sir Vincent Caillard, director of Vickers, Ltd.; and Mr. B. A. Firth, chairman of Thos. Firth and Sons, Ltd.; are all members of the National Service League; and Lord Curzon, one of the mostl active vice-presidents of the League, who is speaking on their behalf in Birmingham this week, is a considerable shareholder in one of the above firms. . All these businesses depend for their success on war scares and on war. It is odd that they should support so heartily a League whose object is said to be the abolition of war and panic. JOHN W. GRAHAM.

A NOVEL TEMPERANCE SUGGESTION.

Sir,—Could you find room in your valuable paper for a few words on the Temperance ques- tion? Many of our churches have been hold- ing their Temperance Sundays recently, and a few more words on the subject will not be out of place. I have always been interested in our Bands of Hope and total abstinence societies of every kind, though I have not taken an active, part in any of them. There is one part of': their programme or method of operation in which I can lieartry enter, namely, the persuasive part, but I could never see the wisdom or sense Of justice in try- ing to get shortlbr hours, Sunday closing, reduc- tion of licences, or in any way interfering with the trade. It seems to me too nnwh like the Ulster minority business or an other minority, which strives to dictate to the whole. As a class we total abstainers are sadly in the minority at present, and I don't think it does our cause very much good. • In fact, I believe it creates a more strenuous opposition from the moderator. But as a rule, minorities of any kind receive very scant treatment at the hands of a majority. Now I think the better plan would be to try and secure for ourselves and any subsequent ab- stainers, a portion of all our towns and cities where we could live quite away from any public drinking , place, and also where our families could be reared and educated quite away from these things, for which we have no use.

The police, the doctor, and even the rent col- lector could visit us occasionally to keep us straight, and I am quite sure that the moderator would feel greatly obliged to us for clearing out, for I presume we should almost have to "get back to the land."

.T. W. MEALOR.

Bradfield Green, Crewe.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
DRINK AND INSANITY.

Sir,—ln his attack on Canon Willis and the local breweries, on Temperance Sunday, the Rev. H. Ogden said that drink was responsible for a considerable part of the lunacy in our country. The statement is one frequently heard from teetotal platforms. It is interesting to note that the most recent research and information do not by any means support this oft-repeated statement. Dr. J. F. Gemmel, medical superintendent, Lancashire County Asylum, in a recent report, says: "I have frequently alluded to drink as an alleged cause of insanity. My experience goes to show that in many cases it is the effect, and not the cause, of insanity, the intemperance being but a prodromus of commencing, if not actually ex- isting insanity." Dr. Sullivan and other medical experts have shown by careful analysis and tables the regional distribution of insanity, from which it is seen that Lancashire, Warwickshire and Cheshire, which stand very high in the scale of alcoholism, are alike in showing com- parativgly very low rates of insanity. Further, at the recent International Congress of Medicine Sir James Crichton Browne stated that the number of lunatics was increasing out of pro- portion to the increase of population in nearly all settled countries, from which trustworthy information was forthcoming, while the dimin- ished consumption of alcohol and the increased sobriety of the people ought to have been fol- lowed by a reduction in the number of lunatics.

When teetotalers have learned to distinguish between cause and effect, they will modify or retract many of the rash statements now made with regard to drink. November 19th, 1913.

EDW.. TUCKER.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
NEED FOR RAISING THE SCHOOL AGE.

-Sir,—The debate of the Education Committee reported in your columns last week, about the raising of the school age, seems to be a step in the right direction, and there is no doubt it would be welcomed by all clerical workers. _ _ _

One of the chief aims of the National Union of Clerks is the raising of the school age to 16, the last two OE three years to be devoted to pre- paring boys for the trade or profession they will take up on leaving school. At the present time our schools only seem to cater for students up to about 12 or 13 years of age, or until they have passed the 7th standard. In some schools 7th standard work used to be practically _a repetition of the 6th.

When a boy leaves school at 13 he has only two alternatives. The first one is to go labour- ing until he has reached a certain age, when he will probably be apprenticed to a trade; the other alternative is he will obt•tin a situation in an office, and in all probability he kept there, beeause the majority of parents like to see their boys come home clean, and never stop to think whether they are suitable for this class of work or not. This is one of the chief reasons for the low financial state of clerical labour,, and also why there are so many incapable and inefficient clerks in the clerical world to-day. There is no doubt that if the school age could be raised, and some kind of preparatory classes formed, it would give schoolmasters a better idea of judging the capabilities of boys, and advising them of the class of work that would be suitable for them. By this means the num- ber of inefficients would be considerably reduced: A MEMBER OF THE WARRINGTON BRANCH NATIONAL UNION OF CLERKS.

THE HOME RULE QU7STION,

Sir,—" Phil Squod," in his presumably vale- dictory epistle, has*the peculiar knack or intro- ducing subjects which arouse the spirit, which otherwise we would prefer to lie dormant, of contention and reply. The first letter he wrote on the Home Rule question was really, an attack on Mr. Asquith for attempting to solve the Irish difficulty by introducing a measure for local self-government, and assuring us there was really no necessity for special legislation for Ireland at all, yet he closes his letter of the 22nd with a scheme of devolution, which not only applies to Ireland, but embraces England, Scotland and Wales, and does away with Dublin Castle rule altogether. More power to you, "Phil." I have great hopes of you yet. .But why split Ireland in two? and for whom are you speaking? If in the interests of Ulster, I would point out that that province alone, by amajority of one of its Parliamentary representatives, is in favour of "Home Rule" as a whole, and against partition. The Ulster Unionists, who are the minority, claim also to be Irishmen, and whilst they are strenuously against Home Rule, I am not acquainted with any expression of dis- memberment by their leaders. If there is any fear of tyrannical treatment by one section against the other, would it be courageous or loyal of the Protestants of Ulster to desire to cut themselves adrift from their co-religionists of the South, , and withdraw from them their, moral and practical support? On the other hand, is it reasonable that the Catholics in the South would consent entirely to leave the Catho- lics and Protestant Home 'Rulers of the North to the tender mercies of the Orange mobs of Belfast and Derry? We have not quite for- gotten the shipyard incidents, and those vile curses, and yet viler obscenities towards the Pope and Roman Catholicism, I have myself seen written boldly and glaringly on the walls in those cities, I think I mentioned in a pre- vious letter. The time is not now to discuss alternative schemes. Nationalists stand or fall 'with the present 13ill, and although I hope.and believe, the present Bill will go through Parlia- ment and become lair, yet it is the unexpected sometimes happens. •:Should we fail this time, then will be the time to discuss alternatives, if any. But the presentation and advocacy by "Phil Squod" of his scheme is in itself an ad- mission of the utter failure of the present condi- tion of affairs. Why has "Phil Squod" introduced Mr. James Larkin's name into the question of "flame Rule." If, as he says," .Larkinism "is sym- bolical of " Fenianism " and " Invinciblism," then' both both " Invinciblism" and " Fenianism," which I thought were dead, or very much dying, have recently made very rapid and enthusiastic strides in England, and amongst inglishmen, who are whole-heatedly endorsing his statements relative to the abject poverty of the workers of Dublin. Whether Larkin him- self is a failure or success in the future, his agitation of the present will ultimately bear good fruit throughout Great Britain, for be- liever as I am in the principles of Home Rule for Ireland, I yet believe it is of far greater importance for the future safety and well-being of the British Isles that the children of to-day, who are to be the,parents by-and-bye, should be well fed, well clothed and housed, so that they may grow up healthy, strong and ~. via orous, capable of maintaining the traditions of the past. "Phil Squod," too, said he was not a party 'man. I accept his statement unreservedly, although it is difficult to sonceive it. Clemency in Liberal statesmen is cowardice; with Con- servatives it is a virtue.; To release Larkin is Weakness, but when the Conservatives released the dynamitards, no one questioned their reasons. We certainly have wandered very wide from the original purport of "Phil Squod's" letters, from the desirability or other- wise of Home Rule to the analysis of crime, but' it has been "Phil Squod's " own erratic and discoursive character which has led us, and we have been obliged to follow him from the straight path, so as to correct the erroneous impressions he would convey. Personally, I consider it a gross insult to an intelligent 'and steadfast people for him to assume, as he does in his latest letter, that they are less advanced than he, because they presume to think differ- ently. There- seerns to be something of the "Lord, I thank 'Thee I am not as other men," about it. Probably "Phil Squod" would be considerably surprised, if he knew the rank and, file of Nationalists better, at their unwavering belief, as the result of sad and bitter experience, in national self-government. We contest his statement that millons of "my country's money" [does he mean the Imperial Treasury?] has flowed into Ireland. It is true, British securities (not gold) have been paid to Irish landlords, the 'golden calves," wziem. "Phil Squod" evidently worships. To secure ' the title of the farmer to the ground he worked, the peasant got nothing; neither did the farmer in cash. Theirs only to repay; both Mr. Bal- four and the late Mr. Wysidham testifying to the faithfulness with which it was being repaid. The Irish leaders, or agitators as "Phil Squod" sneeringly calls them, did not' ask for this. They wanted to pledge Irish credit only. It is neither original nor argumentative to call those who differ with you agitators, and it is scurrilous to suggest sordid reasons, as they only get there by winning the confidence of their supporters. I have for years heard the same taunt thrown at foreign missionaries, temperance and social re- formers, trade • union leaders and Socialists, evangelical preachers and Salvationists at our street corners. All those 'whom we have not agreed with in the past, and who possibly have Wien working self-sacrificingly for the individual and general good of others, have been taunted with being "agitators." "Phil Squod" has made many assertions whilst writing, but they still remain only asser- tions. I give him credit for sincerity of con- Lviction, although I think his present vision nar- row and warped. I hope with, time it will broaden and straighten, and that when' Home Rule is finally disposed of, in the very near future, we shall meet on more mutually con- genial ground. • PATRICK O'BRIEN. 125, Wilderspool-road.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
WOULD 'LOCAL VETO BE USELESS?

Sir,—The temporary success of the Militant '1 teetotal organisations in applying the principle of Local yeto to Scotland, has had the result of inducing them to bring pressfire to bear on the Government to introduce a Local Veto Bill for England and Wales next year. "Prohibition," Gothenburg system, and other wonderful schemes to bring about the mil- lenium, are, all thrown overboard in favour of ' the fad of the moment. The aim of Local Veto is local prohibition, but Without the courage to say so, and, like all half Measures, utterly illogical and useless to deal with the real evils of drunkenness. Surely no one believes that a drunkard will be less a drunkard because. he has a quarter mile further to travel; or that an intemperate community is likely to vote for a reduction of facilities. Its only result would be to allow a number of organised extremists to dictate to the moderate consumer, and to make. it inconvenient or impossible for him to gratify his personal taste. It would be just ,as reason- able to allow a majority of vegetarians to close the butcher's shop because they were, just as convinced of the evils resulting from the con- sumption of beefsteak. . The great increase in club drinking is, so far as can be'seen, directly due to closing tile public-' houses where possible, and when one remembers that the public-house is ,police watched and supervised, and the 'club practically uncon- trolled, it is not difficult to decide which is the greater evil. Here again the remedy has proved worse than the'disease, for even the Government dare not interfere with the liberty of ,the indi- vidual club member, any more than they will attempt to say what people will eat or drink in their own homes.

G. E. HOLME.

November 20th, 1913

MEN VERSUS MEW

A Pointed Address by Mrs. Ransome

to Women.

WHERE MEN ARE IGNORANT.

The most ,striking address delivered in con- nection with the Adult School movement in Warrington and district this week was that given by Mrs. S. A. Ransome at the Sankey Bridges Women's meeting on Tuesday. Her subject took the form" of "a talk" on "Some aspects of the Women's ,Movethent." The speaker said by way of introduction that much misunderstanding existed not only amongst men, but amongst women on this subject. She im- preised upon her audience that the movement was not one which had sprung suddenly into being, but one -which had,grown with the devel- opment of society. There was a real connec- tion between the movement and the revolution in industrial life. - Woman, not more than 150 years ago, had played an important part in family life. Baking, spinning, weaving, &c., were at 'one time her sole business. Inventions and machinery, and the conse- quent re-organisation of labour had displaced her—things which had been the woman's work to make were now made on a large scale in fac- tories. The production of food was being car- ried- on in much the same, way, with the result that woman was placed at A disadvantage., Home life was suffering in consequence of many women being compelled to seek employment elsewhere. The pioneers in this movement held the opinion that many of the professions .which were monopolised by men ought to be open to women. There was plenty of room for women in the medical and legal world, hut all sorts of reasons were manufactured to keep them out. Dealing with the closer relations between the sexes, Mrs. Ransome said the attitude of men towards women was wrong. Under the cloak of politeness men really felt :that women were in the world for their convenience, ,and refused to look at life from the woman's point of view. These relations should bp honourable, and no man should dictate to a woman. Ignorance was at the root of these problems, and it was the ake duty of women, in spite of all obstacles, to m themselves acquainted with the facts gov- erning social and economic life. • ACADEMY-STREET MEN'S.

At the Academy-street Men's School on Sunday Inspector Flood, of the Health Department, gavd an instructive lecturette on "Flies." The speaker said file life of the house fly was pos- sibly four weeks, but its fecundity was enor- mous, 120 .eggs at a time, which were hatched out into maggots under ordinary conditions in eight hours. In two days they emerged from the chrysalis .stage into the full-grown fly, and became a nuisance to man and beast. They were the distributors of germs of various dis- ease, viz., tuberculosis, typhoid, anthrax, &c. The lecturer said that for this. reason every sen- sible person as a duty should work for the ex- termination of flies.

The subject was well discussed. The large number of friends he made showed that he must have hail a winning personality. Then the discussion veered round , upon the different methods of spreading the Christian Gospel. It was said that the Adult School spirit, when it gOt hold of a man, brought out the best in him, and tended to break down any .feeling of caste or snobbishness amongst men.

When she woke him in a morning he said he was not going and that she should go. He dui not give her sufficient money.—Defendant said a man could not get drunk when he was working until 10 o'clock at night. His wife was always grumbling and he had no peace with her.— Alderman Tinnion : You are not a teetotaler, are you?—Defendant: No, I am not.—The Chief Constable: His employers have said he id always "sodden" in drink, but that he is an excellent worker when off the drink.—Complainant asked for an order, with an allowance of 10s. per week. Her husband's wages were 235. per week.—A separation order was granted, with an allowance of 7s. per week.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
WINTER FEEDING,

Useful Hints to Amateur Poultry

Keepers.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
VALUE OF BONE FOOD.

The feeding arrangements during December -de in and special attention. The hot morning meal should now be regularly given, whether birds are kept in confinement or liberty, and it will be ESC onomised if boiled vegetables or clover chaff well scalded are added, with a good pro- portion of fine bran also well scalded. Pea or bean meal, being good pulse foods and heat °producers, may also be added, and as the morn- ings become colder one part of maize meal may be included in the mash twice a week. Poultry are alwAys benefited by soft, spongy foods, and 2oz. per bird per meal will suffice. It Must not be overlooked that to create warmth, exercise is the best means, and sound, plump oats, also wheat; nicely kibbled and scattered in loose Jitter, will soon bring warmth and vigour to poultry. Good egg yields cannot be obtained without meat in some form, animal food being a necessity. A handful of white Canadian peas may also be given occasionally, and at least twice weekly green 'cut bone should be given as a midday meal.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
COLD WEATHER FEEDING.

During the cold autumn months, when the ground is sometimes hard, animal and insct life is very scarce. Those birds which are brave enough to go foraging almost labour in vain; they cannot possibly obtain sufficient of this kind of food to• meet their requirements,. and, Says a writer in "Farm and Home," conse- quently the, egg supply, even from birds fully matured and in a position to lay, is not satis- factory. It. is here where the hand, of man may comn in. most useful and where the supply of cooked meat frequently works wonders. Now, all poultry keepers know that .unless they feed carefinly it will be easy to lose money on their. stock 'during winter. I have never suggested any extraordinary methods of feeding, but have always recommended a liberal diet, still some- thing which would, not cost on an average more -than ljd. per head weekly. Small poul- try keepers—those who' keep from ten to twenty fowls—will often have read about the great value of fresh-cut green bone; but, there are hundreds who have never • tried it. They will not go to the expense of purchasirig a bone- Cutter, but they might easily purchase a few pounds of fresh-cut bone Weekly. In almost every town there are dealers who supply it at this time of the year,-and, as a rule, 81b. can be had for one shilling., Although rather expen- sive, it is something which is not fed very liber- ally, and 811:1 will go quite a long way on twenty birds.

A bone-cutter, the small size will cost about 305.,, but it is much better to spend nearly 50s. and obtain a stropg machine, one with a capacity of 20Ib. to 301 b. per hour, and one where all the mechanical parts are strongly made, able to stand the wear and tear of this work. There has also been placed on the market, and during the last few years attained popularity, what is known as granulated meat. This is pressed meat which has been dried, and with ordinary care will keep for several weeks. It can be broken up, boiled for twenty minutes, and both the liquid and the small pieces of meat mixed into the morning mash. It is an excel- lent way of providing animal food for poultry, but as it costs anywhere from 15s. to 20s. per 'cwt., it is rather expensive.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====

===== 0002601_19131129_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
FARM AND GARDEN.

ECONONIisiNG ROOTS.

The root crops have improved considerably during the past month', says a " Farm and Home" writer. The mild weather led many farmers to leave mangels out until the middle of November, and they gained weight in con- sequence. But turnips and swedes were always poorer crops, and these are still scarce, though they have improved a good deal. After all, most farmers have to face the winter with a none too plentiful supply of roots, which will have to be economised. This will not be a diffi- cult matter, as there is a lot of good hay, and the piistures are Still full of keep.

Stoke cattle may just as well stop out on the pastures, since there is plenty of grass, instead of being taken into yards and fed on straw and roots. If they can get more shelter in the field they do' very'well on the grass alone until we get a turn of Sharp weather, which is often not until after Christmas. Then they need hay in addition, and this can be dropped about the field in little heaps, just as much as they will :clear up readily twice a day. If the stores cannot be wintered thus on the pastures, but must be, brought into yards, they can still, do without i•oots, if necessary, since hay is plenti- ful, and can be used instead of straw. They will do very well on hay with the addition of a little cotton or soya bean cake. It is difficult to fatten cattle without roots, and the more they have the better they seem to thrive. But the supply can he cut down a good bit where necessary, provided that some sub- stitute be given. Treacle and the various sugar foods on the market supply 'somewhat the same ingredients as roots, and help in the same way to make the rations appetising and digestible. Then it must be remembered that roots are about 90 per cent. water, which Suggests that fewer are needed if the chaff be either steamed or damped. Finally, what roc4s•are fed should never be given whole. That is a very: wasteful proeSS. TheY should be either Sliced or pulped.' The latter process is certainly economical, Mit it. has -largely gone out Of fashion in favour of cutting into finger slices, on which cattle thrive rather- better, probably becatise the necessary mastication aids digestion. Fattening cattle can be made to do well with as little as 401 b. of roots a. day, or even. less.

- When roots are plentiful dairy cows may be fed up to 701 b. daily with economy, but when they are scarce 201 b. will answer quite well: Wet brewers' grains Can be made to serve as a very good substitute, giving up to 20Ib. daily.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SEASON'S CROPS.

The Board of Agriculture, in a report upon the cereal crops, including wheat, barley, oats, beans, peas, seeds, hay and niadow hay, says: "The yield per acre this year. of all the seven crops is above that of 1912, and in the case of both ,kinds of hay it is also above the average of the past ten years. "The total production of wheat, upon a re- duced acreage, amounts. to 6,641,487 quarters, which is very slightly 'below last year; and the average yield-31.22 bushels per acre—while ,2-1 bushels better than in 1912, is only just below the ten years' average. Barley has yielded almost 321 bushels, per acre, exactly 2 bushels more than last year, but about 2-sth bushel below the mean; and the total produc- tion is quite 'three-fourths of a million bushels above 1912. Oats are relatively the poorest crop of the sedson, the 37.98 bushels per alisr being 21 bushels below the average, but still 21 bushels more than in 1912; the, total production is also more than last year. "Beans are about 11 bushels and peas about 1 bushel, below the mean, but well above 1912; but their total production, owing to the de- 'creased area sown with these crops, is less than in the previous year. The hay crop ,is satisfactory; that from clovers and rotation grasses being almost 51 cwt. above 1912 and nearly 3 cwt. over the average, and meadow hay being 11 cwt. above average; these figures representing the heaviest yield since 1907. ,The total production of hay of both kinds amounts to 9,052,322 tons, Or 927,000 tons more than last year.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

For the earliest crop of broad beans it is usual to sow •in November. One of the long-pod Varieties is best for this, though the Broad Windsor type can be used if preferred An- other plan is to sow' in cold frames in Deem- ber, r the first half of January, and plant out early in March.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
TRANSPLANTING MONTBRETIAS.

NVhere these flaye become too thick, they should be taken up now and divided, selecting the strongest bulbs for ,replanting. When they are to occupy the same ground it Should be well trenched and manured. This useful ornamental plant is often neglected and allowed to become overcrowded, which, results in weak growths and few flowers. If the plants are not lifted it is not wise to remove the dead foliage, as this protects ,the young, tender shoots, which are so easily damaged by the cold winds.—" The Garden." •

===== 0002601_19131129_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
LETTUCES FOR WINTER USE.

Plants growing in the open at this time of the year are more tender and more easily damaged than in the summer, owing, undoubtedly, to the dull weather and short days. If a frame- light can be placed over Ahem it will be found oil great advantage; or plants may be lifted carefully and placed in a frame, taking care not to damage the leaves. Seeds of the Cos varieties may be sown thinly at intervals in boxes or pans, and placed in a tenaperature of about 55 degrees or 60 degrees., The plants will be very valuable for salad during the winter when cut in a young state.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
PLANTING FRUIT TREES.

Apples, pears, plums, and all kinds of fruit trees may now be planted. A too rich sbil.is not desirabla; the better plan is to feed them instead by rieh surface roots. Deep planting must be avoided, participarly On heavy clay soils, when they are better planted in raised mounds. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 29, 1913.

ALDERMAN SMETHURST

Resigns After Having Made Trams and Electricity Pay.

WARRINGTON BRIDGE.

The Man Who Has Had the Biggest Say in Greatest Modern Work.

Alderman James Smethurst, who came to War- rington from Bolton in 1877, built up a success- ful iron founder's business, and then became one of our leading public-men, has placed two great achievements to his credit. He has put our electricity and tram undertakings, as chair- man of the committee that has charge of those joint departments, upon a paying basis, and he has engineered to a successful issue a movement for the erection of -a new bridge across the Mer- sey. Since the election of ,the committees for the new Council he has resigned his membership of the Electricity and Tramways Committee. lie was appointed chairman of that committee ten years ago, on the 18th November, 1903. At that time both undertakings were in their in- fancy. Current far lighting was first supplied in December, 1900, and the Sankey and Latch- fol'd tramway routes were opened in April, 1902.

Electricity Undertaking.. . This, department up to 1904 had not been worked at a Profit. The accumulated -losses amounted to £1,632, -which had to be, met by a grant from the improvement rate. .It had not been found possible to create a reserve fund or a working balance, and the units sold were only 706,620. At the end of last March the reserve fund amounted to £9,358, and a working balance of £5,990 had been formed. The units sold were nearly five times greater, and showed a total generated of 3,408,000. The increase is being maintained in the present financial year. The number of units generated for the half year

ALDERMAN SNIE't ttU BST.

ended the 30th September, 1913, has increased over the orresponding last half-year by half a million units. The approximate value of such increase would be .21,200. A profit has been .made in each year, increasing from .2129 in 1904, to .22,944 in 1913, and averaging £1,955 for the ten years, making a total of .219,559. This has enabled the committe4;not. only to provide the reserve fund and workini balance, but to grant .22,500 to the relief of the rates and .22,020 in respect of capital expenditure. The loan charges have been provided for before arriving at the net profit, and as a consequence, although several extensions have been carried out, the debt outstanding on the undertaking is only £75,000, as against £73,626 in 1904.

Tramways Undertaking. A similar tale of prosperity can he unfolded here.. During Alderman Smethurst's first year of office a loss of £783 was recorded. The next year. 'showed a net profit of £450, and a profit has been continually made since, the aggregate being £16,921, with an average of £1,692. The sum of £6,000 has been granted in relief of rates, a reserve fund of £lO,OOO has been created, and the balance of the appropriation account is £1,820. The debt outstanding on the 31st March last was only £73,830, as against £95,026 in 1994. The receipts • from passengers in 1903-4 were £15,972, as against £22,903 in 1912-13. The latest results are shown in the accounts for the half- year ended 30th September, 1913. The passenger receipts have gone up from £11,359 to £11,743, and each route has contributed to the increase of £384. The working expenses have increased a little, but a net profit of £1,439 has been made, which is £l5l greater than the net profits for the corresponding half-year.

Success of Re-organisation. Alderman Smethurst attributes a great deal of his success to the manner in which' he has been able to re-organise these undertakings. In the re-organisation of the tramway departnient, a reduction in the number of employes brought. down the wages bill £l,OOO a year. It was clear that the system was badly overstocked with men, and the staff was systematically, reduced, but hardship was avoided by the committee securing situations by arrangements with other tramway undertakings to employ the Warring- ton men as they came out.. Originally. the cars were cleaned during the night, but it was found practicable to do the work in the daytime; this was done in other places. Since that discovery the cars have been cleaned at a time when the sun has provided the necessary light, and ,2400 worth of electricity a year has been saved.

Mr. Telfer's experience prior to coming to Warrington was of great value in connection with these reforms. One of the reforms ttat Alderman Smethurst instituted at the electricity'works was the in- troduction of automatic stokers for the boilers. The first year's saving in coal was close upon £l,OOO, and smoke from the electricity works chimney became a thing of the past. We are fortunate in having what Alderman Smethurst calls "a splendid man 1-4 in charge of our electricity concern. Mr. Mathias, the chief electrician, manages without the expensive luxury of calling in experts when any work has to be done, and he knows how to get the most out of his plant. The extension which is only now to be done would have been done two years ago; says the retiring chairman, had it not been for Mr. Mathias's manipulation of the resources at his command.

"What do you think of the prospects?" asked our representative of the Alderman. "The figures," said the Alderman, "will show 04 the prospects for. the next- twelve months are very bright: The accounts Om* that I am leaving them with, a very good half-year's re- snits to be going on with."

The Bridge. • • Alderman Smethurst still remains at the helm so far as the bridge is concerned. It is over thirty years-since the town first began to think and talk about the need for widening Warring- ton Bridge. Many unsuccessful attempts hav- ing been, made to raise the question. Alderman Smethurst took it up something like twelve years ago, and produced a scheme fel. widening IV means of cantilevers, to the width of Bridge- street at the bottom end, at a cost of about £3,000. Opposition came from Messrs. Fair- dough, Admiral Nares, the River Commissioner, and others. The idea of a one-span bridge took the place of the idea of cantilevers, and the battle became one of steel versus ferro-concrete. Ferro-concrete won the day, and the bridge now in course of erection at the contract price of £14,000, a bridge that will be 80ft. wide when completed, was the result. THE QUAKERS AND WAR.

A Reply to the National. Service League Argu-

ments.

Mr. Robert J. Long, organising secretary of the Northern Friends' Peace Board, writes from Thirsk-row, Wellington-street, Leeds The current issue of the National Service League Notes is entitled "Special Noncon- formist Number," and has been widely circu- lated among the Free Churchmen of this country. Among other interesting matter is an article entitled "The Quakers' Poibt of View," which consists in, the main of two quotations, one from George Fox (the " founder " of the Society. of Friends), and another from Isaac Penington. The avowed object of the reprinting of these extracts is "effectually to dispose of any idea that they did not iecognise the use and actual necessity of force and a resort to the sword of justice.'" An icamination bf the first of the passages referred to--that by George Fox —proves it to be a piece of wholesome advice to his followers to respect the magistracylas the representatives of law and order. Though Friends have often had occasion to deplore many acceptance of actions Which have been committed in the name of law, this does not affect their ptance government. Penington's attitude is clearly that while for him fighting and warfare are impos- sible, he will not judge those who are honestly convinced otherwise. His pamphlet is an appeal for freedom from fighting on the part of those. who have fully accepted the Gospel, and is thus flatly opposed to universal military compulsion. A casual reader of the "Special Nonconformist Number" might well gather that the modern Quaker attitude towards war is a fad, or a species of fanaticism, while if we were to look to the founders of the Society we should find them gloriously devoid of such petty scruples. This simply is not tb.e case.

FARM AND G-ARDEN—Cont.

the tree, and also all damaged roots. Plant firmly and stake securely. If the' roots of • Ibe trees are dry, soak them in water for a few hours. Fruit trees may a planted in wet soils, by placing the trees almost on the surface, placing a layer of broken bricks beneath the ,roots, and filling in the soil in the form of a mound. Improper planting has a good Mal to do with failures to obtain fruit. An open site should. be chosen, but sheltered from the north and east winds. Both light and heavy ..soils trench two feet deep, improving the former if necessary with soil from another part of the garden, and the latter by adding wood ashes, road sweepings, and leaf soil.. Do not plant the trees when the soil is wet, as they never do well so planted.

OWN ROOT ROSES.

The majority of roses sold by nurserymen are budded on a stock. The reason for this is that it is the quickest way of obtaining a flower- ing Plant. It would take more than double the time to have a saleable plant on its owdroot, so that it is Obvious plants could not be sold so cheaply as a budded rose. There, are, however, zHivantages of having roses on their own ,root, Le., grown from a cutting. Should the winter be so severe as to till a rose on its own‘ root down to the ground, it is almost certain to break into strong growth from the base and produce good blooms. It is still not. too late to put in cuttings. Select stout firm wood of the current year's growth, and which has perhaps carried a trus of bloom. Insert about three inches in the soil. Put the cutting in a shaded and sheltered spdt, and make •quite firm. Mix plenty or sand with the soil, and let the base pf the cuttings rest on sand.' Cover wth a handlight for •a time, which may be taken off in nice weather. • • Where the majority of people who try• their hand at raising trees from, cuttings make the mistake is in being'in too much hurry. They must not be disturbed until the second spring following the putting out of the cuttings. If nice growth is made during the first season a little pinching may be necessary to form a reasonable shape. If most of ,the flower buds are picked off until the third season, the reward will be forthcoming with good blossoms.

THE MAYOR'S MASCOT.

'Old Journalist' Discourses Learnedly on the Talismanic Horseshoe.

CUSTOMS OF MANY LANDS.

Old Journalist " writes :

Master James Cunnington's gift to Warring- ton's new Nlayor as a token of good luck sent me afresh to my authorities • for precise details respecting the superstition. .Here is an extract from one of the best of these :

Fixed aloft, on sonic conspicuous part of a dwelling, the horseshoe has been esteemed 'a sovereign safeguard against all malign influ- ences. Milled to the door of dairy or byre it kept' at bay all witchcraft and spirits of evil ; made fast to threshold or doorway it forbade all entrance of fairis, pixies; or nightmares ; affixed to the ,spars of Vessels it ensured the sailors a prosperous voyage; and who can tell how much of the glory of Trafalgar was due to the ,influence, of the horseshoe which was nailed. to the mainmast of Nelson's good ship, the Victory? Even the New World has not dis- dained to adopt this relic of Old World super- stition, as many a shanty of the backwoodsman testifies. Indeed, go where we will through the civilised world, we find the horseshoe regarded as a talisman of mystic significance, accepted, in varying degrees of sincerity and conviction as a means of averting ill, and bringing_ good luck and prosperity to its possessor. In Den- mark and in Germany, asin England and Scot- land and Ireland, nailed to the threshold it be- comes a-prophylactic charm against the intru- sion'of the powers of evil; placed over doors in •Sicily and Southern Italy, it is a powerful amulet to avert tlle malocchio or evil eye, and it is worn for the same purpose by the roving Arabs of the desert; it is found affixed to many an ancient mosque and shrine' of Northern India, and engraved as a sacred emblem of old N-Porse monuments; it is worn by Chinese, in- fants to protect them from malefic influences, and laid under the German child's pillow it guards it from convulsions.

, I well remember the whole sobject. being dis- cussed in the columns of a newspaper a few years ago and one or ,two correspondents ex- pressed the opinion [hat the idea of the sanc- tity or lucidness of the horseshoe was based primarily on the fact that it was iron. In the beginnings of European history, anything iron -was good against .the evil spirits, the fairies. This is connected by students of folk-lore with the view that the original fairies, the malignant beings who were the terror of the countryside, were the survivors of the Stone Age. the flint- weapon men, who had escaped by hiding in caves and wild places from the exterminating invaders armed with. iron.

In what position should the Mayoral horse- shoe be worn to ensure good luck? One con- tributor , wrote:

"Undoubtedly the symbol has its origin in the old pagan mystic superstition which, be it noted, was often rewii-ded in Its negative aspect. The horseshoe does not bring good luck, but •keeps away evil spirits, which, of course, means the, same thing, except as indi- cating the point- of view. The heels of the shoe must point upward; the evil spirits were supposed ko be attracted to and entrapped by the crescent, hut if the heels pointed downwiirds they would fall out, and thus, be liberated; they could not climb to the top, and could only run to and fro along the hottom hi perpetual imprisonment."

Here is quite another view. : "A horseshoe has two attributes, one of pro- tection against man or demon, and one, cOM- paratively modern, of 'luck,' or the promotion 'of the fortune of its possessor. For protection, the prongs must point downwaras ; for luck, upwards. Almost every representation that one can recall of the horseshoe shows it to be thus placed. In Naples (the very.'eentre of the.:.super- stition of the Jettatura or Evil -Eye) are seen horseshoes nailed to the houses, prongs don- wards. It has ever been learnedly contended that that is the proper position for the horse- shoe, either when used as a protective symbol or as a luck token. This. view is founded on the suggestion that the horseshoe form is a Sur- vival of an ancient religious symbol, often seen in Assyrian and Egyptian sculptures, signify- ing the mystical door of life. The horseshoe is the mystical door reduced to its simplest pos- sible form, and as a fetish for bringing good luck or as a talisman to avert the evil' eye, it would have no meaning except with the points downward."

ALDERMAN SMETHURST—cont.

structure that will be the greatest visible monu- ment to Alderman. Smethurst's work for the good of the town of his adoption. As Chairman of the Bridges Committee during the whole of the time the arrangements for the new bridge were being made, and since, he has closely atched the progress, of the work step by step, .id many valuable suggestions made by him ~ave been adopted. The contract, according to the original design, without the training walls, was £10,850; for the original design with no walls, which have re- claimed a considerable amount of land, £14,310 and for the latest amended design the estimated cost is £17,000. The saving in cost as between a steel and a concrete bridge has been from 0t10,000..t0 £15,000. The river has been spanned at Howley by a suspension bridge, which has proved a great convenience, to two large districts, of the town that were previously cut off from each other.

An Active Public Life. Alderman Smethurst was elected a member of the Council 22 years ago this month, defeatingl Mr. (now Alderman) William Pierpoint in Town Hall Ward. He has never had to fight for his seat from that day to this. Perhaps-this has been due to the fact. that he has always attended to his public duties and performed those duties well. The • Meetings of the Council and its many committees, upon which the more active and prominent members serve, are very numerous, and it stands to the credit of Alder- man Smethurst that he has often been first or second in the "List of attendanees," when that interesting document has come to be compiled at the end of the year. The year just ended he attended 170 meetings, besides sitting at the Police Court as magistrate on 104 occasions. Side by side with these heavy public duties, he serves on the Education Committee, the Gram- mar School governing body, the Lango Com- mittee (Inebriates' Home), the Nursing Associa- tion, the Infirmary Committees (General and Working Men's), the Savings Bank governors, West Lancashire Territorial Association, and other bodies. _ On the occasion of the review of the Territorials by the King at Knowsley last July he had the honour of being Presented to His Majesty. He and Mrs. Smethurst had already- been presented at Warrington. He was a great success as Mayor for the two years.l9o6-7-8, when he took such an interest in the children of the borough that many of them will grow up in the belief that he is the only Mayor Warrington ever had. He established the Empire Day celebration in the schools and the children's ball, which subsequently, in the hands of his son, resulted in the permanent endowment of a children's cot at the Infirmary. During his Mayoralty he welcomed,General Bul- ler to the town; received Lord Lilford, who laid the foundation-stone of the Infirmary extension, and Lord Cross, who opened the building on its completion ; entertained Mr. Sidney Buxton, who opened our new Post Office; and laid the foundation stones of the Hippodrome and the Bolton Schools; but it is characteristic of the man, who is as full as it is, possible to be of human sympathy—which, as we have hinted, was instinctively discovered by the children of the town—that among his proudest achievements he places the fact that he has been a principal worker for 32 years on the committee that pro- vides the old men and old women of the town with a tea Tarty about Christmas time. Of that committee he has been chairman since the death of Alderman Sutton.

BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH.

" Ideals."—An Impressin Sermon.

The Rev. H. Middlewood, choosing as his text •the words "For as much as it was in thy heart to build an house for My name, thou didst well, in that it was in thy heart," preached a sermon on " Ideals " to the congregation at Bold-street on Sunday evening. The text referred to the pathetic story of David's frustrated ambition to build the temple of God; and suggested the fact that even un- realised 'ideals carried a blessing. Ideals were the most precious possessions of man, and it was profoundly important that these ideals should be high. Ideals were meant to take in life as a whole, and moulded the character. The Pure love of God . and of man must therefore enter into their deepest essence. It sometimes happened that the ideals were not realised because of conflicting duties, but because there was a possibility of their being frustrated they should not be discarded.

This was the message of the text. Life with- out ambition was a mere creation of circum- sta'nces. Ideals influenced the character very profoundly, and each effort towards realisation brought the idealist nearer the accomplishment of the aim.

ngland had not reached the ideals of her philanthropists by any means, hut our land was the better for the strivings of her idealists towards their realisation.

The choir rendered the anthem "As pants the hart," Mr. Booth being the soloist.

"A QUEST OF AN IDEAL."

An Address With a Moral at Wycliffe P.S.A.

An address with a pointed moral was deliv- ered at Wycliffe P.S.A. On Sunday afternoon by Mr.. Littler, of Runcorn. The title was "A quest of an ideal."

He saw,'he said, in the Art Gallery a picture of a man climbing a steep and rugged hill. On the top of the hill was a woman, who was ever pointing hillier. This picture spoke the mes- sage, a quest of an ideal. The P.S.A. platform was as high as any platform in the country, yet it, did not appeal to all as it might do. Young men to-day were too eager for pleasure, and were forgetful of higher things. Their minds were too full a weekly sport, and other- wise many of their 'hinds were as hollow as the balls they kicked, or. saw kicked about on the Saturday. There was too much sensual pleas- -tire, and it leaked into the office and workshop, as well as into the spare time of the youths. Then many of the youths' chief thoughts were centred only in the gaining of success in life, they were out after the mere bubbles of life. These people must learn to think of the high- est things in life. Success in their ambitions might please, but they did not satisfy. They must find the ideals and aim at hitting them. Sorrow and joy were,-realities which could not be explained; their wfiole life depended upon the unseen and the great ideals. They would ex- perience sickness, but they put their trust in a \living God. What was a man worth when he died? The great one hecame low, the rich one poor. Death did not define between them, and- circumstances, money or fame did not count. The grand ideal was to be rich in faith,' In have confidence -in God, and His realities hereafter. Rich indulgences in life were as nothing com- pared with the goodness and love of God. Their ideal should be to reach out to that goodness, which was attainable. Let their chief ideal be: How can I help ray fellow men, how can I im- prove the Church and society, what can I do for 'others, and- not simply act for myself? and by doing so you will honour the honoured name of Christ.

Mr. Stewart, Liverpool, was the chairman, and he spoke of the pleasure it afforded him in meeting such an inspiring class as that of the Wycliffe P.S.A., a class which ought to help, instruct and bless its members. Mr. Payton read the lesson, and Mr. Healey led the prayer. One new member was welcomed.

THE PROBLEM OF EDUCATION.

The Rev. Dr. Mellor and Personality.

"The problem of education" was the. theme of the Rev. Dr. Mellor, .Preaching in Caird- street Chapel on Sunday evening. Education and instruction, he said, were two different things. With the 'value or otherwise of the latter lie was not concerned except to protest against the pitiful confusion of the one thing with the other, which now vitiated all their judgments about both. In the proper meaning of the word, education was a thing in itself, a value in itself, not a means to some other end, but in itself both means and end. The object of educating a man was simply that he might become more and more educated, more and More himself, more and more develop his own deepest and most genuine personality. To educate' meant literally to "lead out," to leaVout, as from imprisonment the powers' and faculties, the capacity for hope and vision and ideals in a man, to bring out, as they said, the best that was in him, above all, to help him to unlock the door and release all these splendours for, him- self. Education was just another name, for the, development of personality, and when education as a process became a social matter, and it fell to their lot to construct an educational system, the sole object of that system must be the sum- moning forth of the deepegt Personality from its hiding-place and the setting of it free to live its fullest life in the world. That was education. _

Educatiorf Was the culture of the personal lifer it was the process by which Men learnt to know themselves, to put right value on the facts and circumstances of their live, and to acquit them- selves worthily and nobly in all those social re- lationships which the conditions of finite exist- ence imposed upon them. It was a fallacy born of the industrial system to suppose that a man's real life consisted simply in the economically productive work that he could* accomplish. His real life,on the contrary,was only when the sphere of production was left behind, and the spheie of appreciation, enjoyment, and creative activity was entered. Genuine education was concerned with the latter sphere and not with the former. In., order to make a man a more efficient pro- ducer in the economic sense they instructed him, but they did not educate him, and if they confused instruction with education the result was that they had in. the world a moderately satisfactory body of economic slaves, but vary , few genuine free men. Their present system of general education seemed deigned for the former end. They were instructing their chil- -1 dren, not educating them, and they made the situation \worse by instructing them very badly. They wanted to make a child able so to use his native powers that be might appreciate his life, enjoy it to the full, and, perhaps, add to the splendour 'of life in general by the efforts of his own imagination and genius.

ANNIE S. SWAN (A.uthor of "Gates of Eden," "The Ne'er.

Sally was a maid-of-all-work in a small nuburioa,n home. The precise designation of that suburb is immaterial to this story. The incident with which it deals, and which led Up to a crisis, might have happened in any suburb.

Sally was the sole domestic help in tilt, house/I°A of Robert Lidgate, who was a con- fidential clerk to a firm of highly respectable solicitors in the neighbourhood of Gray's Inn. Sally was the child of a Brixton slum, and had been raised in a rag-and-bone store where her early lot had been conspicuously hard. A good lady who had established a girl's club in that area, liking the girl for her bright lace and smart ways, had taken special pains to have her trained in domestic service. She liad indeed taken her for three months into her own home to be trained by one of her own domestic treasures, thereby nerious:y endangering 'the peace of her house- hold. At the end of that time Sally obtained her first place as maid-of-all-work to Mrs. Robert Lidgate, a young bride, before whom she promptly fell down and worshipped. Ethel Lidgate and Sally between them, had no great store of domestic efficiency, and Robert suffered at first, as many young hus- bands do from the doubtful quality ox the viands set before him. But love is merciful to inferior cooking, and the first three months was got over without catastrophe. The young wife was so adorably anxious to please, and looked so pretty with the flush of apprehen- sion on her face, that no husband worthy of the name could have allowed a single stric- ture to pass his lips. Lidgate was fifteen years older than his wife, and a matured bachelor, when he mar- ried Ethel at twenty-three. Such a situation has its pitfalls, and as Ethel was very pretty, Robert's particular pitfall was an inordinate jealousy. It arose out of the humbleness of LLB nature. He was constantly asking him- self what a beautiful girl like Ethel could have seen in a plain, unattractive fellow like himself. He had solid qualities, and his busi- ness prospects were bright, pointing even to a partnership at Gray's Inn, but he had none of the meretricious charms which are sup- posed to be dear to the heart of a pretty, at- tractive, young woman. Nevertheless, Ethel had married him out of genuine affection; if he could have been assured of that all would have gone well. His jealousy prompted him to shut his wife up, or at least to keep her sway from quarters where she would be likely to meet men more attractive than him- self. This made Ethel angry. She was only human, and she liked brightness and variety, likewise she resented her husband's lack of trust. One day, about ten months after their marriage, something happened which bade fair to destr3y thier happiness, and which in its most acute crisis necessitated the inter- vention of Sally. The Lidgatea breakfasted at eight o'clock every morning, which en- abled Robert to eat his meal in comfort,/ and get the train at eight forty at the station five minutes walk from the house.

When they were first married Ethel had I cooked the breakfast herself, but Sally was now supreme in the kitchen, and had devel- oped a real genius for cooking. Her scram- bltga"eggs, Uncle Christopher had pronounced when he came for a week-end had never been excelled. Uncle Christopher was the only uncle of Mrs. Lidgate, and Ethel had some expectations from him. He was a gruff old gentleman, with whom Robert did not get on very well, and he had disapproved in a kind of general way of the wedding. He thpaght his favourite nieoe ought to have done better.

Ethel got down usually before her husband knit to see that all was right in the little (lining-room, but on this particular morning /the had some trouble with a refractory waist belt so that Robert got down first. There were several :etters for the Lidgates, Ethel especially had a good many friends. Three lay on her plate. When her eyes fell on them as she passed to her place her colour rose a little, and she began to make the lea without offering to open them. This was un- usual. Often Lidgate had to remind her gent- ly that time was passing., and that they must get on with breakfast. She pushed the letters aside, and put the sugar into the cups, and even after she had been served with a- portion of fish, she began to eat without SO much as looking at them. "Aren't you going to read your letters, dear ?" asked Lidgate, his eyes blinking slight:y under his glasses. Ethel professed to love these glasses, and indeed they were more becoming to Lidgate than to most men, and gave him a particularly professional look. Presently, there isn't any hurry. nave you not had any that you are so anxious about mine ?"

" On_y one from the Church secretary about the funds, and the second call for the Amalgamated shares. It doesn't seem any time since we had the first, does it ?"

"Not long certainly, but I suppose you can meet it all right," answered -Ethel, and Lidgate imagined her tone absent, her inter- est lukewarm.

" I daresay it can be met. Who's your for- eign correspondent ?" he asked rather dri- ly. Ethel's face flushed again, for now she knew that already Robert' had carefully ex- amined the letters before she came down.

"Looks -like my cousin Charlie's writing. I haven't heard from him for ages, not since I wrote him that I .was going to be married to you." Lidgate had heard of this cousin Charlie, of his handsome looks and happy-go-lucky -ways, and once had come upon a small minia- ture of hiin in Ethel's iewel case, which had caused him sundry qualms. "Won't you open it then and see what he has to say ?" he suggested pointedly. Reflecting that she really had no reason for refusing, Ethel broke the seal, and be- hind the tea cosy proceeded to read the let- ter, Lidgate watching her with ill-concealed anxiety and suspicion. Presently she stopped Tending, and thrust it in her pocket, and her cheeks were very .red. "Charlie's a very silly boy," she said quick:y. "I'm sorry I can't let you see the letter, Robert."

"Why can't you let me see it ?" "You're ungenerous to ask," &he retorted rather hotly. "But if you will have it, Charlie imagined himself in loilb with me be- fore he went away, and this letter is simply a repraach,from beginnina, to end. But he hasn't any right to write like that, Bob, be- cause what I told you when we got engaged was perfectly true. I had three offers, Char- lie's was one of them, but you were my first real sweetheart."

Lidga,t,e made no reply to this charming confession, but bent his frowning brows on his plate. This sight made Ethel feel very un- amiable, and she proceeded to punish him for it with that touch of pure maliciousness which lurks deep down even in the best of women. "He's coming home. He's 'struck lie,' as he ,expresses, out there in the far west, and giving himself a treat. I must go out to Put- ney, and see Aunt Lulu this very afternoon. How frightfully pleased she will be." Lidgaste pushed back his chair and rose to his feet. His ruddy colour was gone, and he locked oddly pale. Ethel did not feel in the leas& sorry for him, indeed, she could with very great satisfaction to herself have given him a good shaking. Uncle Chris- topher asked this morning if you were com- ing down for the day, and I said yes. How did .you know ?" . . .

Liagate's answer was to strain her to his heart with a grip which hurt. In that mo- ment of surrender he saw himself as he was, hard, unjust, suspicious, and he felt himself bitterly abased. -

Presently, and before Ethel could prevent him, he was kneeling at her feet. 'Darling, forgive me, I've been a brute! I don't know what came over me, but every- thing looked black. You see I was never worthy of you, and I thought you regretted our marriage, and—and—" "I know, Cousin Charlie !" she said with her hand on his lips. "Don't let us say a single solitary thing about it any more. I do want to be happy again. It's like an eternity since we were happy. Now cime and see dear Uncle Kit." Next day Sally was glorified by beholdiog a complete return of the old conditions to the little home .she loved so well. ' If you ever marry, and I hope you will some day, Sally," said her mistress as she mixed the ingredients of the best pudding Lidgate had ever eaten in his life, " don't forget always to be open and above board, and don't play with fire. It's dangerous, for you never know when the spark is going to jump up into a big blaze." Sally merely smi.ecl and Shook her head.

WOMEN'S QUESTIONS.

Some Former Fashions that are

===== 0002601_19131129_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
Returning to Favour.

SEX RIVALRY IN DRESS.

The Christmas season now extends over !most as many weeks as it used to days, and

like the last one, it promises this year to be a busy shopping time , for tradesmen. They are expecting to liid "plenty of money about," and, judging by the display of new season's goods

the bazaars, thee are numbers ,of attractive

novelties. to induce people to spend it. There will also be plenty of amusement and enter- tainment for young and old, together with no scarcity in that department of Christmas shop- ping which concerns the table. All the markets will be fully supplied and retail prices ought to be moderate. It is futile in these days to look for cheapness, but there should be plenty of Christmas fare available, at v:-hat we have come to regard as reasonable prices. It has been a splendid year for breeders of cattle, sheep and pigs, and the fine weather has been favourable for the rearing of turkeys and poultry.

A. Wonderful Pageant. The Picture Ball, which will take place in the Albert Hall this Christmas in aid of the inValid , kitchens of Cordon, is altogether original in - charity fetes, since living- figures will stand within the frames and represent those painted by the artists. The history of Art from the earliest tithes • will be illustrated. It Will be a wonderful pageant, and will certainly prove the most .beautiful of all the fetes .ever organised for the cause of charity in the Albert or any other Hall. The colour scheme is very strik- ing, and. consists of a white ground, with vivid splashes of black and gold. Green bay trees will be placed throughout the hall, their dark foliage affording a fine contrast to the whiteness of the background. Two searchlights will be used during the dances, one of which, of a . Olden hue, will, simulate sunshine as it falls ' upon the dancers., The organ will be concealed by artistic drapery, with Venetfah masts on either side, and hanging golden baskets filled with flowers. M. Cassan's band will number one hundred and twenty performers. A Ladies' Hospital.

A little known, perhaps, but beneficent insti- kition in London, is the Ladies' Hospital, which has just enlarged its quarters in Lisson Grove. It had for its founders two notable women— Florence Nightingale—before she had rendei(ed herself immortal—and Viscountess Canning, the -wife of " Clemency " Canning, of Mutiny fame. Until 1909 this modest institution was located in Harley-street, but when the lease ex- pired it became necessary to find a new location, and the Queen opened the larger buildings in 1910. These have proved unequal to the de- mands made upon them and an extension pro- viding seven more beds was recently opened by the Duchess of Albany, bringing fhe number up to nearly 40. Since 1910, considerably more than a thousand patients have passed through the hospital.

The Return of the Cape.

The cape, which has been discarded for quite a long time, shows signs of returning again, and as it comes in a charming guise, will doubtless be quite as much/a favourite- as in the far-away days of its popularity. At present it is seen for the most part as an opera cloak, long and clinging, its fullness set into a high collar, after the fashion of 'the loaks worn tby *ltalian officers; nothing could be more beccim- ing and graceful. For theatre wear these cloaks are most comfortable and suitable, and the fact that they can be .so easily slipped on and off should be a great recommendation, for they have no armholes or intricate fastenings to in- terfere with .their donning, or doffing. Some of these capes have the fulness gathered into a deep yoke, that goes over the shoulders, and is plain or ornamented, according to the taste of the wearer. For outdoor wear, the cape to the knee takes the place of the coat. In black satin with a turn-back collar, but severely simple in cut and trimming, the cape and dress is most pleasing.

The 'Vogue of the Waistcoat. Not only. cutaway coats, but frocks and blouses have fallen under, the, spell of the waist- coat, a decorative ;addition which is worthy of consideration of the expert needlewoman, or of the tyro, for even the latter can make sUch a dainty accessorY forther own use, or for an acceptable present for a friend. In chamois leather, lightly embroidered in. some antique design in black silks, with. black velvet buttons, an original garment may be fashioned with little trouble or expense to wear with black; or very dark suits, or dresses. Even very hand- some crazy. patchwork has been successfully employed in the case of one of ,these-novel gar- Quilted satin, gar- ments. very slightly wadded and stitched in contrasting silks, in a rather in- tricate design, makes another handsome waist- coat. Brocades, of cour'se, are the ideal fabrics for these decorative accessories, and when the pattern is outlined with embroidery, the hand- some effect is heightened. The Young Girls' Burnous.

The once univerSal burnous is still worn by 'ioung girls, and is exceedingly dainty in .this season's many lovely materials, that render a choice ,so difficult. Quite an ideal fabric for this garment is the new shot duchess mous- seline, which is really a silk with a very satiny surface, double in width, and by no means as expensive as its rather misleading name would seen to imply. The hood is usually of the deeper shade of the, two, or sometimes three colours are employed in these shot fabrics, and is finished with a handsome silk tassel to match. The hem should be turned back, in accordance with the latest and most •ipproved decrees of La Mode. The ruche, which has re- turned to favour, makes 'a very effective and very pretty trimming for the young girls' after- noon or evening .dress. It is .used to,finish the tunic' and sleeves, and in tulle or chiffon is as light and dainty as when worn by. the quaint damsels of the far-away distant 'thirties.

Masculine Dress Reform.

The sombre uniformity of masculine costume is only a comparatively modern development, and sartorial experts are constantly predicting a return to more dressy and ornamental .attire for men. They were encouraged by, the mascu- line fancy of highly-coloured socks, tics, and ornate waiWoats, and when the "man in the street " took to the plush hat with a tuft of feathers in ,it, we were told to expect a revival of Georgian or Jaeobian fashions. There were periods when men and women vied with each other in the matter of adornment. Man's plu- mage was, in fact, the more brilliant of the two, but the conditions of modern life _do not favour a return to such a state of affairs. There is no particular reason why the male popula- tion should :banish colour Iran a world that would:be all the better for it, but the masculine dress reformers make little or no progress in this direction. The well-dressed man is once more taking to the silk hat And discarding the dinner • jacket, in favour of the full-tailed gar- ment, but there is no.evitienee to show. that he is returning td Georgian or Jacobian tastes.

WIDNES LAND EXPERIENCE.

At a Liberal demonstration in Middlewich on Saturday night, Sir John Brunner, who pre- sided, said that in Widnes a large tract of waste land in the centre of the town had been pointed out to him. Unoccupied land, according to the law, paid no rates or :taxes. The ratepayers of Widnes had bought. and paid for their own waterworkFi•and gaswurki; but not a copper did the landowner pay forthe waste land he had re- ferred to. Led 'by men of eminence and public spirit, the people of Widnes erected an excellent school, which subsequently required to be ex- tended. For that purpose they wanted 2,000 yards of land from the owner he had spoken of. What was the price?, .11. ,a yard. (",Shame.") For forty years that land paid no rates or taxes, and when it was wanted for the good of the people it was £1 a yard. ("Shame.") Ladies and gentlemen," exclaimed,Sir John, with em- phasis, "I tell you that if you want justice be- tween man and man, alter your . land laws." (Loud applause.)

ST. HELENS POLICEMAN SENTENCED

Savage Assault on an Inspector. William Weaver, the St. Helens policeman who fractured the nose of his superior officer, InsPctor Jackson, during a midnight visit; was sentenced on Monday to six months imprison- ment, with hard labour, on a charge of 'common assault. It was explained that the inspector formed the impression that Weaver was too affected by drink to continue on duty through the night. He told prisoner he could not allow him to 'remain on. As the inspector went on his way he received a blow on the back of the head, and, turning round, he discovered Weaver acting like a madman. Weaver said, "You have got your time in, and you are going to break my home up. I will kill you.'.' He took the inspector's stick and struck him across the face, breaking his nese. Jackson shouted, " You have knocked my eye out," but notwithstanding this the prisoner continued to kick him on the legs. The inspector fainted in Weaver's station, and Weaver fetched Sergeant Heaton, telling him that Jackson had accused him of being drunk, "and I have climbed over him."

MR. LLOYD GEORGE AND VOLUNTARY INSURANCE.

" Most Foolish of all. Mr...Bonar. Law's Sugges

tions."

At an Insurance Act meeting at Rushden on Monday, addressed by Mr. Handel Booth, M.P., the following telegram was read from Mr. Lloyd GEORGE." /

"Hearty felicitation on your two hundredth insurance meeting. Wherever I go I hear glow- ing accounts of the excellent work you,have done in expounding the Act and removing misconcep- tions. Time is on our side, the benefits are spreading, and soon explanatory and defensive speeches will be unnecessary. Mr. Bonar Law's idea of voluntary insurance is the most foolish of all his suggestions. Good luck to you. LLOYD GEORGE." Mr. Harold Smith has declared himself in favour of this most foolish of all suggestions.

THE FIRST CHARGE ON AN INDUSTRY Sir John Barran, M.P., and the Agricultural

Worker.

Speaking at Huddersfield on Saturday on "The Agricultural Labourer: His Claims on Liberalism," Sir John Barran, M.P., said the .agricultural labourer was by no means unskilled and therefore should not receive an unskilled wage. .-As Free Traders Liberals- could never bolster up the food-producing industry of the country at the expense of the food consumers, and he believed that in the agricultural calling, as in all industries, the living wage should be the first charge on the inaustry. If the farmer had to pay more wages, and could not make a satisfactory profit, then the Rent Courts would have to lower the relit. Greater security of tenure, greater efficiency in' the agricultural labourer, compensation for damage done by game—in short, better conditions would be fol- lowed by increased productivity, and he was hopeful that the industry would provide a reasonable return for those engaged in it.
