A LIVERPOOL AGREEMENT.

Widnes. desires to spend £20,000 on water works extension at Netherley, and a Local Gov- ernment Boar cl inquiry was held on Tuesday to consider the suggested loan. Liverpool Cor- poration opposed the ;Widnes scheme. Mr. Jeeves, for the Widnes Corporation, said it was expedient that they should proceed with the work at once, and it was purely and simply from the point of view of expediency, he sub- mitted, that the question of Liverpool's oppo- sition could not arise. Widnes had been granted powers under Parliament Acts, and he did not for one moment see how it was possible for Liverpool to come and ask the Local Govern- ment Board to interfere in any arrangements which had received Parliamentary sanction in 1887, and again in 1008. This was not the proper place to make an application to . release the Liverpool Corporation from the agreement en- tered into by them. Mr. Ilallaway, of Warrington, said his Cor- poration had a similar agreement, and what effected Widnes would interfere with them. From St. Helens there was also a similar objec- tion. Putting on "The Screw."

Mr. Jeeves said that in 1880 the Liverpool Corporation applied for Parliamentary powers for their water supply from Vyrnwy. At that time Widnes had water supplies from three sites, and it was known and recognised that there was a great capacity for future use. In that Act there was an agreement entered into by the Liverpool Corporation on the one hand and by the Widnes Local Board on the other, that whenever necessary, upon a given notice from Widnes, they could demand a 2,000,000 supply per day from their Vyrnwy connection. There was no suggestion in the agreement bind- ing Widnes at any time to take water. In 1908 the Liverpool Corporation applied for an Act of Parliament to terminate and cancel that agreement, and the similar agreements entered into by St. Helens and Warrington. It was perfectly clear and plain, he said. His clients had an ample quantity 6f water, and it was pnly proposed to increase that quantity by developing existing sources. He deprecated the pressure and screw the Liverpool. Corpora- tion were putting on Widnes. Mr. Hetherington intimated that the Corpora-

tions of St. Helens and Warrington were ner- vous of the fact that if, Liverpool succeeded, their turn would mime. As far as he knew, they wanted to save their position.', After some evidence was called the inquiry was adjourned:

From that time on I received medical treatment, but was tortured with awful pains. _

Every change in the weather made the ' Rheumatism worse: My limbs swelled, and my flesh seemed full of scorching agony. I could not lift my arm to dress myself. FAMOUS INVENTOR DEAD.

Sir William H. Bailey, the well-known 'engi- neer, formerly. Mayor of Salford, died on Satur- day at a London hotel, in his 76th year. He was a successful inventor, the author of innu- merable pamphlets on scientific and literary subjects, and a popular lecturer. He was also a large shareholder in and a director of the Man- chester Ship Canal, and was knighted on the occasion of Queen Victoria's visit to Manches- ter to open that waterway. Sir William was chairman and managing director of W. H. Bailey and Co., Ltd., Albion Works, Salford. He filled the office of president of the Library Association of the United Kingdom from 1906 to 1907, and was a governor of the John Rylands Library, Manchester.

A GREAT CURE ALL

That Overreached Itself and Fizzled Out.

The "Daily Express" for. nearly three years— May 26th, 1908, to June, 1911—published at the head of its news column a text announcing that " Tariff Reform Means "—something or other. , Weeding all transient meanings out of the list,. there remained assurances that "Tariff Reform Moans" More Work and Fewer Workhouses; .Real Pensions for Old Age; Pensions at 65; Higher Wages; Protection for Home Industries (the "Express" did not avoid the dauger-woriTh Protection for British Workmen; Fewer Trade Disputes; An Adequate Navy; Lower Poor Rates ;'Work and Food for the Poor; Protection (the word recurs many times); A Check to Socialism; Protecting our Workers as Other Countries protect theirs; Naval. THE EXAMiNER, SATURDAY,NOVEMBER 29, 1913.

IHE "BABIES' WELCOME."

New Eranch Opened.

Such success has attended the efforts of the Mothers' and Babies' Welcome during its two and a half years' lease of life that it has been decided to extend its boundaries, and for the future the Bewsey district will have a branch in its 'midst, and this, in Catherine-street, was opened on Thursday 'afternoon by the Mayor, on behalf of. the Mayoress. The Bewsey branch. of the Welcome, is, through the kindness of the Adult School, held in their school in Catherine- street,. where, on Thursdays, the officials of the Welcome will be in attendance during the after- . noon.

-- -- Mrs. Venn presided and Was supported, amongst others, by the Mayor and Mayoress, Miss Broadbent, Mesdames Hill, T. Pemberton, Ransome, F. W. Monks, S. Mellor and Simpson, and the Medical Officer of Health kDr. G. W. N. Joseph). Mrs. VENN said the principal thing was to bring the babies regularly to be weighed. They were delighted at the success which had attended the Welcome since it was opened. The MAYOR, on behalf of the Mayoress, had very great pleasure in declaring the Welcome Open. He was sure much good would result. Up to the present the authorities could, only visit .children for the first year, but they hoped to extend the age to five yeark, Dr. G. W. N. JOSEPH, in thanking the mayor and Mayoress, said in his experience much was gained by having a central depot like that, where the mothers and children could call at. It was wise to have the babies weighed. Weight was a great index to a chilT during infancy. The infantile death-rate of children under twelve months was 92 per -thousand, which was lower than in any other town of the size in Lanca- shire. The death-rate for the next few 4-ears in life, although not so high as in other towns in Lancashire, increased, and it was extraord- inary that many of them died in the first few months of the second year—just after they had Passed the age of visitation. It was proposed to appoint another nurse, and with visiting the child until it was five years would mean until it had reached school age. and on beginning schooling it came under the school medical work.

the Liverpool Scotsmen—if that was not an Irish way of putting it—and marched with them to the skirling of the pipes—(applause)—and he could enter into their feelings of pride and clan- ship expressed at that annual festival. As the years ran on his love for Scot- land grew greater; he was one of those who were answering the ancient pugnacity of the Scots- men, who raided England, by raiding Scotland every year in order to enjoy its exquisite scenery and its many other charms. He had great pleasure and pride in proposing the health of the Caledonian Association. (Ap- plause.)

THE SWAN WITH TWO NECKS.

Another piece of Old Warrington has passed out of use, and will shortly pass from sight. On Wednesday last the old "Two-necked Swan" saw its last day of usefulness. The "Swan," as it was familiarly called, was one of the numerous coaching inns which were a feature of the old town. . For a long bolo it has been hidden from the casual observer_ nd approached by a modest passage, but it seems quite „likely that it was once a prominent object in Bridge-street. it has been suggested that the building line has been put forward, and that its front marked the line of the old street. We prefer to think that the "Swan" stood

Swan". is its true- title. But the idea of a two- floated swan. is a curious error. The true title as originally used was "Swan with two nicks." It is the custom of the Vintners' CompanSr to mark their swans with two nicks cut in the beak. Nothing could be more, appropriate i for a tavern sign than the Vintners' emblem of a -swan marked with two nicks' upon the beak, and the sign became a common one. But where the meaning of the nicks was not known it was easy for ordinary folk to suppose that "two necks" were intended. The carving in Bridge-street is. based upon this supposition. The marking of swans by cuts upan the beak

INN THAT BECAME AUCTIONEER'S SHOWROOM.

The title "Swan,' as applied to an inn, is interesting. Like the peacock and pheasant, the swan was an emblem of the parade of chivalry, and every knight chose one of these birds which was associated with God, th..; Virgin, and his lady-love, in his oath. Dating from the days of chivalry, the thtee birds became popular everywhere as inn signs. The "White Swan" was frequently adoirce•l as a compliment to Anne of Cleves, who was descended from a knight of the Swan.

is still practised, and Royal swans are marked with five nicks, two lengthwise and three acruss the bill. The swans on tile Thames, which are still under the care of the Vintners, are cut in their tender youth with the "two nicks" and carry the identification mark through life.. We do not know. that any history attaches to the "Swan." Doubtless its hospitable roof has sheltered many famous travellers as they have called for refreshment or repose on their jour- neys along the North road, and its walls lind chimney corners have heard warm discussion's upon events which we only know in the cold pages of history. • It is one of the oldest of our inns, and HE removal breaks another of the few remaialug links with the coaching days of the past. A. M.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
WASTE BASKET SECRETS

Police Find Betting Slips That Lead

to a Conviction

TALE OF AN UMBRELLA SHOP.

The sequel to the police raid yesterday week on business premises in Horsemarket-street was heard - at the Warrington Borough Court on. Tuesday, when John Sampson Blythe, carrying on business under the name of Wilson and Co.,umbrella makers and 'repairers, was charge with using the premises, 48, Horsemarket-street, for the purposes of betting. Mr. T. S. Steel defended.

The Chief Constable stated that some months ago it came to the notice of the police that the shop was being. used for betting and observa- tions were kept, extending, over a considerable period. On Saturday, September 20th, P.C. Anderson saw 18 persons enter the shop and leave again almost immediately. Apparently they could not have been on very important business, or they would have been kept longer. On Monday, September 22nd, practically the same persons went into the shop. They ap- peared, to be regular callers. Observation' was kept on the premises on various other dates, and on 'November 17th Sergeant Holland, and P.C. Anderson, between .11.45 a.m. and 1.30 p.m. saw 15 persons enter the shop and spend prac- tically no time inside,' and on NOVember 17th the same thing occurred again.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
Well-known "Sports."

"Practically the same people- entered the shop each day," said the Chief Constable, "and these people are known to the -police to be well- known 'sports' in town." These people had undoubtedly gone there for the purpose of giving in betting slips. 'The men were regular callers each day, and when observation was kept on November 19th and 20th it was with the same result. On 'Friday, Nov- ember 21st, Superintendent Williamson, Ser- geants Holland and Reddington, and P.C. Ander- son raided defendant's shop, having fortified themselves with a search warrant, and when they .got there they found a man with th defendant in the ,front shop. The man stated that he was there for legiti- mate business, and for a time he adhered to that statement. . The police told him he was not telling a truthful story, ttnd that they had a warrant to take all persons into custody who were found on the premises. The man then seemed to have faltered and admitted' that he had gone there for the purpose of betting. The defendant was then questioned, and he, after the other man had let the cat out of the bag, at once admitted the charge, and said "Well, I have been a fool, that's it; but I have not been doing it long, only about a month or two."

===== 0002601_19131129_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
In the Waste Paper Basket.

When asked where his books were, he replied: "You will not find any books; I did not keel) any. I have not been doing much; only a few sixpenny bets from four or five people. They asked me to do it, and I have paid out more than I have taken." The defendant was taken into custody, and on the premises being searched there were discovered a promisory note for 4:5, a .15 note of the Bank of England, and ..t.5 3s. in cash, in addition to letters, telegrams, betting papers, and some of the slips related to horses running at Manchester on, Friday last. The Superintendent took possession of the waste paper basket and found it contained a very

large number of betting slips which had been torn up. Many of these pieces were placed together; and they were found to relate to bets ranging from 6d. to .11. In addition, there were an extraordinary number of pieces of paper relating to horses running at Newbury, Doncas- ter, Manchester, and Warwick Races. There seemed to be a system in vogue that the defend- ant wrote all the bets on one piece of paper, and tore the slips up. There were also two football coupons, but it was not suggested that these were in connection with the betting business, but merely that the defendant was trying his .own luck.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
A Plea of Guilty.

Mr. Steel, on behalf of Blythe, pleaded guilty, and stated that the case was summed up in the words of the defendant himself, when he said: "'I have been a fool. % I have only been doing it for a week or two, taking bets from four or live friends." He was instructed that the defend- ant carried on a ,legitimate business as an umbrella maker and repairer in Horsemarket- street, and there was no necessity to do any 'betting business. During the last few months he had conceived the idea of taking bets from his friends, and he had only taken money from his friends. 8o far as the defendant was con- cerned, he had acted stupidly, and he suggested that if the defendant knew anything about the betting law he would have destroyed the slips which were amongst the paper in the waste paper basket. ' Defendant was not one of those men who made betting his business. He had his own business, and he now saw the folly of the whole thing, , which he undertook to oblige his friends.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
Fines! £25.

Councillor H. Roberts, chairman, asked where defendant's friends were?

Mr. Steel answered that they were not there. The Chairman: I point out that that: is usually the case. Mr. Steel: I was going to say that. Mr. Steel added that defendant had a legitimate business, sufficient for • the maintenance of himself and wife. The defendant had instructed him to say he was going to give •up betting, and that they would have no trouble with him again.

The Chairman asked if defendant carried on the betting business on his own account. Mr. Steel: Entirely, yes, sir. _

The Chairman, after -a brief consultation with the other magistrates, said that the defendant would be fined £25 and cf sts, and he said that was a moderate fine, in view of all the circum- stances, and having regard to the fact that the defendant carried, on the business on his own account. The Bench would not accept that plea in any future case.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
"MISERABLE, PALTRY,"

Bishop Slates the Contributors to the Missionary Cause.

THE MISSIONARY EXHIBITION.

The great missionary exhibition in the Empire Rink concluded ,the first part of its run on Saturday With a record of success. Restarting on Monday, it was anticipated the record of the earlier portion would be beaten. It is a con- siderable time since a function has aroused so much enthusiasm and ittterest amongst local Churchpeople as has done the missionary exhi- bition.

Canon Matthew, rector of Wigan, presided on Saturday, in the absence of Lord Wolmer, from whom a telegram was subsequently received, stating that he was on his way. Bishop CORFE, late of Korea, opened the ex- hibition, and said that from his experience of the S.P.G., he knew it did a most useful and valuable Work. He had seen the society' at work in Africa, China, Japan and North Canada, and could -testify that it had been true to its original charter, which empowered it to send out Missionaries into all parts of the world. The society taught Shim that if he became a mission- ary he must be unselfish and to enlarge his ideas. He had started an association of prayer. He asked. the Christian people he saw in Eng- land before he went to pray, for there was a great value in prayer. It taught p'eople to get a better understanding of things, and he would ask each of them to pray for those who were engaged in the mission field.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
BUSINESS MEN AND MISSIONARIES.

Tile exhibition was opened on Monday after- noon by Bishop Morley. Mr. B. S. Townroe presided over a fair attendance, and said that exhibition was an outward and visible sign that Warrington looked beyond its own confines— looked beyond the seas in our own dominions, and throughout the world. In industrial centres like Warrington, there was an objection to missionaries very often. found. Men con- nected with business had very often thit objec- tion, "What have we business men got to do with missions or natives in foreign parts?" There were three needs—oil, soap and biscuits— the ingredients for which came from foreign lands. (Hear, hear.) The S.P.G. did a very noble work in Manchuria, and their energies and labour were felt to a large extent in this coun- try. (Applause.) Bishop MORLEY felt very pleased with the fact that people were beginning to take a more prominent interest in missionary work. He said Christ was coming, and he believed they could de a great deal in determining the time when that should be. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
IGNORANCE THE CAUSE.

Bishop of Liverpool and Lack of interest.

Dr. Chavasse, bishop of Liverpool, opened the exhibition on Tuesday, Mr. John Coxon pre- siding over a large attendance. . , The CHAIRMAN said that exhibition had called public attention to the society which was 200 years old. It was an important society, the im- portance of which could not be exaggerated, but there were people who considered the funds too slender, and did all they could to increase them. On looking round the exhibition one was struck with the richness of every detail, and their thanks were due to Canon Willis and the Rev. the Hon. C. F. Cross, the two hon. secretaries, and to the members of the laity, who had so ungrudgingly given their time and energy to make the enterprise such a conspicuous suc- cess. They all knew the great work the Bishop had done in Liverpool and throughout the country, and it was not surprising there was such a large attendance when he came in the diocese. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
A Debt of Gratitude.

The BISHOP of LIVERPOOL said the diocese of Liverpool and Chester owed a great deal to the promoters of that exhibition. He was sure that that exhibition would stir not only the surface, but the depth of every parish in the two dioceses. One of the greatest maxims of Chris- tianity was "What I have, I give," and they should all bear that in mind and spread the. Gospel. They should pray to have a heart as wide as God's. He ventured to think that some- times indifference was not the only cause or the main cause of the lack of interest in missions. He thought it was due in great measure to ignorance—not-for .want oof 'heart, hut for want of knowledge. Some people might say that their giving capacity was limited; that if they gave to for- eign.missions they would lose at home—schools, hospitals and churches would be the losers, and not the gainers. Nothing of the kind would happen, Missionary giving:did not limit home giving, but increased it largely. The promoters of that exhibition had done a great deal to re- vive the spiritual life of the Church of Eng- land in certain deaneries, and were beginning a work, the results of which would be spread to the ends of the world. (Applause.)

LAMENTABLY SMALL CONTRIBUTIONS. Mr. W. Collingwood presided over a large attendance on Wednesday afternoon, when the Right Rev. Bishop Mylne, late of Bombay, per- formed the opening ceremony. The CIIAIR.IIAN said he was particularly inter- ested in the Indian Mission field, having been a resident in India for many years. He could speak with personal knowledge of the great work that and other societies were-doing. They were constantly hearing people decryinr, and running down missions, because they did not see the immediate results. He had seen the wonderful things that were. being done by the earnest workers, who had gone out, and he was sure that the exhibition would stir • up interest in foreign missions in this district. (Hear, hear.) . One of the results- of that exhibition would be to increase the lamentably small con- tributions from this district. A great deal of ground was being broken, and a lot of the old superstitions swept away by the advance of civil- isation, and it was for them to supply something to take their place, otherwise the results would be lamentable. (Hear, hear, and applause.) - Bishop MYLNE used a few of the exhibits to illustrate his remarks, and he gave an address which could not fail in its appeal. Personal experience, he said, could give a full sense of the moral and spiritual value of missions. Re- ferring to Hindooism, he said that religion was just "caste." Really the work of the missions in India consisted of wrestling with the evils of caste; emancipating the people from that even more than anything else. A religion of caste was "following the custom of ancestors." A religion which might make stealing a man's duty, if stealing had been his father's ocupa- tion, was not of a very highly moral or elevating character, hut such was Hindooism. It was an extraordinarily immoral thing.

"MISERABLE, PALTRY, DISGRACEFUL." Bishop andsMeagre Contributions. Major Parkes, who should have presided on Thursday, was unavoidably absent, and the chair was taken by Major. Appleton, the open-

===== 0002601_19131129_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
ing ceremony being performed by Bishop Neligan, late of Aua:land, New Zealand.

The CHAIRMAN said he thought Major Parkes would have touched upon cadets being mission- L. because, • in a way, he was connected with the Cadet movement. To-day there was a want of discipline and a lack of religion amongst our growing population. Every true member of the C.E.M.S. and every member of the Church Lads' Brigade could be missionaries for good in their own homes, and when they went to other parts use all their influence for good. (Applause,) Bishop NELIGAN spoke at length of the mission- ary work in New Zealand, and mentioned the excellent work that exhibition would do-in the district. Later in his address he said English capital was going into India, Ceylon, Africa, China, and elsewhere, and the result was they were robbing the natives of their faith. What was wealthy England giving for the extension of Christianity amongst these people? It was giving 16s. Bd. out of every £lOO of national revenue, and giving a dirty, miserable, paltry, disgraceful 2s. out of every £lOO of national income.

No man believing in Christ could be a pessi- mist. He was bound to be an optimist, but an optimist became an ass if he did not look at facts staring him in the face. They should see things as they were and do their best to better the conditions. (Applause.)

A " DEAD " CHURCH.

The exhibition was opened yesterday by Bishop Mather, formerly\ of Antigua, Mr. George Scarr presiding. The CHAIRMAN said that the Church which was not a missionary Church was dead, and a dead Church could do no good at home,

Houghton; Canadian children, Misses B. Catterall and A. Broomhall: Canadian mother, Miss E. Tartan; Australian children, Misses S. Broomhall and R. Martin- dale; Australian mother, Mrs. Boardman.

The members of St. Jarnes' Church gave the pageant, "One Family," to a large audience on Saturday evening. The performers had been excellently trained by Mr. W. H. Stansfield, who himself appeared as Pope Gregory. Miss Marie Hawley was the pianist. BOYS' AMALGAMATION

Church Lads, Chapel Lads, Cathollc Lads and Scouts

'JOIN HANDS.

There is a very commendable effort on foot to form a permanent committee of all the boys' organisations in the town so as to work together harmoniously and for the common good. A meeting has been held by the various asso- ciations' representatives in the League Hall, Bewsey-street, and everything .pointed to the fact that all were in favour of t,his amalgama- tion for the purposes of drill, athletics, ambu- lance work, &c. The first combined route march will take place next Thursday evening, when the following organisations will make their rendezvous on the lawn facing. the Town Hall—the Church Lads' Brigade. (Cadets), Catholic Boys' Brigade (Cadets), Baden-Powell Boy Scouts, Mr. Starkey's Cadets, and the War- rington Free Church Boys' Brigade. The "First Post" will be sounded by the St. Paul's C.L.B. Buglers, and this will be followed by an address to the lads by his Worship the Mayor. The ;combined parade will then proceed along the following route, under the command of Lieut.- Colonel F. M. Appleton, assisted by the Senior Officer, of each of the organisations who take part, as his staff officers :—Sankey-street, Bridge- street, Mersey-street, Church-street, School- brow, Fennel-street, Buttermarket-street, Horse- market-street, as far as St. Paul's Church, where the,column will break up. The band of each unit that possesses one will play in suc- cession along the route. It is to be hoped that this will be the fore- runner of many such combinations of these organisations, all of which have at least one object in common—that of making the lads of the town into good and nprigla citizens.

THE SHAKESPEAREANS. New Society Formed.

To judge from the attendance and the interest shown at the newly-formed Shakespearean Society's meeting in the Church House, Bridge- street, on Tuesday night, its goal should be success, with numerous triumphs on the way. A Provisional Committee had previously met and drafted a set of rules, the adoption of which and the election of officers formed the chief business of the meeting. Mr. L. R. Rowson pre- sided, in the absence of Mr. R. Garnett.

Mr. HODGSON, who was acting as secretary pro. tern., read the rules, which suggested that the society be called "The Warrington Amateur Shakespearean Players," and that it should be promoted for the purpose of playing Shakes- pearean and Other plays, with an annual sub- scription of ss. The sole control of the per- formances and the arranging of them to be in the hands of the committee, who should 1/t) empowered, in cases of emergency, to elect any outsider to take part in any productions nf The, society without payment of the subscription. Mr. W. ASHTON suggested that 6s. should be the annual subscription, but Dr. CHARLES 1). LAW opposed him, and after considerable dis- cussion the meeting decided upon, the ss. sub- scription. __ .

The following officers were unanimously agreed to :—President, Sir Gilbert Greenall; assistant secretary, Mr. Hodgson; treasurer, Mr. H. M. Ashton; producer, Dr. Charles D. Law: acting manager, Mr. Bradshaw; hon. auditor, Mr. -Bradshaw; committee: players—Treasurer, Assistant Secretary, Producer, Mr. W. Ashton and Mr. Wheeler; non-players—Mr. Bradshaw, Mr. Garnett, Mr. Rowson, Mr. Warburton, Dr. Mellor and Mr. Loader. Much satisfaction was shown when: Mr. Brad- shaw announced that Mr. A. M. Loader had offered his services to the society, and, without any discussion, he • was co-opted on the t:om- mittee.

WARRINGTON SCOTS

Celebrate St. Andrew's Day in Characteristic Form.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. CROSFIELD AND SCOTLAND.

For the 25th time Warrington Scotsmen assembled on Thursday evening at the Lion Hotel to celebrate St. Andrew's Day, and, inci- dentally, to prove the strength of the patriotic spirit Scotsmen maintain wherever their lot be cast. The Warrington Caledonian Association is a strong, healthy body, a goodly membership being secured from the thousands who hail from Bonnie Scotland and live in Warrington. A first-class menu was provided by Host Lee in the ballroom, which was tastefully decorated with Scottish flags—the Lion • Rampant and the blue and white banner of St. Andrew. The Chairman, Mr. J. Fulton-Smith, and the Mayor and other chief guests were escorted to their places by Piper-Sergeant Stoddart, of the Liver- pool Scottish, to the tune of " Bannocks of Bar- ley Meal," and the same picturesque individual headed later the chef and his staff when the Haggis, "great chieftain o' the puddin' race," was brought into the room, along with' the "drams a' l'Ecossaise." Nearly seventy members and visitors were present, including. Mr. J. Fulton-Smith (presi- dent) and Mr.. J. L. Davenport (vice-president), to whose combined efforts the success of the Association in Warrington is largely due. The President had on his right his Worship the Mayor (Councillor P. Peacock), and on his left Mr. W. P. Rylands, the other occupants of the top table being Mr. A, H. Crosfield, Lieut.- Colonel Fairclough, Alderman Forshaw, Alder- man Tinnion, and Messrs. W. Skinner, W. Rus- sell and M. Nicholls (chief. constable). Others present included /Messrs. B. Shearer (ex-presi- dent), G. Bowman (hon. treasurer), J. Tennant (hon. secretary), B. Williamson (assistant secre- tary). W. Murray, M. W. Grant, Dr. Law and Dr. Lumb.

REEKY WARRINGTON!

The CluritmAN, in opening the after-dinner programme, announced that', hi answer to a greeting sent by the Norwich Association, the following had been wired from that meeting:—

Prae reeky Warrington [" Oh," and laughter) we send Oor Norwich brithers greetings! May plenteous spirits aye attend. Yersels an' a' yer meetings. '

WHERE WARRINGTON DOES NOT SHINE. The loyal toasts having been submitted from the Chair, and fittingly honoured, Alderman FORSHAW, proposing the health of the Navy, Army and Reserve Forces. eulogised each branch of the service, claimed better pay on behalf of the men, and alleged that it was the news of the coming of Capt. Appleton and the Warrington Volunteers to take part in the Boer War that led Kruger to abandon the siege of Mafeking.

Colonel J. D. FAincLoron, replying, said the services were suffering from lack of numbers. They were trying to make bricks without straw, but they were looking to the general public for that support to which they were entitled. The Territorials stood in need of more money, for they were not adequately backed up financially; but they did not want any assistance given them at the ,expense of the Army estimates. 'The local " Terriers " were 300 men short—they had 700 men towards an establishment of 1,000, and as 200 came from Newton-in-Malerfield, they would see that Warrington was not doing what it ought to do proportionately to its popu- lation. The battalion, in common with the 2nd Battalion and the sth Battalion, had been con- spicuously successful with the maxim gun, but in numbers they did not shine, and he bespoke the active interest of all in favour of the batta- lion. (Applause.)

all there was to be said about their country (Hear, hear, and laughter.)

RYLANDO, proposinc, the• toast, "summed up" Scotland as a beautiful country, with a terrible climate and a magnificent people. (Hear, hear.) He thought the word Gaelic was intimately connected with the word Gaul, and that the Scotch people originally inhabited the whole of Western Europe, but Scotsmen in those early days were so much coNerned in breaking one another's heads that they had not time nor the inclination to write an account of them- selves, and as our information came from their enemies, it was not altogether to be relied upon. (Laughter, and' hear, hear.) It was not at hll clear what was the difference between the meaning of Picts and Scots. It was not great, because they absorbed each other, but an etymological reflection led them to a word which suggested that Pict was derived from a word which meant robber. (Loud laughter.) The most popular suggested orikin was that the Romans called the Scots " Pictus," because of their love of colour; which ultimately revealed itself in the tartans of the clans. Right down to the end of. the 13th century Scotsmen were: never conquered, and then Edward I. had but a brief success. In 1603 King James of Scotland became King of England; England became the vassal of Scotland, and Scotland had ruled Eng- land ever since. (Laughter.) There was a rumour that even yet the courage of Scotsmen might be put to the test. Just as Edward tried to seize the country, a Welshman to-day— (laughter)—was going to try to steal the coun- try by a stealthy method of purchase—(laugh- ter)—and we poor Englishmen would watch the contest between the representatives of the acquisitive Welshman and the canny Scot. (Laughter.)

THE MAYOR AND COMING IMPROVE- MENTS.

-- - Mr. W. Mon - ar, who proposed "The town and trade," had a tilt at an eminent man in the Council," and as a puddler and smoke- maker, held that smoke and trade could not be separated. He expressed the opinion that under the Mayoralty of Councillor Peacock, the town would hold its position among the noteworthy places of the country. The MAYOR, replying, spoke of the favourable position occupied by the town owing to its situa- tion and its many industries, and hoped we should have continued prosperity. We had recently learnt that Rylands Bros., Pearson and Knowles, and others had improved the condi- tions of their workpeople, and he thought the firms themselves would benefit from the in- creased efficiency of the employes as a conse- quence. (Applause.) There was a good deal of municipal work ahead. He promised to do his very best to have the bridge completed before his term of office expired. (Applause.) They had been hanging too long on the job of im- proving Market Gate. In committee that day they had decided to clear out he Old Market Hall to Make the circus at Market Gate com- plete, and he believed as a result of what had taken place that day that Buttermarket-street would be widened before he left the chair. The baths would be proceeded with and a reduction in the price of gas representing £4,000 would be made. A small increase in the rates might be expected next year, but he thought we would have benefits more than commensurate with the outlay. (Applause.)

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 29, 1913.

THE PASSING E,.

IMPROVEMENTS TO COME. The Mayor, at the Scots' dinner on Thursday night, made the.interesting announcement that it has been decided to go on with, the work of completing the Circus at Market Gate. It is al case of if it were done, then 'twere well it were ' thine quickly, and Councillor Peacock will earn the gratitude of all if he remedies matters, where, as he says, we have been too long hang- ing upon the job. He hopes to see Butter- market-street completed-during his occupancy of the chair, and Warrington Bridge, too. It is a worthy project, and we hope the Mayor will prove a true prophet.

are a' long-suffering people, but eventually we may rebel against this refined style of tyranny that rides rough-shod over every •interest but its own. Unless the people do make an effec- tual protest through their representatives on the public bodies, there can be no doubt that the nuisance will grow to greater dimen- sions still. As the Irishman is alleged to have said, "Our cup is overflowing, but it is not full yet!"

" F.C.G."

Sir Francis Carruthers Gould, knighted in 1906, in recognition of splendid work done for many years in journalism, particularly on its 'illustration side, has admirers whose 'name is legion. Strong in seizing points and an adept at presenting them in the strongest possible light against his opponents, without giving way to the temptation to be offensive, to which so many cartoonists succumb, Sir Francis has long been • a.power in the political world. Be- sides being the caricaturist for ",The Westmin- ster Gazette," he is its assistant editor. He as born at Barnstaple so long ago as 1844, the son of an ardhitect, and was educated in private schools in his native town. For over twenty years he was a member of the London Stock Exchange, and for many years he illus- trated the -Christmas number of ~" Truth." Before "The Pall Mall Gazette " changed hands and sides he was a constant contributor to that journal: Since then he has peen attached to the, "Gazette." His activities have take; him in divers directions, lecturing, volunteering, travelling, studying natural history, writing. But sketching is his great forte, and it figures. at the head .even of .his recreations. We thought our readers would be glad to learn a few of th leading characteristics of a famed personality, especially as :from to-day, they will have the privilege of frequentiy seeing his telling, but refined, cartoons in the pages of ' "The Examiner."

AMBULANCE WORK, DOCTORS, AND EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

Time was, before the doctors lent themselves to the most unscrupulous political agitation in the history of this country, the agitation that aimed at the destruction of the measure that set out to, get the workers insured against ill- health and the misfortune which conies in. its train, when they readily did all they could, wherever they could, for the good of their fellows, often without fee or reward. Perhaps when their -aberration haS spent itseif tliey will return, to their own proper selves, but in the meantime the community is suffering in a way it ought not to be called upon to suffer. There are hundreds of thousands of ambulance men in this country wilo owe their training to the self-sacrificing 'services of doctors. Avho have. lectured to them without fee or reward. But, the Insurance Bill was introduced, and, bY some process of reasoning, which could only be dictated by political motives, the British Medical. Association resolved that henceforth, its mem- bers would be unable to lecture for nothing. and ambulance classes must pay a guinea a lecture, or do without them. As the St. John Ambulance Association will not accept any but a 'properly qualified medical man's certificate as a guarantee of efficiency, and as /hose who wish to become ambulance men and women are mostly of the working-class type With whom guineas are scarce; it has followed that recruits have' 'become exceedingly scarce. The price of the doctor is prohibitive,' and while the War- rington Ambulance Brigade is languishing for new blood to replace the wastage in the ranks. caused by time and other reasons there are many who ,are desirous of taking tip the work, but are unable to do so in the absence of the essential doctor's certificate. As we have seen, the event has proved the fallacy of the argument that the Insurance Act- would injure the doctors. On the contrary, it has been the making of them. The nation is now their patient,- the nation is a good debtor,,Who pays promptly. and Makes, no bad debts, aild the nation 'pays the doctor the whole value of his time and labour, 'whether he worts 'or not. Will the British Medical Association re-con- sider their hastily-come-to decision, and again take in hand a body of workers who, without pay or reward of any kind, but often at con- siderable expense and inconvenience, are wil- ling and anxious ,to make themselves fit to.be of service to' the community in sudden emer- gencies?

===== 0002601_19131129_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY RENTS INCREASE.,

_ We publish in another column an article on the vital question why rents in Warrington are on the increase. The case is stated frankly

from the point of view of the landlord and the speculative builder. That rates have gone up of late years, if. not by leaps and bounds, at least to a material extent, is true, and the land- lord is entitled to recoup himself by 'apportion- ing a fair share against his tenants, whose rent includes the rates. Our correspondent draws attention to the many " free " advantages we now enjoy, from education to bowling greens, and claims the right to pass on the charge to those occupying .his property, to the end that the free fallacy may be exploded. But his Main contention is that employers of labour, who can I make profits up to ten per cent., should in- crease wages to counterbalance the decreasing purchasing power of a sovereign, and enable people to pay rents that give a fair return, though far short of ten per cent. Otherwise Municiparand Government aid must be sought, or, he- argues,- we shag soon be homeless, .at any price. It is an intexesting article, and con- tains many points—presented, as we say, from the landlord's side of the case that may be studied with advantage.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OLD FRIEND.

The many friends in Warrington of the Rev. J. F. Matthews, who for a long period made a deep impression upon the life of the town, will be pleased, though by no means surprised, 'to know that the reverend gentleman has long sinco taken up a corresponding position to the one he held here in the larger sphere to which he went When he removed to Sheffield. Recently the "Sheffield Daily Telegraph" had a column article from his graceful pen on the mission and aims of the modern university. For one so bound up with the welfare and advancement of the people the subject was most congenial, as every line of it indicates'. Dealing with the work of the modern universities in general and the Sheffield institution in particu- lar—which are competing on the popular, side with the still more or less exclusive " ancient " universities—Mr. Matthews says:

British democracy is appallingly and lament- ably ignorant of ,swhat it is most assured, the " fons et origo" of its own vitality. Here the University will touch the life of to-day, and link it up in the minds of men with past ages. As the Twentieth Century renaissance more freely and fully expresses the life of the demo- cracy, its hopes and ambitions, its growing passion for knowledge and the amenities of age, it will look to the modern University to give a satisfactory and a lasting interpretation of them.

I conceive that it Jul peilorm no greater service to men than to take its place at the very heart of our Commonwealth, watch its move- ments, make vocal its desires, direct and fashion its reading, lead its thinking, widen its horizon, and satisfy its ample life. It will be a great protective father, and the nursing mother of our future citizens.

Sooner or later there will be a great uprising of the Democracy ,in these lands. When Charles I. was a child keen observers noted his aincnteerriseitlite prayed that this char- ' and dcted along right chan- nels. We can only pray the,same way that when Democracy thinks it has attained to years Um,ilifguhlnt ebsse of discretion and it time has arrived, it will be an educated-and a sane Democracy. The Rev. J. F. Matthews has done his share not only to reveal to the people their poweib, but to teach them their responsibilities.

DRINK AND INSANITY: CAUSE AND EFFECT.

It is not often We have such a candid con- fession from an apologist of alcoholic drink as the one which appears in our correspondence cOlumns to-day over the 'signature of " Edward- Tucker." This gentleman is well known as a writer who keeps a very keen eye indeed upon all temperance advocates, in order that h may correct their misstatements and rebuke their exaggerations. To-day he takes to task the Rev. IL Ogden for his "attack on Canon Willis and the local breweries." That, we think, is a Slightly inaccurate 'description of the attack; Which Was upon all breweries, with comments upon Canon Willis's pride in his connection With the local breweries. But that is a small matter. Mr. Tucker's complaint. is that Mr. Ogden said drink was responsible for a consid- erable part of the lunacy in our country; and he says that when teetotalers have learnt to distinguish between cause and effect they will modify .or retract many of the rash statements now made with regard to drink. To prove that Mr. Ogden is confused in his reasoning, 'Mr. Tucker, quotes Dr. Gemmel, medical super- intendent, of the Lancashire County Asylum, Who, in a recent report, says that in mall:cr- eases drink- is the effect and not the cause of insanity, the intemperance being but a pro- dromits of commencing, if not actually existing, insanity. This may be good evidence _from a brewer's point of view, but we fail to recognise it as such. Whether a man drinks and makes himself insane, or drinks because he is insane, is of little account to temperance advocates. The alithority quoted by our correspondent does not, by any means, say that drink is not the cause of insanity. On the contrary, he says : "1 have frequently alluded to drink as an alleged cause of insanity," and lie goes on to speak of the many cases in which the insanity is the cause of the drinking. So that ouil friend is hit both ways by one he fancies, for some strange reason, to be a champion on his side.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
Boys' COMRADESHIP.

The fine spirit of comrades-h-i P-which domi- nates those splendid organisations; the Church Lads' Brigade, the Catholic Boys' Brigade, the Baden-Powell Scouts, Mr. Starkey's Cadets,iand the Free Church Boys' • Brigade, was never better demonstrated than by the decision come to this week to amalgamate forces in the work they are doing for the common good. The first visible sign of the change will be seen next Thursday night, when a ,combined route march over a great area of the town will take place, starting from the Town Hall lawn, after the Mayor.has addressed the boys, who may be ex- pected to be present in full strength. His •V'orship will have a fine theme and he may be relied, upon to make the most of the occasion. lie knows too much of the value of unity among men to be unable to point a powerful moral to those who will, be the men of the immediate future.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
STATE OF THE ROADS.

Never since the days prior to the establish ment of County Councils have our main roads been in , such a deplorable condition as now. The heavy traffic, especially between Manches- ter and Liverpool, is making terrible havoc of what were admirable roads for ordinary loads. In the recent dry weather one had to turn one's back to a passing motor or traction engine, to avoid being partially blinded Ly dust; now the rains have come it is more necessary than ever to turn to dodge the volumes of sludge every passing vehicle hurls about. There is a length of the Manchester-road beyond Paddington that has been worked into ruts, the like of which have not, been seen for years. Those ruts, in a frozen condition,, will mean disaster to traps, cycles and motors, but the trains, which have taken to the roads instead of remaining on the railways and are reSponsible for the mischief, may survive by reason of their weight. HELP IN THE. MEANTIME

We understand that in the meantime the local ambulance 'authorities have approached the Education Committee to establish a class at the Technical Institute for first-aid to. comply with the rules of the St,Jolin Ambulance Asso- ciation as to professional instruction. We can imagine no better use to which the .Education Committee could put twelve or thirteen guineas than to pay a doctor to give these necessary and valuable lessons. It would be a calamity if first-aid work* collapsed for the sake of a few pounds, especially when the Education Committee have Command of prac- tically unlimited funds.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
AN IMPRESSION On a Steelworks in Course of Erection.

Swing to the march of Industry! Yon armoured row of steel-cased domes Is reared where once stood red-tiled homes A shrieking, steam-girt monster roams, Once smiling fields of corn.

Echoes thunder from the site—. Excitement greets the innovation. No local need for deprivation, Day by day the excavation Takes ,its toll of hands.

Furnaces are raised aloft, Sinuous, Heaven-lighting, flaming things. Crash on crash the metal rings, And from the shapeless mass there springs Tall cylinders of iron.

Brick by brick shoot up united Households of stupendous powers, Boilers, mills, steel web-like towers, While beneath them subtly cowers A snake-like main—for gas.

Swift the toil and sure advancing, The opening day's approaching fast, A furnace will be soon in blast— Huge forces organised at last— And steel from iron created. S. G. THROSSELL (Latchford) in "T.P.'s Weekly."

UNEXPECTED SUPPORT FOR THE

LAND POLICY.

Hide-bound Toryism has reason to take alarm at the prospect of the Government Land Policy finding support far outside the ordinary party boundaries. It, is not only "Young. Oxford" which has been converted. Last week ,the Oxford Union Society rejected by a large majority a motion of no confidence in the Gov- ernment Land Policy, after a persuasive speech by Mr. LLOYD GEORGE. On Tuesday, the Cheshire Chamber of Agriculture passed unani- mously the following resolution:

" We consider it is to the general interest that the agricultural labotirer should secure a' wage which will enable -him to maintain himself and family in comfort. We do not disagree in principlq, with the proposal to fix wages by some sort of wage tribunal. It must, however, be laid down that any pro- posals shall provide that lower wages than the minimum may be paid to partially effi- cient, labourers."

This is a practical acceptance of the policy sug- gested by the Land Committee's IlepOrt; and explained in Mr. LLOYD GEORGE'S speeches.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
MAKING TROUBLE FOR THEM-

SELVES.

There has been a determined Tory effort to hustle the Prime Minister over the Irish ques- tion. Last week the "Times ". professed to be in a .position to announce the Government's deeision upon concessions to be offered to Ulster. This week the Times " has jeined in a general chorus from the Tory Press, complaining that the Government will not declare its policy A month ago, rr. ASQUITH asked for a full and frank exchange of opinion. There has been a great divergence of opinion in the Tory speeches subsequent to that invitation. The Opposition must be singularly blind if , they imagine that, when suggestions are invited by the Government for guarantees which would satisfy Ulster, they are quite free of responsi- bility for the unrest which they have to a large extent encouraged and fomented in Ulster, and can leave to the Government the whole task of finding a solution which will satisfy the dis- contented Ulster minority. They are blinder still if they, do not see that, should Home Buie be defeated and the Irish majority put into the position now occupied by the Ulster minority. a Tory Government would be confronted by a much graver difficulty than now confronts the Government.

WOMEN LIBERALS.

A Good Ship That Has Had a Successful Voyage

AND HAS OTHERS IN STORE.

One very significant feature in the report of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association which will cause the Tariff Reform dames to have many jealous pangs is the fact that the Women's Liberal As.tociation can boast of 2,431 paying subscribers.

Great enthusiasm prevailed at the annual meeting of the association, which was held in the Winmarleigh Cafe yesterday afternoon. After a committee meeting, Mrs. Crosfield pre- sided over a large number of auxiliaries, when the ordinary business was transacted.

A CLEVER SIMILE. The Good Ship " W.L.A." In the course of a lengthy and interesting report, Mrs. OSBORNE, the energetic secretary, said :

In submitting the 22nd report, I am glad to be able again to congratulate thd members on the continued prosperity of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association. During the past year the association has been joined by many new members, and has lost only very few, by withdrawals and deaths. We have to regret the loss, by resignation, of Mrs. Walker, who had so ably filled the position of honorary

treasurer. Our best thanks are duo to her for her .untiring efforts on behalf of the association for upwards of 11 years. She is succeeded by Miss Crosfield, who, we know, will do the work well. (Applause.) The association now numbers 2,43l—honestly— paying subscribers. Numbers alone, however, do not constitute the welfare of a society; they must be accompanied by work, by evidence of life, by effort for the ends for which the society exists. Looking back on the events of the past year, we can say with truth that the declared purposes of the association have been diligently followed out.

The chief events of the past year may be summarised, to use a nautical simile, as follows: The good ship " W.L.A." has arrived in port, after a very successful year's voyage. She has carried passengers to Fairfield, Howley, Latch- ford, Whitecross, Orford, Town Hall, Bewsey, and St. John's, where. successful tea meetings and socials have been held; to picnics at South- port and Belle Vue, to_presentations, to the lay- ing of a foundation-stone, to a fancy dress ball, to public meetings, conveyed delegates attending conferences at London, Chorley and Chester; and now we are met to sign on the crew to be in readiness for the next voyage, 1914, when we hope to have our new. dock—the Crosfield Memorial Hall—to embark the 2,431 passengers, whose names are already booked, and those others who will join us in our cruise of good hope. • In our President we havo a splendid Commo- dore Captain. Our Vice-presidents ably assist her a-s experienced officers; our Hon. Treasurer i 8 a trustworthy purser; our committee are engineers with every qualification for their posi- tions. The auxiliaries are the sailors, who keep I the ship to a high level of efficiency. There is work for all to do, and we want all those who have not yet signed on to do so, and to put their heart in the work for which they feel them- selves most suited. When the crew is signed on again, all is ready for the passengers, and soon we may have to signal for the pilot—Mr. Crosfield—who will take us safely through the turbulent seas, until we arrive at Westminster, when he will take leave of ug, so that he can take the seat awaiting him in the House of Commons. (Applause.) After giving an interesting resume - of the functions which took place during the year, Mrs. Osborne continued: In connection with the pic- nic, the Honorary Secretary organised a draw for dolls, kindly given by Mrs. A. H. Crosfield, Miss Crosfield, and Mrs. P. H. Brown, Orford, in aid of the "Walter McLaren" Memorial, and the proposed Auxiliary Fund of the Women's Liberal Federation. Including donations of 10s. from Mrs. Z. Armitage and ss. from Miss B. Dutton, a sum of .25 was realised. (Applause.) RESOLUTIONS SUBMITTED. .._„;

Resolutions protesting against the new Work- house Order, and requesting the Government to postpone it until it could be discussed in Par- liament, were sent to the Prime Minister and the President of the Local Government Board. A resolution was sent to Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., asking him to suppOrt any amendment making the maternity benefit the wife's legal property, as we believe that the benefit should be legally hers. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR AND MAYORESS.

We offer our congratulations to the Mayor and Mayoress on their attainment to those honour- able positions, and hope that their year of office may be successful in a marked degree, as we have every hope that it will. (Applause.) In conclusion, thanks are due to all those who have so actively, associated themselves with the work during the past year. The 'future *is bright with hope, and the unanimity which exists amongst the committee, officials, and members make t; one proud to belong to the Warrington Women's Liberal, Association. (Applause.) Mrs. CROSFIELD commented upon the excellent and satisfactory report, which was adopted, on the proposition of Mrs. O'BRIEN.

Armitage, Mrs. James Dodds, Mrs. W. Pierpoint, Mrs., Henry Roberts, Miss Bessie Dutton, and the Mayoress, Mrs. Peter Peacock: honorary treasurer, Miss Cros- field ; secretary, Mrs.' E. Osborne; General Com- mittee, Mesdames Pemberton, Locker, T. Locker, F. Lindley, P. Delaney, H. Dutton, O'Brien, Halliwell, Bunch, J. Locker, Pendlebury, Haw- thorn, Sanders, Browne and Percival, and the Misses Walinsley, Booth and Short.

The Treasurer's report showed that there was a balance in the bank of et 24, and it was ex- plained that there had been several large items of expenditure which would not have to be met next year.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
MRS. CROSFIELIYS WATCHWORDS.

The report having been adopted, Mrs. CROSFIELD gave her presidential address. She thought the year's work ha Ll been very sat- isfactory all round. She was proud bf the asso- ciation, and she thanked them for re-electing her as President. The Secretary's report showed that they had done good work in the social department, and the auxiliaries knew what had been done in the educational depart- ment. They were also satisfied with the elec- tion of Councillor' Peacock as Mayor for War- rington—(hear, hear)—for she believed it was many years since they had the pleasure and privilege of having a Liberal as Mayor. She would like to give them three watch- words for the • coming year—Encouragement, Energy, and Enthusiasm. By encouragement' she meant that they must do everything with their whole heart; by energy that they ought to put their whole strength in the work they were doing. They wanted concentration of all their efforts in, the great cause. (Hear, hear.) They must also work with enthusiasm—not only with great faith and zeal, but with ardour. Mrs. Osborne had referred to the ship, which was a very happy, simile, and she (Mrs. Cros- field) quite agreed that no work could be good if it was not organised and ,not divided into departments. These was plenty of work for everybody. If they, in the various departments all did their part, the combined efforts were bound to give a perfect result. The most per- fect result which could attend the efforts of the Women's Liberal Association was to return a Liberal at the next election. (Applause.) Their Secretary, who was so zealous and devoted, never spared time, trouble or energy for the good of the association—(hear, hear)— and she (Mrs. Crosfield) thought Mrs. Osborne was the Captain, pilot, engineer and sailor, and, what was more, perfect in each capacity. Their very best thanks Were due to Mrs. Osborne for all she was doing for the association. (Applause.) Mrs. OSBORNE said it would be her earnest endeavour to do all she could for the association, but without so many willing helpers, where would she be? She could do nothing alone. The auxiliaries, and all the members, were deserving of thanks. She was convinced that if they all worked together the association was bound to be a great success and Mr. Crosfield would be returned the next time. (Applause.)

Cheshire Lines Railway Breakdown.

Considerable delay was caused in the 'Man- chester to Liverpool traffic on the Cheshire Lines Railway at Warrington on Wednesday afternoon, by an accident to- the engine of a goods train, which left Warrington Station for Brunswick Goods Yard at 4.40. Before the train had got clear of the town the driving rod of the engine snapped and became fast in the stonework of the bridge. The half-past four express from the Central Station, Manchester, was held up on the Manchester side of Warring- ton, and 'did not proceed on its journey until half-past five, when the single line device was brought into operation. The Manchester to Liverpool traffic was delayed, but trains travel- ling in the other direction were not affected.

Boys' Brigade Service. • The .Rev. H. Ogden conducted a memorial service in the Golborne-street Baptist Church in memory of George Monk, son of Mr. and Mrs. Monk, 125, St. Mary-street, who'was a member of the Free Church Boys' Brigade, and who died 'Huddenly after an operation for appendicitis at the local Infirmary.. The lad was a member of the St. James' Church Choir, All the boys of the brigade were on parade and. present at the service. The Rev. 11._ Ogden took 'for his text. The child is not dead, but aleepeth." The Aoir sang "Abide with mt.," and Mr. Baines rendered a solo in a. feeling manner. .

Aehievement of Mr. R. Jackson Moss. Mr. R. Jackson Moss, the well-known consult- ing optician of 25, London-road, Stockton Heath, who for some years has been a qualified mem- ber of the British Optical Association, has recently attained Fellowship of the Institute of Ophthalmic Opticians (London), and is thereby entitled to use the affix F. 1.0. Mr. Moss's practice as a consulting optician has been of rapid growth, an indication of which will be seen in another column, where he announces the opening of .a new :sight-testing room at No. 6, Market-street, Warrington, at which address he will attend Wednesdays and Saturdays, for the convenience of an increasing number of town -clients.

Mr. Frederick Taylor: Resigns Posi

lion as Gas Secretary.

At a meeting of the. Gas- Committee on Tues- day, Mr. Frederick Taylor, the secretary, ten- dered his resignation, and it was resolved "That the same be accepted, -and that the committee record their appreciation of Mr. Taylor's long and faithful services to this department." •Mr. 'Taylor, who has been in the employ of the Gas Department, both in its company and Corpora- tion days, has thoroughly earned the apprecia- tion the committee so readily accorded him. He has been ill'now,for several .months, and for nome weeks has been confined to his bed. H.e and his family command the sympathy of the -town:- It will be some :consolation to them to know this, and to feel that Mr. Taylor's Ser- -vices will always be held .in high esteem.

Captain E. J. Smith Memorial of the

" Titanic.

At a meeting of the Captain E. J: Smith Memorial Committee, held at 6, Little Cloisters, Westminster, under the chairmanship of Bishop Boyd Carpenter, it was arranged that Lady Bcott should be the sculptor of the memorial to be erected, at Lichfield. The monument will take the form of a bronze statue, rather more than life-size, on a pedestal of rough granite, with a suitable inscription. Arrangements are in progress with regard to the Liverpool part of the memorial scheme, which will consist of a stained-glass window, in the new Cathedral. Contributions should be sent to the Manager., National Provincial Bank of England, Lichfield, .a/c Captain,E. J. Smith Memorial Fund. The principal subscriptions have come from private friends, but it is hoped that, many others will Also take the opportunity of joining in a tribute to so brave a -man.

Wa'rrington Boy Hero in Canada. News of a Warrington boy's bravery in the —Far West" reached, his native town this week, detailing how Sidney Smith, the eleven-year-old son of Mr.. and Mrs. S. Smith, who arrived at the Dome last summer, met his death. Mr. Smith, the father, was well known in Warring- ton, for he held the position-of works manager to Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, Dallam Forge, for some considerable time. On Sunday, Nov- ember 2nd, the young hero was playing on Edward's Lake, Ontario, in company with a twelve-year-old girl named Mina Hogg, of the Dome. The girl fell 'through the ice, and with promptitude the deceased stretched himself flat on the ice and reached towards his drowning playmate, who grasped the proffered band. :lieu-ever, the ice was too 'thin, and gave way under their combined weight. The boy • fell -through and did not again rise. The girl was ;rescued by means of a rope which was thrown to her, but it was four hours later when the -dead body of the brave lad was recovered. He was buried on Monday, November 3rd, in the Porcupine Cemetery, after a service in the ,church. The wreaths, included those .from ,sehoolmates. A Canadian paper, commenting upon the occurrence, states that "much sym- pathy is felt for the bereaved parents, but the pride which the people of Porcupine feel in the last gallant act of their little son should help to soften the severity of their loss."

The Platelayers' Dispute. Up to last evening there were no fresh devel- opments in the dispute of the. Cheshire Lines. platelayers at Warrington. The men persist in their policy of refusing to perform work out- side their usual duties. At a meeting on Tues- day night the following .resolution was carried unanimously "That this meeting of the various grades employed by the C.L.C. confirm the sug- gestion that shauld occasion arise, we refuse to undertake any duty usually performed by plate- layers; ; furthermore, we demand that the vic- timised men be re-instated, failing which we are prepared to support any action deemed necessary for their .reinstatement."

Warrington Old Boy's Success. Mr. 3. A. Jones, who belongs to Ws.rrington, has been appointed, headmaster of the Mill-lane Council School, Batley, at a salary. of £175 per annum. Receiving his early education at the People's College, Warrington, Mr. Jones; at the age of -twelve, won an open scholarship for the Warrington Grammar School, where he con- tinued his studies for four and a half years, and had • a most successful career. He became a Pupil teacher at the People's College, and after- wards proceeded to college at St. Mark's, Chel- sea. He toolehis London B.A.'degree at the age of nineteen. Mr. Jones is at present first assistant at the Higher Elementary School, Crewe, and will take up his new position at Batley on January Ist next.

Jolley, the honorary secretary, read his report,- which stated that the total receipts were .453 4s. Bd., a decrease on the year of .4 16s.'10d. The expenses had been £l9. Roberts proposed that Mr. A. 3. Jolley be re- elected honorary secretary. This was heartily carried, and Mr.,Jolley, in reply, stated that the next Hospital Sunday would be the 21st occa- sion on which he had acted in that capacity. It was decided to hold Hospital Sunday on March 115th.

STIRRING INCIDENTS AT GREAT LIBERAL DEMONSTRATION.

Last evening there were rousing scenes in the Parr Hall. The occasion was a Liberal mass meeting, and as the speakers were men of local Tame, as well as national repute, a large audi- ence filled the hall to hear the stirring Liberal messages they had to deliver. Ulster and Home Rule, and the Land for the People, were the chief themes discussed, and each orator had some interesting points to drive home. The audience were most appreciative of the way things were told "as they are," not as they are supposed to exist by the other side. Supposition was left alone—hard, cold facts were furnished. The Chairman was Mr. C. E. Parker, and upon a platform, representative of the pick of Warrington Liberalism, were Sir Alfred Mond, M.P., Mr. P. M. Oliver, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, the chief speakers of the evening; and many others, including Mrs. A. H. TORIES AND THE TARIFF

DILEMMA.

Mr. Parker's Analysis of the Situation.

The CHAIRMAN, who was received with ap- plause, said they would probably agree with him that if the Taruf Reform question is not already a corpse, it is at least very sick, and has scarcely a kick left in it. (Hear, hear.) This was not altogether due to the attacks made by such able men as their guest of the evening, but was largely due to the various alterations in the proposals made by the Tariff Reform party itself. He was amused in reading the account of the great Conservative Convention held a fortnight ago. The leader, who was made leader because a staunch Tariff Reformer, after speaking of the difference of opinion which existed in their party, those on the left being Unionist Free Traders, on the right Whole }loggers, and the centre composed of those who were neither one nor the other, but a curious blend between the two—(laughter)--said: "There is, on the one hand, a large number who are devoted to the whole policy of Tariff Reform, including the immediate imposition of agricultural duties. On the other hand, there, are some who are opposed to any change in our fiscal system. But these sections have recog- nised that, important as our fiscal system is, in comparison with other dangers by which we are threatened, it sinks into insignificance, and each section has recognised that the only way by which we can present a united front to our com- mon enemy is that each should abate something of its demand, and, as a consequence, we do prNesoewnt laetunoistedinsfprzt."thi(sLaoungied htter.)front, con- tinned Mr. Parker. Professor Hewins, secre- tary of the Tariff Reform League, representing the section of Whole Hoggers, declared at the same g,thering that the only alternative which he saw for the whole-hearted adoption of Tariff Reform was the wrecking of the partyt (Laughter.) Coming nearer home, we found League, in in s the President of the local Tariff Reform go peaking of the change of 'front, asked: "What shall we say of the latest manifestation of political cowardice and ineptitude?" (Laugh- ter.) And again, " To calmly propose, as some of our would-be leaders do, to drop the food taxes altogether is a policy which is only worthy of a party which is destitute of either principle or honour." (Hear, hear, and laughter.)

CAMPS WITHIN CAMPS. So much for the Whole loggers. Now let them hear a voice from the Unionist Free Traders. Sir William Forwood,'who, as a com- mercial man, carried more weight than both gentlemen named combined, wrote, the other day, to a meeting, organised by the Free Trade Union, and at which Sir John Simon—(cheers) —was the principal speaker "I think," he Wrote, "the time has arrived when we -Unionists who hold Free Trade opin- ions should exprees these opinions with more boldness and cand3ur than hitherto, for I am persuaded that our party will not be returned to power as long as they hold on to Tariff Re- form." (Applause.) This was a sample of the formation of the united front of which Mr. Bonar Law spoke. (Laughter.) This was the way in which the sections of the Unionist party abate their demands. Free Traders could treat this so- called Tariff Reform party, with the so-called "united front," with - indifference. (Flear, hear.) Sir Alfred and he, and all other Free Traders, had an easy job before them as far as Tariff'Reform was concerned, and, without lay- ing aside their Free Trade armour, they could turn their thoughts to the real live issues before the country. The Free Trade battle for the Present was won. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BAR OF RIGHT AND JUSTICE.

It is not my intention, continued the Chair- man, to go into the details of the questions which at the moment really interest the electors. I wish to call your attention to a characteristic of the way in which, more than ever before, the people of this country are approaching social and political questions. Every social and poli- tical issue is being brought to the bar of right and justice. (Hear, hear.) This not for the first time, but now in a more definite way. {Hear, hear.) Por example, such questions as this have been asked :

"To what extent is it right that a minority should coerce and dictate to a majority? Is this right?" (Applause.) "Is it right that in the wealthiest country in the world there should be a considerable proportion of our people who have not the wherewithal to keep body and soul together and are constantly very. close to the verge of starvation?" (Applause.) "Is it right that with our colossal wealth many of our citi- zens, with their wives and children, are housed worse than many of our horses?" (Applause.) "Is it right that, with an abundance of land, air, and sunshine, land cannot be got, except at almost prohibitive prices, on which to erect houses, *here they are required at rents which the people can pay?" (Hear, hear.) "Is it right if a business man, a shopkeeper if you like, by his energy and industry adds to the letting value of premises he holds on lease, that on the termination of this lease the landlord should have ,the legal power to appropriate the increased value, in a demand for a higher rent, with the alternative of quitting the premises?" (Hear, hear.) This, and the like questions, are pressing for a solution, and the strength of the demand for the solution on the insistence of jus- tice and right between man and man. I have no doubt we will hear more of the details of the questions I have indicated from the speaker of this evening, and I will not further anticipate further what Sir Alfred may have to say to us. (Cheers.)
