===== 0002601_19131129_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
The Home Rule Question.

In the survey I intend to make this evening of the political field, or as much of it as your patience and my time permits, there is .one question which, though of long standing, is still the most actual topic of controversy before us at this moment. That is the question of Irish Home Rule. The consistent and persistent efforts of the huge maj6rity of the Irish people for over a century have been directed towards' the restoration of that Parliament, whose home is still to-day one of the architectural features of the City of Dublin. -Leader after leader has arisen, and election after election has 13epn fought in Ireland with the same unvarying result. I will only recall to your memory the results of the elections in Ireland since the ex- tended franchise gave them a free opportunity to express their views, that is to say, in 1885. Since then the results of the General Elections in Ireland have been as follows

General Election 188-5-, Home Rulers 85, Anti- Home Rulers 18; 1886, 84, 19; 1892, 80, 23, 1895, 81, 22; 1900, 81, 22; 1906, 85, 18; January 1910, 82, 21; December 1910, 84, 19.

OVerwhelming Majorities. A democratic country has no valid argument to oppose to a desire expressed by such Over- whelming majorities election after election. There is no thinking man of any political party who does not recognise that there is a case for Irish self-government, that this case must be met, and that the only question is when and how.

As to when, surely, the time is long overdue. When I had the privilege of accompanying the Prime Minister to Ireland on his memorable visit to Dublin last year, we drove in a mighty procession through long streets filled with crowds of determined, enthusiastic, orderly and hopeful people. I could not help feeling that it was not a political demonstration in the ordin- ary sense—it was a nation expectant: of the hour of its deliverance. As to when, now. As to the method, we have for some two years not only discussed at great length the Home Rule Bill, introduced by the Government, but we have twice passed that Bill through the House of Commons by large and decisive majori- ties. On the first time of the third reading of that Bill the majority was 110, and on the second occasion 109. Thus_ it was carried, on each occa- sion by a substantial majority, even if the Irish 'members were entirely excluded from the divi- sion. (Applause.)

A Complex Measure. I will not weary you to-night with the details of what is necessarily a complex measure. I will briefly say that while it satisfies the legiti- mate aspirations of the Irish people for self- government, it maintains, undoubted and un- questioned, the supremacy of the Imperial Par- liament. It is important to point out that among other matters the collection of taxes and the management and control of the Royal Irish Constabulary are reserved to the Parliament of the 'United Kingdom, the latter for a period of six years. I wish further to add, with reference to what I shall have to say later on, that there is a special clause in the Bill prohibiting all legislation interfering with religious equality. This is so important that I propose to read it to you in full: -

'3. In the exercise of their power to make laws under this Act (the Irish Parliament shall not 'make a law so as either directly or in- directly to establish or endow any religion, or prohibit or restrict the free exercise thereof, or give a preference, privilege, or advantage, or impose any disability or disadvantage, on account of religious belief or religious, or eccle- siastical status, or make any religious belief or religious ceremony a condition of the validity of any marriage, or affect prejudicially the right of any child to attend a school receiving public money without attending the religious instruc- tion at that School, or alter the constitution of any religious body except where the alteration is approved on behalf of the religious body by the governing body thereof, or divert from any religious denomination the fabric of cathedral, churches, or, except for the, purpose of roads, railways, lighting, water, or drainage works, or other works of public utility, ,upon payment of compensation, any other property. "Any law made in contravention of the re- strictions imposed by this section shall, so far as it contravenes those restrictions, be void."

Ulster Protlcted. I would further point out that Ireland will still be represented in the House of Com- mons at Westminster by 42 members, of which no less than 15 will come from the• Province of Ulster. I want to emphasise this point, as Sir Edward Carson has repeatedly stated that his supporters object to being driven.. out of the British House of • Commons.

I could understand an objection on their part against diminished representation (which, nevertheless, the Conservative party in this country has illogically enough advocated times out of number, so far as Ireland is concerned). What I cannot understand, however, is how it can be seriously maintained that whilst this connecting link remains, while it is possible for Ulster Protestants to send their representatives to place any grievances they may have before the British House of Commons, they still declare that they will not tolerate being expelled there- from.

Parties Equally Balanced. The question of Ulster has assumed such large proportions in the public mind, and in this con- troversy, that it only right for those who advo- cate Home Rule to devote to it careful and close attention. 'Let us therefore carefully examine both the facts and the credentials of those who claim to:i spedk for Ulster. As to the facts, let us first take the Parliamentary representation of the province since 1885, You will no doubt be surprised to learn that taking the province as a whole, the majority of its Parliamentary representatives are in .favour* of Home Rule. Turning to the religion of its inhabitants, you will see from the census of 1911 that the Catho- lics numbered 690,000, and the Protestants of all sects 888,000. Thus the Catholic and Protestant elements are more equally balanced than the speeches of the Ulster Orangemen would lead you to suppose. One of the great difficulties, of the whole Ulster problem is precisely that you are nowhere in the presence of a homogeneous population. Indeed, even in Belfast itself, you will find that one of the four seats is held by a Nationalist, while in the four counties with a Protestant majority of the population, the ex- clusion of which has been sudested, tliere is' a large majority of Catholic' Nationalists.

Bogey of Religion. None of those who so vehemently protest their detestation of Irish self-government, who raise

===== 0002601_19131129_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
The Carson Attitude.

Throughout all the long debates to which I have listened on this question, and all the num- erous speeches that have been made upon it, we have never yet succeeded in getting the Ulstermen to state clearly either what they fear or what they would be satisfied with. I have heard Sir Edward Carson, in a speech in the Housa of Commons, say he did not fear legis- lath-e persecution by an Irish Parliament He seemed to hint more at some administra- tive disadvantages, which he has, however, never defined, and for which he has never pro- posed any. remedy. In fact, the position which he and seine of his friends take up is that what- ever happens, and whatever is decided, either now or at any future time, they will never per- mit the Irish people self-government No .new elections confirming the present decision of Par- liament, no safeguards, no exclusion, will satisfy these desperate loyalists.. They will bully, they will threaten, they will , arm, and raise 'civil war; they will render government impossible, attack the forces of the Crown, but they will never tolerate that Ireland, to use the expression of Lord Dudley, "should be governed according to Irish ideas."

Not As a Surprise. For those who are acquainted with their his- tory this attitude of the latter-day Orangemen does not come as a surprise. Always intolerant, always menacing and ready to burst into rebel- lion' whenever his wishes are not acceded to, a descendant of the men of 1869; who threatened to kick Queen Victoria's Crown into the Boyne if the Irish Disestablishment Bill were passed, the Orangeman is prepared to-day to address the same threat to her grandson, King George. These Orangemen are now, as they were then, prepared to die in the last ditch. Now, as then, they will neither argue nor reason, concede nor compromise. This, is not the- first time that Sir Edward Carson has tried his utmost to wreck 'measures for the better government of his native land. In 1898, when the Unionists were in power, he predicted that their Local Govern- ment (Ireland) Bill "would lead to social revo- lution in Ireland," and attacked the Unionist Government of that day almost as fiercely as he now does the Liberals. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
" Utterly Wrong."

- Of course, it is hardly necessary to tell you that his prediction on that occasion has proved to be utterly wrong. He also violently opposed the devolution policy advocated by the late Mr. Wyndham and Lord Dunraven, saying of it "that much as I detest the former proposals of Home Rule, which had been so emphatically condemned by the electorate of the U.K., I should, I think, prefer them to the impractic- able but insidious schefne put forward by the so-called Reform Association." Other Unionist leaders do not appear to agree with Sir Edward Carson in his purely negative attitude. For instance, both the Marquess of Lansdowne and Mr. Austen Chamberlain have suggested that a solution should be found by creating a system of Federal Parliaments for the different parts of the United Kingdom. How this would solve the Ulster difficulty I con- fess I cannot understand, and they have not explained. I have little doubt, however, that one day we shall have to resort to a system of Federal Parliaments, in order to enable us to overtake the work which the present House of Commons is too busy to deal with. But even compared to Irish Home Rule, such a compre- hensive federal scheme is one of enormous com- plexity. It therefore seems more reasonable to make a beginning with a country that has been fighting for Home Rule so long than to wait for an indefinite period when all the different Par- liaments could be set up simultaneously.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Bonar Law's Dodge.

All Mr. lionar Law has to suggest is another General Election, which would in any case re- lieve him ' of the necessity of fulfilling his pledges to the Carsonites. Sir Edward Carson has in advance repudiated the result of any election whatsoever, and the question I would like to ask is, With whom have we to settle this matter; who are the principals and who are the agents in the transaction to which we are in- vited? And, above all, Why we should be asked to make any proposals whatsoever? Our pro- posals have already been placed before the country and before the 1-louse of Commons in the most detailed and aut#oritative form pos- sible. _ . _

If we were Conservatives and not Liberals, those proposals for the settlement .of the Irish question would ere now have taken their place upon the Statute Book. It is only the existence of the House of Lords and its obstruction of Liberal legislation that has compelled us to pursue this question through another Session of Parliament. Having won the battle, having, after a generation of effort, reached the goal, having fulfilled the legacy of Arr. Gladstone, why should we now be asked to haul down the flag and appeal to a defeated army for terms of peace? Let us continue upon our appointed path, and let neither clamour nor threats weaken us in our purpose.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
Ready to Listen to Proposals.

- We are now, as we have been from the very first, ready to listen to any reasonable propos- als andlo remove any doubt or fear. We have said as much all along through the authoritative representatives of the Liberal party. We are still ready to listen to reason, but it would be criminal on, our part to yield to violence. (Loud applause.) Our opponents, bankrupt in policy, with no solution to offer for a question which would con- front them with even greater intensity than it does us to-day; have no glimmer of an idea of how they would deal, in case they were suc- cessful, with the outbreak of. a grievously dis- appointed nation. Throughout the whole con- troversy they have not shown the slightest trace of statesmanship, nor have they brought forward one fruitful or practical idea for the solution of a problem which every statrsman must realise as being at the foundation not merely of national welfare, but of Imperial consolidation. (Applause.) _ _ _ . _ . . .

, .. _ . .. ' I agree with what the Prime Minister has said at Leeds. We have produced bur Bill, we have passed our Bill, and we will stand by it. (Ap- plause.) It would be criminal to desert it. . .... . .

• - It is not for us to make proposals. It is not for a victorious army to make terms. We shall carry out this great legacy left us by Mr. Glad- stone to the full. (Loud applause.)

REPLY TO REV. E. C. E. CARLETON. Sir Alfred's Stinging Rebuke. I have had sent to me an extraordinary letter on the subject of the Welsh Church. It. is signed by a gentleman, who signed himself E.

C. •E. Carleton. (Laughter.) I have read the letter with a certain amount of astonishment and amusement. It strikes me as in very pecu- liar taste, and still more so in regard to its facts. lam informed the writer is a clergyman of the Church of England. Had I not known that, I should, have been inclined to describe his letter as perilously near blasphemy. This gentleman has paid some attention to my tomb- stone, about which I am not worrying at the present time, and a peculiar thing is that he says that the paoney from the Welsh Church, after expenses have been paid, is to be spent on museums. He goes on to describe his views on museums, and I do not agree with what he says, as I consider museums most valuable educa- tional establishments. He said the whole of the Welsh Church funds would be utilised for museums.

There was not one word about museums in ;h0 Welsh Church Bill. (Loud applause.)

"Most Extraordinary."

Really, this clergyman is most extraordinary. Ido tell him to first get up his facts. (Hear, _ _ _ .

_ hear.) It was very bail to be accused of stealing money for something for which you are not going to use it. Not a penny piece of the Welsh Church money is going to museums at all. (Ap- plause.) Then this clergyman went on to ask why should they destroy the museums which existed in Wales. Nobody proposes to do that. He goes on to say that the Welsh Bill will confis- cate the old cathedrals and parish churches in Wales. I wonder if this rev. gentleman has ever seen the Welsh Bill, which he has at- tempted to criticise. He would be doubtless sur- prised to learn that not only are the whole of the cathedrals and churches, but the mission houses, the palaces of the bishops, the rectories of the rectors, the vicarages of the vicars, will not be handed over to the new body and distri- buted to the churches that will be formed, and the 'Welsh people will not lose a penny piece. (Loud applause.)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY,NOVEMBER 29, 1913.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
Overwhelming Opinion.

The State has no right to discriminate in any form of belief amongst its people and religious equality for the State is one of the fundamental principles of Liberalism. That is intensified when you have a country like Wales, with an overwhelming population of Nonconformists. It is immensely intensified when you have planted in their midst for cen- turies an alien Church, which is out of sym-

pathy with their national faith and their lives. A demand has been made for generations for religious equality in Wales. That has always had my hearty support. (Applause.) It has had the support of Liberal Statesmen since 1880; and therefore there is nothing to ask a Liberal audience but to approve of that principle. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
Taking the Tithe Tax.

I come now to the question of endowments, and what are we doing? We are not taking a single church, cathedral, palace, or a single building. We are going to take that compul- sory 'tax of tithe which Welsh Nonconformists are forced to pay to ministers of religion, to whose denomination they do not belong and whose church they did not attend. The Non- .conformists had to pay, and they, had built , their own places of worship on the hillsides. This makes me very indignant, and we are called robbers and despoilers. (Laughter.) Why cannot they pay for their own church? (Hear, hear.) It is the Church of the richest people in , the country, the Church of the landowner, and the poor farmers have scattered the hillsides with chapels, and every penny of the cost they have found themselves. (Applause.) What are we going to do? We are not going to spend the money on, museums. (Laughter.) That money is going to the County Council, and is going to be used for the public benefit—(ap- plause)—scholarships for the poor, cottage homes, anil it is going to be used for purposes which everyone in the country will benefit by. And those are the purposes for which the money was originally intended. The money was orig- inally intended for the benefit of the people, and not for a fraction of the people. It has been appropriated by one sect of the people, and I am not ashamed to defend that Bill on any platform in the country. (Loud applause.)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LAND QUESTION.

The first great political meeting I ever at- tended was in this town on the Bth September, 1885. As a very young man I was taken to hear the great speech made by Mr. Joseph Chamber- lain on that occasion. ,I have looked up that speech, and I find that in the course of it Mr. Chamberlain dealt with the problem in which he was then keenly interested, and in which we are as keenly interested to-day—the eternal problem of how to obtain the best result for the public of the land of the people. I will give you one short quotation from that speech. Mr. Chamberlain said: "We propose to give the Popular representative authorities the right ,co obtain land fix- all public purposes at its fair value without paying an extortionate price to the landowner, for the privilege of re-entering on what was the original possession of the whole community. We propose also that the local authority land for labourers' allotments, for artisans' dwellings, and for small holdings. We propose that tenants should have entire security, so long as they fulfil the conditions of their holdings. . . . . . . Then there is the question of the taxation of unoccupied land, or sporting laud, of ground rent, and of mineral royalties. I advocate all these methods ,of taxation. . . . . .

===== 0002601_19131129_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
"Enormous Blackmail."

Mr. Mond showed how the land value had in- creased. They wanted, he said, the land for the people, and not the people for the landlord. Once accept that common-sense principle and they would soon step away from the present condition of enormous blqckmail. The rate- payer was the person who Made the price of the land increase on the outside of the towns, and the owners of the land had done nothing to- wards increasing the value of it. One tA the great fundamental reasons why they heard so much about labour unrest, and why the economical conditions remained so bad, in spite of the great increase and the prosperity of the nation, was that the landlords had ! absorbed out of the additional wealth an en- tirely undue proportion of the increased wealth. Here was a man who had done the least and got the most, and had neither risked capital oi worked neither brain nor muscle. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
"VERY BRAVE, VERY ANGRY." Mr. P. M. Oliver and Ulster.

Mr. P. M. OLIVER, in the course of a pointed speech, said he was surprised at the cordiality and the light-hearted optimism of the meet- ing. Liberals did not seem to realise that while they were fiddling Home was burning, that, in the words of Colonel Sykes, "war had already begun in Ulster." (Laughter.) Perhaps they had heard the cry of wolf too often. They might remember the history of the Parliament Act. ,One of our aristocratic traitors, or to be More correct, one of our hypothetical traitors, Lord Willoughby de Broke, had said that before the Parliament Act should becomr4 law blood would flow under Westminster Bridge. Sir Alfred Mond had probably observed, while taking tea on the terrace, that .the Thames was the same dear old dirty colour that it had always been. The Conservative peers were to die in the last ditch. Of course, it was only a metaphor then. They only meant that they were very brave and very patriotic, and very, very angry. And having been very brave and very patriotic, and very, very angry, honour was satisfied; they voted against the Bill, formed the Halsbury Club, and partook of a banquet. So they really didn't die. They only pretended to die, and had dinner instead. (Loud laughter and applause.)

Mr. Harold Smith's Lead. Even Conservatives could not follow out the idea of civil war to its logical conclusion. A. prominent Conservative in a neighbouring divi- sion had followed the lead of Mr. Harold Smith, and had declared that all the men who had signal the Covenant would fight. If they did not, they would be traitors, and traitors were shot. The logical conclusion was that those men, refusing to fight, would be shot. "no," said this gentleman, "they won't be shot, but people will refuse to speak to them." Was there ever a more illogical anti-climax? It re- minded him of the nursery rhyme: Little Miss Muffet sat on a buffet, Eating her strawberry jam, There came a big spider and sat down beside her, And little Miss Muffet said— " Will you be good enough to go away."

- • - (Laughter.) But though they might refuse to believe in the possibility of civil war, let them not blind their eyes to the fact that there was an Ulster problem. A mere negative position was not

sufficient for Liberals. They did not want merely the absence of civil war, they wanted also the spresence of good will. Fortunately, there was plenty of good will in Ulster.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
Protestants Favour Home Rule.

A large meeting of Protestants had been held in the little town of Ballymoney in favour of Home Rule. The "Times" threw out a chal- lenge for any other meeting of a similar kind to be held in any other town of Ulstev. The Pro- testant Home Rulers of Coleraine took up the challenge. The Town Hall was shut in their faces. Moderate opinion was repressed. In a dark Ulster lane a Ballymoney PrOtestant was subjected to a gross attack. Shots were fired, but by the guidance of Providence he escaped. Moderate opinion-was subject tat tyranny. Re- pressed and tyrannised, this moderate opinion , was growing, as all good things repressed and tyrannised must grow. And it was to this mod- erate opinion that they looked for the finture welfare and happiness of Ireland under a system of Home Rule. (Loud applaus,..)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
The Housing Question.

The Conservative platforms of the Knutsford Division had lately been ringing with the cry of housing. He took it as a compliment to himself, for he had spoken on housing before ever the Tories did, and when he preached the gospel of housing the Tories did not receive it with favour from him. The chairman at one of their meetings had said: "He wondered if Mr. Oliver was trying to get en the Urban District Council, or into the Imperial Parliament of Great Bri- tain. Let them get out of their minds the silly un-Britis)i notion that they were simply people living in a village. They should try to live and think Imperially, as members of the greatest Empire the world had ever seen." He believed he was a democratic Imperialist, but he aid not understand these things. His mind wandered away to that little cottage in Lincolnshire, that was mentioned in the Land Report. There. with two bedrooms between them, there lived a grandfather and a grandmother, two grown-up sons, a grown-up daughter, a married liaughter, her husband, and their five children. Tie endea- voured to imagine those people trying to live and think Imperially as members of the great- est Empire the world had ever seen. How stale and tawdry were Conservative perorations when brought into the light of fact. (Applause.) And now, for all their fine talk, the Conservatives had nothing to offer, but a Royal Commission. To- morrow, and to-morrow, and to-morrow! Let us act, say the Liberals; let us wait, say the Tories. And so, while year in and year out the Royal Commission sits, the farmer will see the estate sold over his head; the labourer of Wilt- shire and Dorset will receive.his meagre pit- tance, his wife must scrape and starve, and the cry for cottages all over the land must go un- answered. _ _

"We must wait," Milton had said. " They also save who only stand and wait." But the cry was only wrung from his heart when the light was taken from his eyes and he wa; blind. They, too, if they only stand and wait, are blind. But we, in the full light of day, o'er eyes set on the goal, shall march into the pro- mised, land. (Cheers.)

NATIONAL SERVICE LEAGUE.

Captain Crosfield and the Territorials.

The local branch of the National Service League were out on Thursday night, setting the seed of their propaganda, for, in addition to the annual meeting in Bold-street Schools, there were five meetings in the open-air. Captain Crosfield presided at the Bold-street meeting. Captain CROSFIELD referred to the Territohals, and said he spoke as a Territorial, and he did not believe with those people who said that the Territorials had no right to point out &ficien- cies in their own forces, because if. nobody pointed them out, and there was nobody more able to point out the deficiencies of the Terri- torials than the Territorials themselves, how were they going to get the eyes of the people opened?. The weakness of the Territorial Force was a very serious matter, for it concerned each one of them. People were beginning to realise that the ,Navy and the home 'Defence men must work hand in hand, for it .was no good having a very strong Navy if they had nothing behind it. To strengthen the Home Defence behind the Navy, they wanted a sound Citizen Army. That was the business of the National Service League. They would take the Territorial Force as it stood, and apply the principle that every man should do his share, making it impossible for a man to lose his job, because a man should not be so handicapped, and they would get over a crowd of the difficulties with which they were faced to-day. If the Chancellor of the Exche- quer did "fork out" another million and a half in order to bolster up the Territorials, perhaps they would for a time increase, but he doubted very much if the training would be more effec- tive. (Applause.) MT. COULSON KERNAHAN also spoke and the fol- lowing were elected officers for the ensuing year: Ekecutive Committee: President, Mr. T. H. Lyon; chairman of the Jommittee, Mr. 11. Roberts; Mr. E. Ball, Mr. Bellian, Miss S. Broadbent, the Rev. W. Brace- camp, Mr. G. Dale, Mr.. Arthur Down, Captain Elgood, Mr. J. Elwell, Mr. Aylmer Frost, Mr. E. J. Hall, Mrs. Lyon, Mr. and Mrs. B. S. Town- roe, Mr. J. H. Smethurst; Mr. L. Voisey, and Captain G. R. Crosfield (hon. secretary); General Committee, consisting of the above„ together- with Mrs. J. D. Fairclough, Mrs. Frost, Mr. C. ,Haslam, Mr. F. W. Monks, Mr. W. P. Ryland% the Rev. Canon Willis, the Rev. Father Wilson.

Wesleyan Sale of Work.—The Wesleyan Church at present is a hive of industry. The deft fingers of the lady members are hard at work turning out articles, which, along with those sent by friends, will be disposed of at the sale, of work to be held in. the Schoolroom on Thursday and Friday next. This sale of work promises to be one of the most interestink ever seen in Stockton Heath. There something suitable .for everybody will be found. The sale will be opened on Thursday at 2.30 p.m. by Mr. PA M. Oliver, who is well known in the district. Mrs. F. E. Penketh will perform the opening ceremony at three p.m. on Friday.

National Liberal Federation.—The annual meetings of the N.L.P. were held at Leeds this week, When a record attendance of delegate attended the various meetings. The Prime Min- ister made a great speech on Thursday night at the Coliseum before a vast audience of repre- sentative Liberals from all parts of the king- dom. The Knutsford Division was represented by Messrs. H. N. Bickerton, J.P. '(Siddington), A: W. Heyworth, J.P. (Alderley Edge), W. Crothers, J. E. Robinson, H. S. Ratcliffe (Wilms- low), ,J. H. That Swing Bridge.--Several residents of Stockton Heath are contemplating removing, on account, they state, of the delay occasioned by the delays at the Swing Bridge. "For people who have trains to catch," said one, it is a sheer impossibility to depend upon getting, into town in time. The delays at the Swing Bridge are getting more frequent and of longer duration each year, and for this cause a numbef of us will have to leave the Heath and go to a place where we shall not have such delays." •

Irish Labourers in Trouble.—Two Irish farm labourers, giving addresses at Appleton, were charged at the Occasional Court at Stockton Heath on Tuesday, with doing damage at the Ship Inn, Walton, and with assaulting a bar- maid.' The defendants were John Wade and Owen Carney.—Constable Worthington said on Sunday night he received a telephonic call from Mr. Williams, of the Ship Inn, saying that one of his barmaids was being assaulted. He pro- ceeded to the hotel, where he took the prisoner Wade into custody. The other prisoner was arrested later on a warrant, to which the pris= oner answered "I know nothing about it"— They were remanded for eight days.

FIFTY YEARS OF TEMPERANCE.

To celebrate the Jubilee of the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union the members of the Hill Cliffe and Stockton Heath United Band of "Hope gave a most successful entertainment in the Wesleyan Schoolroom on Monday even- ing. There was a good gathering. The Rev. George Curtis presided and was supported by Mr. W. R. Laws, the Warrington Police Court Missionary, Mr. F. Gandy, Mr. J. Moss, vice-president, and Mr. G. Whiffield, sec- retary. A• fine programme was given by the members, who recited the pledge in unison, and sang a number of melodies and hymns. The first Band of Hope song'was well sung'by Bertha Stringer, and the duet "We shall pre- vail " was given very pleasantly by Lizzie Brooks and Isabella Clarke. Miss llilda Taylor recited "Dear dolly" in an expressive manner, and other recitations were given by Ada Rams- bottom, Mary Ramsbottom, 'Sarah Gamble, Edith Travis, Lily Ham.blett .and Winnie Stringer.

Three dialogues. "Our dolls," "The Post Office Bank," and "A Band of Hope Talk,' were extremely well given by a number of members, whose efforts were loudly applauded. Mr. W. R. Laws gave a most instructive ad- dress, in which reference was made to the 50 years' work of the movement.

Mr. F. Gandy presented the souvenirs awarded by the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union to a large number of members. A vote of thanks to the speaker and all who had taken part was proposed by Mr. G. Whit- field and seconded by Mr. J. Moss. Mr. G. A. Whitfield was• responsible for the musical arrangements and accompanied on the pianoforte.

FIFTY-ONE YEARS OF CHURCH WORK,

Mr. J. Hallows' Remarkable Record.

THE VETERAN IN A REMINISCENT VEIN. The annual prize distribution of St. Thomas' Men's Class, B Section, took place on Sunday afternoon, when, at the request of the members, the prizes ,were given out by the leader, Mr. J. Hallows, who is giving up the leadership of the class owing to sickness at home.

After the usual service, at which tile prayers were taken by-Mr. C. !Hallows, and the lesson read by Mr:'. Morris, Mr. Hallows, in place of the address, briefly referred to his past experi- ences as a.Sunday School teacher. He started, he said, just 51 years ago, in the. room they were gathered in that afternoon, by teaching a small class of infants, and, one by one, he passed through every, class and was superintendent. In this last office he was succeeded by Mr. Lane, who would long be remembered for the good work he did in connection , with the Sunday School.

Mr., Hallows also referred to the first Band of Hope in Stockton Heath, which was held in connection with his class. He lately had the pleasure of seeing one of the pledges hanging up that was signed 47 years ago, and this, like many others, some of even an earlier date, had been loyally kept. He thought that date was a record at least so far as a Church Band of Hope was concerned.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0194.txt | included=true | included =====
Reading Room in a Shed.

The first reading room in the village, said Mr. Hallows, was begun by the members of his class in a shed in his own yard. Afterwards it was moved to the old coach-house belonging to the Vicarage and Was fitted up with forms and tables by the members. Later, through the kindness of the late Mr. Down, the members were enabled to enlarge it. It was open eacli evening from seven to ten o'clock, and on Sunday evenings during Lent a 'service was held from eight to nine o'clock.

There was a small library, from which books could be taken home to read. Miss Tofts and the Vicar took a great interest in this, as well as in the general working of the room. There were the usual games and fencing sticks and boxing gloves were used at certain hours: There was also a gymnasium and a large 'bagatelle board, the latter being presented by Mrs. Parr, Many a pleasant evening was spent there, and many a social and potato pie supper held in those olden days.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
First Public Reading Room.

Afterwards a committee was formed, and with the consent of the class and Mr. Hallows the things were removed to a large room near the Twenty Steps Bridge, and was now used by the. Adult School. That was the first public reading' room in the village and continued until the completion of the Ship Canal, when it was re- moved to the present Church Room, which was brought from Runcorn, where it had been used as a Mission Room.

With reference to the present Class B. he thought they had each acted up to their class motto, "Upward and Onward." They were nearly all working bees in the class, and no class could succeed unless ther were progressing He attributed their success as a class itore to P good committee and a noble band of workers than to himself.

In Mr. Morris they had a splendid secretary who, though a busy man, spared neither time nor effort to assist the leader and help forward the interests of the class. (Applause.) And with regard to Mr. Smith, their organist, who had been with .them some years, in fact ever since he took over Class B, he .could not speak too highly of him, and asked him to receive a small silver matchbox as a memento ,of the many happy hours they had spent together in the Master's work. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 29, 1913.

Bicycle and Fishing Clubs for their loyal sup- port.

Silver Medals as Prizes.

As he looked at the prizes, he felt much hon- oured in being asked to present them. To him the prizes spoke of interest in class and earnest efforts to attend. There were 36 first class prizes, including 14 silver medals, supplied by Mr. Crossley and suitably inscribed, and four second class prizes. The Vicar, who, being single-handed, was not able to be with them long, and very kindly referred to the work done by Mr. Hallows. Several members also spoke kind words, in- cluding Mr. Bailey, a former secretary, who had COM@ over from Liverpool to join them for the afternoon. Mr. Hallows asked them to give their new leader, Mr. Law, all the assistance possible, and in giving out the last hymn, "Stand up for Jesus," said he hoped they would always stand up for their Church, their weaker brother, and their God. (Applause.)

PRIMITIVE METHODIST ANNIVERSARY. A Series of Successful Functions.

The anniversary of the Primitive Methodist Church was celebrated last week-end. On Sun- day the Rev. Edward Beal, of Manchester, de- livered encouraging messages at the morning and evening services, his subject at the' latter being "Faith." The choir gave a good render- ing of Martin's. "Bless thou the Lord" in the evening. On Monday a large company partook of the popular "anniversary tea," provided by the ladies of the church, which proved to be as acceptable as any of its predecessors. The evening was devoted to a lecture by the Rev. H. J. . Pickett, the newly-appointed Principal of the Primitive Methodist College, Manchester, and who is well known as a lecturer of more than ordinary ability. His subject Was "Wilberforce," and his por- trayal of this truly great man riveted the attention of his large audience from the very commencement. The lecturer showed his hero, at the age of twelve, deeply impressed by the preaching of George Whitfield at Wimblpdon, when he was almost persuaded to become a Methodist, and was only saved therefrom by the prompt intervention of his worldly-minded mother. No effort was spared to overcome his religious tendencies, and for a time his family succeeded in stultifying his loftier ambitions, as, during his :University career, he was avid in his pursuit of the wild gaieties which .char- acterised the period. His innate sense of jus- tice, however, served him in good stead, as, after winning £6OO from companions who could ill- afford to lose the money, he threw his winnings back on the table and vowed lie would never play again, a vow which he faithfully kept. When eventually converted to Christianity, he promptly advised his intimate friends of the change in his life, and decided to devote the whole of his wonderful abilities to the "Refor- mation of the Mariners of the People; and the Abolition of Slavery." The fight was long and strenuous; the slave question bit deeply into the pockets of the people—during the last two decades of the 18th century over £16,000,000 was paid to Liverpool shipowners in respect of this traffic—and both in Parliament and out of it Wilberforce had to contend with opposition of the most unscrupulous nature; his enemies threatened to shoot him, and he consequently wore a suit of mail beneath his clothes, and was under the special protection of guarding police. The British Government eventually paid -t22.000,000 for the redemption of slaves, and Wilberforce had the supreme satisfaction of liv- ing to see the successful termination of his labours as far as the British possessions were concerned. - - - _

The speaker was humorous and pathetic in hums as he dealt with the peculiarities' and corruptions of the times, and the lecture alto- gether was of exceptional interest and splendidly delivered. The Rev. R. W. Ferguson presided, In tendering a vote of thanks to the lecturer and others, Mr. G. L. Welfbrd gave a financial statement, which showed that the anniversary. had been a success.

Water Rate.—The Water Committee of the Council on Thursday recommended that the price to be charged for water be at the rate of is. 9d. p„ Lop gallons.—The Council adopted the recommendation,.

Sanatoria Arrangements.—M a meeting of the Sanitary 'Works Committee of the Lymm Coun- cil a circular letter was read from Dr. Yonge, (county- medical officer, asking whether the Coun- cil would (a) undertake provision of shelters for tubercular cases on terms to be arranged; (b) provide storage facilities for shelters. It was resolved that the committee, recommend , the Council to provide storage for shelters as indi- cated.

Health of Lymm.—The Medical Officer gave an interesting report at the meeting of the Council on Thursday. He stated that the esti- mated population of the village to the middle of the year was 5057. During the last quarter 28 births were registered in the district-14 males and 14 females. The annual birth-rate was equal to 22.1 per 1,000, whilst the death-rate equalled 10.3. One inquest was held during the quarter. There were two deaths of infants. Two persons died of Amer, three from organic heart disease, one from scarlatina, one from in- fluenza, and one from bronchitis at the age of 78 years.

Main Road Estimate.—The Roads Committee of the Council met on November 20th, and the Surveyor pfoduced his main road estimate for 1913-14, and it was approved, the sum amount- ing to £1,150 ss.

COUNCIL AND JOINERS. Mr. Moston Submits Resolutions. TRADE UNION WAGES.

"Fair contracts" resolutions, that have been endorsed by representatives of the Amalgamated Society of Carpenters and Joiners, were laid before the Council on Thursday by Mr. E. Mos- ton, the Lymm branch secretary. • "The idea," said Mr. Moston, "is to keep work in the district. There has been a lot of work going out." The resolutions were as follows: "That clauses be inserted in all contracts of the Council, re- quiring that the contractor shall sign a declara- tion that he is paying, and will continue to pay, .his workpeople—except such as may be employed under special provision agree(' upon by the em- ployers and the organised bodies of workers— the trade union rate of wages, and that the conditions of employment conform strictly with the rules of the various ,trades..

"Should the. Counbil„ have, in its opinion; reasonable grounds for believing that the above conditions are not being complied with, the contractor shall be required to grant facilities to the Clerk of the Council or his representatives to examine the wage-hooks or wage-sheets show- ing the Wages paid to and the hours of labour observed by all those employed by the contrac- tor. . .

"The .contracter, or any agent or servant of his, acting with his authority, knowledge, or Consent, shall not on any account be permitted to give or offer any gratuity to any officer Or servant of the Council. "The contractor shall not assign or underlet the contraa or any part of it, or sub-contract except with the consent of the Council, and upon suCh conditions as the Council may think fit; but if the tenderer, at the time of tender- ing, states his desire to sub-let any portions of the work not usually done by him, the Council will consent, provided that the sub-contractor shall be responsible,' however, for all the work done and for being carried out under the same conditions as if executed by himself. "That deductions Shall be provided for in the event of any breach or breaches of the.contrAct by way of liquidated damages, and when a con- tractor persistently, evades the terms of the con- tract, the Council shall be entitled to determine Same, such contractor shall not be allowed ,to again tender for work of the Council.-

THE " DANCING " LIGHTS. Vi-hat Is Wrong With The Gas?

MANAGER TO INQUIRE.

Has the Tango craze spread to Lymm, and been taken up by the gas lights? Complaints were made at the Council meeting on Thursday that the gas lights in some houses were jump- ing up and down. The Rev. CT' . B. Thurston characterised the nuisance as "sickening." Mr. Donaldson (gas manager) said he would investigate the cause, and see what was wrong. He had not noticed anything wrong in his house nor anywhere else he had been. Councillor Smith: I was in a house at Broom Edge, and it was quite impossible to read in that house. Mr. Donaldson: Mr. Holt's gas engine affects the light there. If you notice the movement of his engine and the movement of the lights, you will see that they synchronise. Councillor Gould (jocularly): There you have the culprit. Mr. Donaldson: One thing is that they are short of supply at Broom Edge. The Chairman: If you go round Heatley you will see some of the .lights only half on. It it affects the street lamps it will affect the houses as well. Mr. Donaldson: I have been round to see the lamps, .and I tholight they were very'fair. Councillor Groves: I have always had a good supply, but this" last ten, days it has been hor- rible.

The Chairman: For goodness sake, Mr. Don- aldson, look into it. Dr. De Jong: I had thought it was due to a new mantle.

Councillor Groves: My light jumped up so much that,the bottom of the mantle was broken. The Gas Manager was instructed to look into the matter.

Parish Church.—A special service was held at the Parish Church on Sunday morning in con- nection with the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in foreign parts. The preacher was the Rev. A. Lewis, formerly missionary in South Africa. He said the, Missionaries had been sent to the local churches, not that they were more eloquent preachers than their own local clergy, but because they had first-hand knowledge of mission work. A great responsibility rested not only With the general public, but also their own Church, for their prayers and sympathy for the missionary at his lonely outpost in the far-off lands. A suta amounting to something like 210 millions was spent respectively on drink, gamb- ling and tobacco, whilst only two millions was expended on foreign mission, and unless they aroused their sense of responsibility the Church would be bruaght face to face with a grave crisis. PENKETH.

Pioneer of Local Ta nn i ng. —Mr. William Walker, of Whitehaven, formerly of Bolton, passed away on Thursday morning at the ripe age of 82. Mr. Walker had considerable finan- cial interest in various tanning concerns in the town and district. The commencement and de- velopment of the Penketh Tanning Co., Ltd., was largely due to his initiation and support, and for many years he acted as chairman of this company. The development, of the tanning trade in England during the last half century was due to Mr. Walker, perhaps, more than to any other single individual.

Whist Drive and Dance.—The memliers of the Penketh Liberal Club held their annual whist drive and dance at Sankey Schools, and, as usual, was a great success. There were no fewer than 130 persons present, and Messrs. Hall Bros. provided, the dance music. Result of whist drive :—Gentlemen : 1, Mr. R. Acomb ; 2. Mr. Smith Tunstall ; consolation, Mr. E. Houghton, W. Lightfoot and B. Darlington.

A Boy Hero.

The meeting over, the party adjourned to an- other room, where an excellent toast list and musical programme was gone through. Mr. H. CritchleY gave the "Past and Present Managers." Mr. J. Swinton, sen., responded. "The President," proposed by Mr. T. • Red- dish:was received with musical honours. The other could hardly be called an 'Old Boy," as he was onl3 about 14 years of age, but he was a scholar at Orford School until he went over to, Canada a year or two ago. He was out one day on the ice with a little girl playmate, when the ice gave way, and the girl fell into the water. He im- mediately .jumped in and succeeded in rescuing her, but sacrificed his own life in the effort. Mr. Cann said, in .his opinion, that lad was as much a hero as if he had been killed on the battlefield.

On the proposition of Mr. J. Whitham, seconded by Mr. W. A ttwell, it was resolved to send a note of condolence to the parents. Mr. T. S. Steel gave " The Old Boys," and Mr. John Gilbert responded.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

11r. L L. Paton, M.A. The High Master of the Manchester Gram- War School, who will be prominent at next Aursday's prize-giving at the Parr Hall, is de- cidedly one of the most popular of his class. ills services in relation to such functions are in constapt request, and Warrington is fortunate in securing them for, the occasion now in ques- tion; I quite expect to find, that the authorities responsible for the arrangements have only suc- ceeded after repeated invitations. He is a most genial as well as wonderfully energetic man, and every one of the students who on Thurs- day evening will receive prizes and certificates expect a hearty handshake,- as well as congratulatory and encouraging words, from Mr. Paton. That gentleman is naturally proud of his own school, with its history and tradi- iions extending over 400 years ; and all associ- tiled therewith think well of their enthusiastic and talented master.

Most Valuable School Period. In the course of his speech at last week's meet- ing 'of the local Education Committee, Mr. Upson observed that it is generally recognised that from 13 to 14 in the life of a child is the Most Valuable of the school period. I have heard Mr. Paton speak on kindred topics, and he goes .further than that. He holds that each succeeding year spent by a student at the sec. ondary school is of .double or treble the value of the previous one; so that the scholar who, in- stead of going to work at the age of 13, is able. to pass to the higher. grade school for three Years, gains enormously in the long run. Of my oWn knowledge several who have enjoyed this advantage are now enjoying important .and lucrative situations, and many of my readers could give Similar testimony.

The Half-time Question. It will be remembered that the Inter-Depart- mental Committee of the Board of Education, Who two or three years ago inquired specially into the subject of school attendance, "went , for" the half-timer. As sympathetic writers commented at the time, the system of a little education ,and a little work, has now .no friends ten beyond the comparatively few poor families Who benefit by the earnings of the children. The results are totally unsatisfactory, both from an educational and an industrial point of view, While the effects upon the physical health of the children are usually lamentable. The evidence laid before the Committee practically amounted to a scathing indictment of this survival of a bad industrial period. However, while propos- ing the complete abolition of the practice, they suggested that the needed reform should be brought about gradually in the sense that it should not apply to any children who at the date of the abolition might be dividing their waking hours between mill and school. As was remarked, an able-bodied father in full work Who sends his children of tender years to the mill is not entitled to consideration; but there are many cases of widows, or women with sick husbands, who would suffer seriously if The little ones were suddenly turned adrift and could take nothing home at the week-end.

"Kind Hearts," Indeed! From this to a subject discussed at last week's fleeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians IS an easy and not unnatural transition—l Mean that of a proposed .special Christmas igqint to recipients of outdoor relief. I strongly aeprecate the levity displayed whenever this is brought forward—to say . nothing of the strange inconsistency which, differentiates be- tween the two sections of ,paupers in the •Union. The assumption that the extra shilling 9r two would be spent on drink was perfectly gratuitous and an insult to helpless dependents, and the argument that it would allow facilities for "a glorious burst up" or " fuddle " was absurd on the face of it. I have experience of at least two other .Unions where it has long beep the annual custom at: his seilson to grant in. out-relief an additional shilling or 'so foi-• each adult and about half that for every child, and I never heard of any such abilses 'as a majority of our local Guardians seem to have anticipated, in case of compliance with the fieN;:. A. M. Mitchell's humane and. not unreasonable motion; in short, their .professed fear was as groundless as the rev. tefitleman's belief that the Guardians had "kind hearts beneath their expansive waistcoats proved to be.

Pitiful Cases. The circumstances attending the death of the Young miner in St. 'Helens Hospital. reminded Inc of like instances that have come under my Personal notice. I have known more than one such patient to linger even longer than poor Mather did with a fractured spine. Not long ago I spoke to one sufferer of the sort who has since died.. Helow, the point of injury, of course, the body was practically lifeless, being imme- diately and irrecoverably paralysed; but the Victim of the terrible accident was quite con- scloUs and talked rationally and cheerfully,: At the inquest on the unfortunate young collier last week it was reported that an action for compensation had failed, the County Court judge deciding that when Mather -met with his injury he Was doing something that he ought not to have done. Years ago, when in East Lancashire, I knew a railway porter whose duty it was to attend a branch line station at cer- tain hours each day, and on this account he had to walk across the town. On one of these journeys he slipped on a piece of orange peel, and ,his leg was so badly broken that he has never been able to. work since; yet he was:de; • prived of compensation on the plea that he not actually on the company's premises at the time, and that the mishap did not arise through his employment by them.

Much Married.

In a Lynim case at last week's County Court sitting it was stated of a deceased person that he had had three wives. In one town in which I lived many years there .were two tradesmen, each of whom had been married four times, and another whose third was ill said he hoped she would recover, .as he did not want to go "on that .errand again," the inference being thatt he would not hesitate to do so in case of recurring bereavement. He was like the one who tainted at the funeral of his third wife, upon which a bystander said, "Let him alone, he'll soon re- NviVe.”

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 29, 1913. ME BURIED CITIES OF THE EAST.

How They Were Lost to Sight and the Stories They Are Now hevealing,

RUINS UPON RUINS.

We have been pleased to discover that the articles on Archeology have been much appre- ciated by many young readers. We have Mr. Mounfield's promise to prepare two or three others in the near future.

All who read for the first time about recent discoveries in Bible lands desire to ask. many questions. They want to know how it is thab these things are only just coming to light, and how it is that the writings have been pre- served through so many centuries. Then there is the question, How did the ancient cities come to be buried at all? We propose in this article to tell our readers about the region called Babylonia, and,about its buried cities. Earthquakes or volcanic eruption S did not bury the cities of Babylonia, nor has the drift- _ _

Lig sand of the desert covered them, as many Lave imagined. The fact is that the cities have

ried themselves in their own ruins.

All the way from the modern city of Bagdad, southward to the Tigris and the Euphrates ,vers, stretches a perfectly level plain of allu- The clay-like soil, dry and hard, is ..ren only because it lacks water to nourish - The rain of a Single winter transforms parts of the desert into huge flower gardens. During the long period of Babylonian prosperity the valley was intersected with canals, which irri- gated its most distant corners; but now, with few exceptions, the canals are filled; those which are still open are navigable for the native Craft, or they hav turned vast tiNcts of the desert into malarial swamps. Babylonia, 'herefore,(is either a barren desert or an almost Ipenetrahle swamp.

vium

lIILLS THAT MEAN CITIES. • Dotting the alluvial plain are thousands of artificial mounds, which mark the sites of former cities or structures. Wherever there is a hill, there is a buried city or hamlet. The mounds date from every age •; some mark the sites of the earliest cities of the world; some con- ceal the palaces and temples of 6,000 years ago; others contain but the. ruins of the crude mud huts of the Arabs of our own age; yet to the casual observer the mounds, excepting in

were the clay tablets or pots or other family belongings, they were, all slowly buried by the dirt falling from above. The clay Covering, baked by the summer sun and thickened by tile continually falling dirt, forms a perfect protection. Thus tile delicate tablets are pre- served as if in a water-tight or air-tight .case, and though many have, thus been buried for several thousand years, they are as perfect as .when they left .the hand of the,ancient

===== 0002601_19131129_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
DESOLATING WARS.

Another cause for the abandoning of a town, ' especially in ancient times, was war. The wars of the Babylonian were frequent. It was the custom of the ancient victor to enslave the inhabitants of the town which n conquered. Sometimes also he forbade the sur- viving inhabitants to occupy the town; and, to enforce the commands, he transported them to another Community. The Jews were forbidden for more than a hundred years eVen to look at Jerusalem.. Saigon • transported the people of Samaria to Assyria, and Nebuchadnezzar took . the inhabitants of Jerusalem to Babylonia. It was the ancient custom whiCh depopulated many of the Babylonian cities. Whatever the , Cause of depopulation, whether war or the ces- sation of the water supply, the result was the spine. The mud brick walls of the houses slowlY disintegrated, and, falling down, formed themselves .into mounds af clay. It • would happen thilt some iime after the .abandonment of the city the conditions were so changed that others might wish to occupy the spot. A new town of unbaked mud bricks would soon spring up on the ruins of the older one.

The western builder, if about to erect a struc- ture upon a spot occupied by the ruins of an older building, removes the ruins, but not so the Oriental. The Babylonian of every age has always sought for his home some elevated spot, where he may build. There the air is cooler and less filled with the storms of dust which travel on the ground. From the height he can see further over the desert to detect the enemy which may be approaching. The only eleva- timi which, he can find' is the ruin of an aban- doned town, and upon the highest point of the

THE MOUNDS OF BABYLON. -

size, all seem alike. Many rise more than a hundred feet above the plain; others are so low that they are scarcely discernible. .:•.We...are told in one - of the first chapters of Genesis that when the early people of the world entered the plain of Shinar, as Babylonia was then called, they built a tower to perpetuate their name. There were no stones to be found In the level plain, and therefore they made bricks of the clay of the soil. :Mortar was also lacking, and in its place they used the bitumen from the hot sulphur springs. Thus the tower was built. Excavations show that this description of ancient building is correct in every detail. When the early Babylonian built his city, he constructed its• walls of unburned bricks, moulded from the clay-like soil. The king wno would build a palace, or temple, first con- structed a high platform of mud bricks, and upon its summit the palace or temple, also of the same mud bricks and merely lined with burned bricks, was placed. The private people built their houses of clay, which were sometimes moulded into bricks, but more frequently the clay was heaped up just as a child builds a mud play-house. The four mud walls of the private house were thus carried up to the hgight of eight or ten feet; an opening was left for the doorway, and across the ,tops• of the walls were laid the trunks of date trees to Support tne flat roof. Reed mats, and then layers of loose reeds, were spread upon the supporting poles, and over all was a thick coating of mud, and the roof was coln- plete. The only wood used in the construction of the houses was the poles of the roof; nd• all the remainder of the material was the unburned clay of the soil. Seldom were burned bricks employed as a facing for the, walls, save in the more imposing towers and palaces. • The modern Babylonian-builds his mud hut in a similar manner. He surrounds his gar- den with walls of mud; his troughs for water- ing his animals are of mud; his oven for bak- ing his bread is of the same mud. It frequently happens that a village is deserted by its inhabitants. The canal or river may dry up, and the people leave at once for better quarters. As one travels over Babylonia he may see recently-deserted Arab 'villages in every stage of decay. First, the decaying trunks which ' support the roof fall in, half-tilling the cham- bers beneath with the clay covering. The tops of the mud brick walls are then exposed.. Slowly they grow less in height, and sooner or later all that remains of the village is a low, Irregular mound formed by the clay of the dis- integrated bricks. If in the houses which were thus abandoned

ruin he builds his house. A second city thus grows upon the ruins of the first, only in its turn to be abandoned, and to become mud, when„a third city appears upon the still higher mound.

This is the reason why the excavator finds in the Babylonian mounds' several cities, one be- neath another. . The higlierthe mound, the longer the site has been occupied.. Nippur, for inStunce, which is on the highest of the Baby- lonian mounds, was first occupied about 4,500 years before Christ by the Sumerians ; about 3:oouOnti3r.yC: built ilt Semites,there. who then entered the Again, a thousand years later, or about 2800 8.C., the kings df Ur rebuilt the 'place; the later Babylonian and Assyrian kings have also left, there the trace's of their cities; and the Per- sians, Parthians, Jews and modern Arabs, each in their turn, have perched their houses upon the ruins of those before them. Thus, during a period covering nearly 6,000 years, the mound has grown to the height of more than a hun- dred feet. •

The two great cities of Mesopotamia whose names are most familiar are Ninevah and Babylon. It is estimated that a thousand men, working for a hundred years, could not ex- cavate the entire city of Ninevah. The ruins of Babylon are equally great. . Dr. KoldewY, with a continual force of 200 men, has worked on Babylon for several years, and, though he has employed a small railroad to carry away the dirt, he has made but little impression upon the mounds. The 'largest ruin of the group, called Babil, he has. not touched; in two of the others he removed a vast amount of the rubbish Of Arabic, Jewish,- Parthian and Persian periodS , before he came to the temples of Nebuchad- nezzar's time. Little or nothing earlier than the age of Nebuchadnezzar appeared, fOr Senna- cherib, the Assyrian king from 705 to 681 8.C., completely destroyed the city, scraping even the ancient foundations 'into the river. Thus the Babylonian cities, the great: cities of the ancient world; built of bricks.of mid, have turned to mounds. Thus the tablets of clay that record the history of the people who lived there, the household utensils, and all that the ancients left behind when their cities, were aban- doned, have been preserved beneath the pro- tecting clay. It might almost seem as if Provi- dence had preserved the ruins and their con- tents for a time when men should be eager to learn the things of the past, and should be able to appreciate and understand them. ' Perhaps in another article we may talk abbilt the discovery of the Palace of Nebuchadnezzar at Babylon. A.M.

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

Countryfolk, on occasion, still find substi-

'Rs for soap among the wild flowers of the

hedgerow. The best of thesc is the soapwort, which contains a mucilaginous juice 'which lathers freely, btit almost equally good for washing purpoks are the corn cockle, the wild lychnis, the scarlet pimpernel, and the familiar clove. The fruit of the horse. chestnut also has sapolline qualities, and is largely used in place of soap in rural districts' on the Continent..

Fighting Plague With Soap.

The authorities of the Dutch East Indies are continually fighting the danger of the plague, which threatens the communities, especially in the harbour towns, where rats from the incom- ing vessels -spread ,the disease- germs. Dr. Pijl, Government :physician for the harbour of Soerabaja, has now, found that a mixture of petroleum and soap, applied freely to the local- ities where the rats swarm, - will effectively destroy their fleas and with them the plague bacilli. This new disinfectant is cheaper and more efficacious than carbolic acid.

" Tommy's " Sweet Tooth.

An extra ounce of 'sugar. has been added by the War Office to the daily rations of soldiers On 'field service. Soldiers until recently only received 2oz. of sugar a day with their field rations, but under the new regulations 3oz. is the allowance. The War Office has not made this additional grant merely !for the soldiers'

pleasure, hut be-Cause sugar is now recognised as such an excellent sustaining food. Sir James Crichton-Browne recently stated as his opinion that the steady increase in the -con- sumption of sugar in this country was one dl the encouraging signs of the times.

World's Longest " Peace " Highway.

There has just been completed the first see- on-of one of the longest continuous highways the world. It will extend from Canada,

parallel with the whole length of the American Atlantic Coast, to Miami, in Florida. Comple- tion of the .whole road is expected by January, 1915. The section now being completed is the 42 miles lying between Rouse's Point, on the United states border, and Montreal. It is named the "King Edward Road." From Canada the road will traverse -ten states and will be the main north and south artery

through the most densely populated portion o: North America.

Chewing Gum—Repulsive Facts.

A drug authority, says science 'Siftings," draws attention to the fact That chewing gum is collected as a milky juice in the tropics during the rainy season .by workmen of a very low type. The result is it has a considerable mix- ture of foreign substances, including bark, leaVes, and insects which have been attracted by the flowing sap and become entangled in its sticky rivulet, and all the flotsam and jetsam of a tropical forest and of a camp of careless and ignorant labourers. A microscopical exam- ination" of the commercial gum even shows, .fts might.,be antic,ipated, -‘bits of epithelial, tissue, Probably from the hands of the Mexican peons.

Hot Air tor Diphtheria.

Dr. liendu, of Paris; has discovered a novel and seemingly successful method of treating diphtheria by inhalations of hot air. Having proved that the microbes of diphtheria are destroyed by exposure to a heat of 140 degrees F. for five minutes, or 158 degrees for two minutes, he tried the effect of air at such tem- peratures upon the disease itself. He found that he could inhale, dry air at 212 degrees F. for two minutes, and at 140 degrees .for half an hour. It was, of course', necessary to protect the lips and the rest °tithe face.- Having proved what the human throat could bear, Dr. liendu tried the hot-air treatment upon thirty-three diphtheric patients, at the same time treating

thirty-three others with the ordinary an dipbtheritin serum. The results were identiet .the mortality being 15:per cent.,in each case.

St. James's.

Their Majesties' restoration to use for even- ing parties of S. James's Palace, as a portion of the celebration of the marriage 'of ,Prince Arthur of Connaught and the Duchess of Fife, has proved a happy inspiration. It is said that half a century has passed since an evening party .was held in 'this most ancient of the habitable Royal palaces in Lyndon, famous throughout the world as being the home of "the Court of. St. James's," to which every , foreign Ambassador is formally accredited, and from Which each succeeding Sovereign is first Rroclaimed. The last occasion is understood to have been associated with the wedding of the, late King Edward and Queen Alexandra; and, with such a happy link of historic memory to furnish so excellent an omen, St. James's bids fair to be no longer devoted only to the courtly pageants of daylight.

Gas in Tree Cavities.

Professor J. A. Ferguson, of the Pennsyl- vania State College, reports a curious phenomenon connected with ;the cutting of hardwood trees in the Ozark Mountains. Cavi- ties near .the base of the trees are often found to contain gas. When these cavities are cut. into by the oak tie cutters of the region the gas escapes with a whistling sound, showing it to be under pressure, and if lighted it will burn With a faint yellow flame. The sides of the cavities containing gas are in all cases dark- ened, and, look' as though seared with a hot iron. The•poptilar belief of the district is that these trees are' connected through their 'roots with a subterranean supply of natural gas, and the land on which they grow is. valued. accord- ingly. Professor. Ferguson believes, however, that this gas is the product of decomposition of the heartwood of. the trees.

LALItIOHTS IILSERVED PARIS BY NIGHT.

London leans back after dinner, yawns, and settles down to rest. Paris having dined, lights a cigar and proceeds to enjoy itself. With us work is the most serious business of the day; with the Parisians it is a necessary but disagreeable prelude to the evening's pleasure. When the Londoner goes to a theatre, he must shave' a little earlier than usual and gallop off at breakneck speed in eatbs, tubes, and motor-buses; he knows that he has only four hours in which to enjoy himself; the Parisian knows that he has the whole of the night and a good part of the morning. Hence you do not see any- thing of the hurry and rush manifest in our capital between the dinner hour and theatre. As you pass along the' boulevards between seven and eight, you see Paris dining leisure- ,ly.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE CAFES.

All the while the newsboys dodge in and out, yelling " Voici l Soir ! Voici le Jour- nal!" all the while the camelots, or hawkers of toys and curiosities scan the faces Of the , diners for their favourite prey, the foreign- er; all the while, the stream of workers, who have perhaps already dined, flows fast to- wards the ibig railway stations. Down the Rue de la Paix trips the princess-like manne- Iquin—not so fine as when she shows off Doe- millet's and Paquin's marvellous gowns to tempt the ladies of the grande monde—but smart and chic enough and too proud to re- spond to the advances of the young fellows who dog her footsteps. These gentlemen meet with better success on the boulevards farther east and south, .where many an ac- quaintance is begun with a smile and a nod, to be renewed or dropped next day at the discretion of the lady. The Place de la Re- publique is crowded; the huge lumbering motor-buses thunder past, reckless of their inmates and pedestrians; at their stopping places they are besieged by suburbans who have taken tickets at the kiosk adjacent and wait eagerly for their numbers to be called by the gruff conductor, "657658-65 D-660 —cornplet (full)!" The bus rolls on and 661 has to wait ptrhaps in the rain for its suc- cessor. The tubes—" le chetro "—have somewhat relieved the omnibus traffic, and their trains are crowded in the evening to an extent that would not 'be permitted here. Men and wo- men fight desperately for places, and work- men do not hesitate to use their clenched fists against young girls and old men. By nine o'clock the main boulevards— broad. tree-lined, scintillating with light.— are given up to loungers and pleasure seekers —two-thirds of the population of the city. In the cafes friendly and family groups have collected. They lave met here, perhaps, every night for years. A party of middle- aged men—old cronies—are playing manille or trictrac in one corner. At the next table two draper's assistants are entertaining their sweethearts; further off, a stout bald-head- ed pater familia& argues volubly with the wife of his bosom on a question of business, for the husband has no secrets on that score from this consort. The waiters dart to and fro, alert and intelligent, banging down be- fore each customer his " consommation " on a saucer on which its price is marked and which he will turn upside down as soon as you have paid him. .

WHIMSICAL " MAYOIIII, ELECTIONS.

The _ election of mayors i accompanied in ' several instances by curious customs, such as weighing his worship,and the new Mayor having to kiss effusive females; but these quaint cere- monies are dying .out, just as the practice of eleeting bogus mayors has almost disappeared. .Until 1850 there was a custom at Eveshant of -electing a "mayor of Bewdley-street," an, elec- tion Which .would seem to have been promoted in a great measure by publicans. The eh:Viol] always took place in Whit-week, and the fortu- nate • one was escorted by" a procession to a well-known; hostelry, where there was a good deal of drinking, after which his " worship " was placed on the back of' a market gardener's cart and _drawn up and down Bewdley-street, at the bottom of which was a ditch, which played no unimportant part in the ceremony. As _a m'atter of fact, .ort reaching this stream the cart was turned smartly round in the hope . pitching the "mayor" into the mire, which not infrequently happened if. he had imbibed too freely. When this did occur another "mayor" was appointed and the same process was repeated until at length the "mayor" was found sufficiently sober to retain his position in the cart. Some amusing cartoons have come down to us of the "mayoral" elections at Garrat,

near Wandsworth, which arose over an en- croachment of a Common, and which idtained such 'notoriety that they formed at.last the sub- ject of a farce at the Haymarket Theatre.

iNIORN OF FREEDOM OF THOUGHT.

The name of Dr. J. B. Bury upon the title- page of a book arouses exPectations at the out- set, ,but even those who know Dr. Bury's work best will hardly fail to be astonished at the in- genuity and vigour with which he has painted a panorama of the ages within the limits of a pocketable sketch-book. Here is a bright ani- mated narrative which starts with. Nthe begin- nings of Greek civilisation ,and ends in the pres- ent year, tracing through all the mazesig re- curring onslaught and repulse.that struggle for free thought and free 'expression, which has even yet to issue in its final victory. The most important conclusion ever reached! by the mind of man was, in Dr. Bury's esti- mation, the idea that it is wrong to try to coerce a man's Opinion. But before that idea took root at all, its seed had been watered with streams' of blood., Man is naturally apathetic, and, when once he accepts an idea, he has the strongest- possible objection to, having it dis- turbed. And since ideas which are pot demon- strable, as facts are the hardest ideas to defend, man has always been most indignant at finding this class of imaginative idea subverted. Hence the bitterness of religious struggles, which filled the hemlock bowl for Socrates, and set a torch to the fires of Smithfield. Along that path is written a shameful record of intOlefance. The fining of Anaxagoras, the prosecution of Euri- pides, the crucifixion of Christ, and all the other instances of resistance to the living idea, are landmarks upon the progress of • man's mind towards light and; liberty. The conclusion .of the whole matter is that "children ;should be taught, as soon as they are old enough to under- stand,when it is reasonable, and when it is not to accept what they are told on authority."
