A FAMOLS HIINED CITY.

As a member of the expedition which was sent out in 188.1 by .the committee of the pales- tine ExpNration Fund, Professor E. Hull, writ- ing in the "Outlook," gives an account of Petra, the deserted capital of Arabia Petrcea, some of whose buildings remain 2,000, years after they were built,. Mount for rises immediately above the city, and is recognised by 'nearly all writers as the sepulchre of Aaron, the High Priest of Israel. .

We turned off, says Professor Hull, from the Arabah by a wide valley reaching into the heart of the mountains, and through which ran a stream of pure water, with luxuriant bushes of oleander and several other plants and shrubs growing alongside. Numerous flocks and 'herds were grazing in the valleyovltich became nar- rower the further we went, until it narrowed into a winding pathway leading over a ridge of rocks to the entrance into Petra on the western side, and from which the first view of the city was gained.

Having surmounted the low ridge of rock by which access. is gained to the margin of the city, the view of the interior burst upon our party in all its grandeur. We were confronted by a vast subterranean hall or amphitheatre, roughly four-sided-and bounded by vertical sandstone cliffs rising- to heights of 1,000 or 1,200 feet; 'either perforated with caverns or. holes; or sculptured with facades of temples, palaces, rock-hewn dwellings, and tombs; in various states of preservation, or else crumbling into ;min.

Beyond these other terraces and tors of sand- stone come Into view. Here indeed the' sand- stone formation of Arabia Petrtea displays itself In its grandest proportions and most varied forms.. To the left rise the massive terraces of rock culminating in the summit of Mount for, breaking off in, a stupendous cliff facing the west, with "'an elevation of 4,780 ft., and com- manding a view, extehding from' the Gulf of kkabah to the Jordan Valley and Western Palestine.

These lofty cliffs of Nubian sandstone afforded to the inhabitants for over a thousand years opportunities for sculpturing in their. varied styles-, .the facades for the tombs of the dead or the-'. temples of their deities. Hence we can recognise traces of Egyptian, Greek and Roman art, some of which are in a state of wonderfp4 preservation. Of these Khazneh is unquestion- ably the gem.

When first seen through tte narrow chasm called the Sik, which forms the channel for the stream and the entrance to Petra from the east, the Khazneh appears like a beautiful vision. [he delicate rose-tinted facade, supported by its graceful column and lighted up by a blaze of sunshine, is %set off in high' relief when seen from the deeply shaded recesses of this narrow cleft. •

The tericved cliffs of -sandstone •forming the walls of Petra pass eastward under the over- lying beds of cretaceous limestone which crown the great tableland of the Arabian desert.. But towards the west they ,are pro- jected forward; so that Mount Hor, rising in a precipitous wall of naturgl•masonrY,•tier above lief., and breaking oft in a nearly vertical face towards- .the west, becomes the, most con- spicuous height in, a region of great cliffs and precipices.

The base of the cliff of sandstone rests upon a solid ridge of granite and porphyry, and the summit of the cliff is surmounted by a rude platform on which is built a little white mosque supposed to cover the site of Aaron's tomb. The. position of the tomb is in complete har- mony with the Biblical narrative of Aartges death. The lsraelitish host:- was encamped at Kadesh on their way from Aka:bah by the val- ley of the Arabah.

Wishing to ascend to the tableland of Edpm, Moses sent a request to the Edomite prince for permission to pass through his territory by "the king's high way," and was brusquely re- fused. Meanwhile the camp of the Israelites was probably pitched in the Wide valley lending to .the entrance of Petra alongside the look with oleanders (Wady Haroun), and having on the left-hand Mount for towering over the scene. It was a fitting position as the resting- place for one so highly honoured ; and -from which he was enabled before closing his eyes on the terrestrial scene around him, to view from a disk:thee the Promised. Little James was at his desk listening intently to the teacher, who was explaining the meaning of the word "income."

A FUgERAL ACCIDENT.

An alarming accident occurred in Bolton-roatl, Blackburn, on Saturday. A funeral cortege was waiting outside All Saints' church, when a wag- gonette, proceeding to the Blackburn Rovers' football ground skidded in the tramlines and crashed into one of the rear coaches. Fortu- nately the mourners were all inside the church At the time, but the driver of the coach nar- rowly escaped being injured.. His vehicle over- turned, and the pole of the waggonette, broken by the force of the collision, penetrated the woodwork at the point where he had just been seated. With considerable preSence of mind he threw himself forward on to the backs of the horses, and so escaped with a shaking.

FARMERS AND THE DESTRUCTIVE FOX.

Mr. W. Thomas, landowner, of Sandbach, who recently, announced his intention of shooting foxes or foxhounds found on his land, on Mon- day, with three supporters, patrolled his land at Kinderton, where the meet of the Cheshire Hunt took place. Each carried a gun. The Hunt avoided Mr. Thomas's land, hut a fox and same of the hounds crossed one of his fields. They were, however, not within range of the guns.

The Cheshire farmer who is exhibiting posters on his land- prohibiting hunting and declaring "all foxhounds and foxes will be shot" has reached only the first stage of angry protest against the damage by hunting folk, says a writer in the " Manchester Guardian " Miscel- lany. Mere threats to shoot foxes do not spill much blood, and some farmers in Warwickshire a season or two ago found them of no use what- ever, so they went to work on very thorough- going lines. One day the master of the hunt received a note from a well-known farmer to say that he would be sure to find foxes in a certain covert, near the appointed place of the meet on a certain day. The hounds were taken to covert, but it was the master of the hunt who found th 6 foxes. There were six of them, all hanging by their tails from a branch of an oak tree at the entrance to The wood.

Another farmer was more FM-bile in his way of "taking it out of" the M.P.H. He, too, sent word that on the day of the meet there would be a fox in one of his coverts, and when the hounds appeared he himself was there to con- firm his message. The hounds were thrown in, and they worked the covert from end to end without giving a whimper of music to indicate the existence of a scent. It was a bad case of "drawn blank," and, bringing his hounds from covert, the master asked the farmer for an ex- planation. "I know there's a fox in that coppice," the farmer said, " fOr I buried him there myself this morning:=

Women's Work Temporary.

In the case of most women they follow their occupation with the knowledge that it is only temporary, and will be ended by their marriage, which in itself is a temptation to bid lower than the male, who consequently finds himself in the position of keeping a wife and children on wages which are low because his partner has in the past assisted in forcing them down to a ridicu- lous point. Trade unionism is going ahead even with re- gard to women, and there is no reason, why it should not assist the female clerk; in any case, if we admit that female competition is injurious while left free play, we are also compelled to admit the opposite—that the only remedy is unionism for both male and female, so as to eliminate; as far as possible, the undercutting of wages to the detriment of the male. The only obstacle in the way is the fallaciOns hope of the clerk as already indicated, coupled with his or her personal inertia and class pre- judice, and it will be interesting to see how soon the clerk will realise the importance of combina- tion as it is realised by the employers, bankers, lawyers, doctors, 'teachers, engineers, &c., and without which the position of the clerk will be eveu worse in the futurz; than it is at present

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 29, 1913.

CLASS PREJUDICE.

The Reason Why Clerks Are Not Better Organised.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADE UNIONISM WANTED.

By a Member of the Warrington Branch National Union of Clerks. In these days of militant trade unionisin and collective association for the defence of artisam and professional interests, the belated entry of the clerk into the field of activity • raises some interesting considerations, both with regard to the present position of the clerical profession and its prospects for the future from the point' of view of trade unionism. The ordinary trade union is a result of the industrial revolution of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, machine production hav- ing eliminated the individual producer, placed him in the factory and made him part of a very complex industrial organisation. Trade union- ism is therefore the endeavour on the part of various crafts to hold such advantages as they have, and to win in a collective manner such other advantages as are possible by combina- tion, though out of reach of the individual unit. It is not, therefore, surprising to find that the most skilled artisans have the strongest union, i and that unionism weakens n .direct relation to the lesser degree of skill required in a trade. The engineers, with their great society, are 'a contrast to the labourers, who are but little organised, and each represent the opposite -poles of trade unionism, while the great bulk of the workers are in the category of unskilled lab- ourers without ,any union whatever. To find the clerk, therefore, on the. same plane as the ordinary labourer is startling enough, but one is inclined to agree with the explanation that the two most difficult classes of workers to organise are the unskilled workers, because their low rates of pay preclude strong finance to a union' and, on the other hand, the skilled workers, who have, or imagine they have, the possibility of graduating into the capitalists' ranks, or at least quitting the industrial, field.

The Clerk's Position. The clerk undoubtedly comes under the latter category, for while the ordinary artisan in the workshop or factory knows perfectly well that he has only the very remotest chance of ever starting a factory of his own, the clerk engaged in commerce, day by day, still imagines he has a chance to join the captains of industry, by way of the administrative and commercial side of industry. That the idea is to a large extent fallacious is demonstrated frankly enough by the present-day condition of the clerical profes- sion, where the low-paid clerk is rather the rule than the exception, and where the competition is particularly keen, as indicated by the adver- tisement columns of the daily papers, and the shoals of replies in answer to any advertisement, however small be the salary offered. Quite apart from these last evidences, however, the whole trend of modern commerce is quite against the clerk escaping from his profession into the commercial class. Time was when under the old days of individual business, where the employer was the manager of his own con- cern, the clerk might remain in one place all his life, rising step by step, or by seniority, until he became the employers' right-hand man, and perhaps start in business for himself -on the experience gained in his employer's service. Those were the early days of commercial life and prompted the writings of men like Smiles, and the compilation of that work so familiar to boyhood, "Rising by Perseverance." 'Since that time, and, in fact, within the last fifty years, the limited liability company has come, the syn- dicate, and, last of all, the trust, bringing great changes, not only in the organisation of pro- ductive industry, the concentration of capital and machinery, and the elimination of waste, but also in the administrative side of business.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
A Mere Cog in the Wheel.

The small office now gives place to the great I bureaux of such limited liability companies and tiusts, and the two or three clerks of the office of the past are replaced by small armies of men, and women, too, for that matter, who are pretty much like teeth in a great administrative machine. Concentration of this character has led to differentiation of function, with the result that each individual clerk tends to become specialised on one operation, and the possibility of getting into his fingers all the operations of the office are remote. Such being the case, that very fact alone suffices to fix the clerk perman- ently in one particular groove. Furthermore, for effective organisation of a great industrial concern, this process is necessary and inevitable, and: to-day each operation in the office of fifty years ago is now divided amongst innumerable clerks instead of being performed as a whole by one•man. , The ledger is divided into sections, with a clerk to each section, the costs are got out by another body of men, the letters are written by scores of male and female stenographers, they are filed by a horde of office boys, and so one might go on through each of the operations of the office, while, further, the concerns them- selves are split up into innumerable depart- ments, each complete in itself, but subserving the purpose .of administrative organisation as a whole. These facts must be recognised, and the clerk of to-day must bear in mind he has come to stay, and that organisation and central- isation of capital and business are the trends of the future, so that the clerk must live no longer in the inspirations of the'past, but rather adapt himself to new conditions by Specialisation of his functions, and by organisation raise the status of his profession. The hope of the clerk for the future is certainly in organisation and specialisation, and they are few and far be- tween who will assert there is no necessity for some sort of organisation to raise the condition of the clerk, but immediately such an organisam tion is proposed difficulties are raised as to the form it 'shall take, the chief objection being the difficulty of classification of various grades of clerks.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0254.txt | included=true | included =====
Combined in One Union.

The objection has some basis and will be con- sidered, but it must he observed that such classi- fication, if necessary, cannot take place until the organisation is of. sufficient numerical strength, and, as a consequence, the first step must be the general combination of clerks in one union. The organisation should he broad enough in its scope to admit all who join, and should adopt a minimum wage for the adult clerk as an immediate aim. It should further be able to provide benefits for unemployed mem- bers, and for sickness, and also establish a bureau for unemployment.. Its course then should be a consistent and steady, 'peaceful agi- tation against the low wages and evil conditions of clerical life, and a steady propaganda amongst clerks to convince them 9f the hopeless- ness of their position without combination, and to gain their co-operation in the effort to raise the educational and financial status of the pro- fession. When the organisation has increased sufficiently in numerical strength, then will come the time for classification, and this will follow as a natural course of events. There are already certain classes of clerks who could be classified in such a union—i.e., stene- graphers, lady typists, language clerks, ledger clerks, &c., and, as might seem fit, these may form special groups in the union, in order to formulate and press forward their own demands in accordance with the special character of their employment, and at the same time have the support of .the whole body of the organised pro- fession at their back. With the growth of the combination the principle of classification would gradually extend until there would be an organisation of clerical labour,-represent- ing every grade, catering for the special inter- ests of suc,ll grades, and yet assisting each other jp. a reciprocal manner, and all bound together in one solid body, with the basis of a minimum wage and certain demands of a general char- acter.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0255.txt | included=true | included =====
A Powerful Lever.

Such an organisation would place the most powerful lever in the hands of the profession for an improvement in its character and posi- tion. The consideration of clerks and trade unionism would not be complete without a refer- ence to the question of female labour, and what acAion is to be taken with regard to it. The female clerk has come to stay, and the general movement of women in the direction of taking a greater part in the economic, social, and political world cannot be stayed by the mere prejudice or old-fashioned ideas of the Mother Grundy sort. Whatever may be one's personal

RAILIVIEN AND POLITICS.

A Warrington Branch Decides in

Favour of Political Action.

WAR AGAINST CAPITAL.

There was a good gathering of the various grades of railway workers, comprising the War- rington No. 1 branch- of the National Union of Railwaymen, in the Friars' • Green School, on Sunday evening, when the alteration of the rules of the Union to comply with the require- ments of the Trade Union (No. 2) Act, 1913, was under consideration. Mr. William Adams occu- pied the chair.

The StdRETARY, Mr. C. Wozencroft, in explain- ing the object of the meeting, stated that before the Union could take part in political or muni- cipal action of any kind, the new rules would have to be adopted by the various branches throughout the country, and. a ballot of the whole of the members would then be taken by the Executive Committee. They were gathered together, said the Secretary, not to decide whether to take part in political action, but to consider the proposed partial alterations of the existing rules to meet the requirements of the Act. He further stated that if it was ulti- mately decided to raise funds for political action, any of their members who objected to be levied for that purpose could obtain an .exemp- tion. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0257.txt | included=true | included =====
Resolution Proposed.

Mr. ADAMS was thoroughly in accord with the alterations, and proposed a resolution to the effect that they sanction the partial aiterations so as to enable the executive committee to take a ballot on the question of political action. He thought that as a rule the union stood rather aloof in that matter. He-was not going to say, of course, as chairman of the meeting, that he thought everything ought to be fought on poli- tical lines, but at the same time they were work- ing men and they had to look to the future of their families. He thought it was high time that they did deliberately do something in the matter, because there was riot the least .doubt that the privileged classes had had a long, long say. There was no doubt Labour would have to rise and take its part, and definitely, too, take that part. .If they wished to emancipate Labour they would have to use their vote in the political field, for very shortly it would not be a question of Liberals or Tories, but Labour and Capital. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19131129_art0258.txt | included=true | included =====
An Amendment Moved.

Some discussion was created as to whether it was advisable, to have political action, and an amendment was proposed and seconded that the rules be left as at present. Mr. I. SHAW stated that the workers could make themselves felt in the industrial field far more effectively than by political action, and evi- denced the cases of Driver Knox, James Larkin and Driver Caudle. He strongly objected to the money of the Union being diverted from its original course. In reply, Mr. ADAMS said the existing funds of the Union could not be used for political pur- poses. The political fund would be raised by a levy on each member of 3d. per quarter. _ _ _ _

_ Eventually the resolution iR favour of the altering of the rules was adopted by a very large majority. Cheshire. Lines Trouble. Later Mr. C. J. Tuw,utns, chairman of the Cheshire Lines Platelayers' Vigilance Com- mittee, addressed the assembly, and gave a resume of the platelayers' trouble. He appealW for contributions to the fund established for the relief of the suspended men, and stated that the L. and N.-W. platelayers at Liverpool, who had recently received an advance of 25., were helping their C.L.C. brothers_ handsomely. '

H. CHAMBERLAIN moved the following reso- lution: "That this meeting of the members of the Warrington No. 1 branch of the N.U.R. sup- port the efforts of our C.L.C. brethren, the platelayers, in their demands for an increase in wages and better conditions generally, and we trust that all grades on the C.L.C. will con- sistently give their 'assistance to their fellow workers by refusing to do any duty but their own while the dispute lasts, and we trust all members will do their best financially towards the fund established." This resolution was seconded and carried unanimouslY.

INCREASING RENTS—Continued.

On the question of rates the manufacturer scores heavily, for there are works in Warring- ton that were assessed and rated on a working day of ten hours, which are now working every hour of the night and day and Sunday as well. This saves the cost of constructing additional works, of extra ground rent, interest on capital, and there is treble the output on plant, with no additional rates. He again the source from which increased dividends are drawn, though termed enterprise," is certainly not equitable. I am not opposed to the economy, especially in plant, but I want fair and just measure of pay- ment for the advantages gained,

If any person is still sceptical as to the big profits made by manufacturers, a walk round some of the largest works will convince him of the high state of prosperity. • Who are better able to pay qie increase than those people who get the most gain? These are just a few of the reasons why the rents in Warrington are to be advanced. I am a property owner and a member of the Property Owners' Association, and I recommend all owners to join, not with the idea of fleecing the tenant, but to enable him to get a fair profit; and the worker to maintain a proper standard of living that is in keeping with the education that has been thrust_ upon ,him. I also hope that evry man who is dissatisfied with the present price of his rent will inquire if hi 6 master has a larger profit than his landlord, and make his complaint accordingly. The best means of solving this problem is for thoughtful people to insist on the Corporation exercising its powers to advance four-fifths of the purchase money of one house to any one of the inhabitants, or for the Government to grant mortgages to builders at a fair and reasonable rate and for a reasonable number of years. In conclusion, the Gdvernment has promised to build a great number of houses to cope with the demand. Unless this is done, and done quickly, we shall see an, ever-increasing emigra- tion of our artisan class.

GEORGE SMITH.

The Limes, Orford, November 2eth, 1913.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0260.txt | included=true | included =====
INCREASING RENTS.

Some of the Causes and Remedies.

HIGH RATES AND LOW WAGES.

TO THE EDITOR OF "THE EXAMINER."

- Sir,—Why are rents increasing?ilis is the one topic of conversation to-day; the one ever- repeated question'. It is heard in the tramcar ; asked but never answered in the motor 'bus; and even the Mayor is besought by letter writers to solve the absorbing and disturbing riddle. Why has the landlord done this thing which is evil in the sight of so many, and what is the excuse? The experienced man of business would not he so simple as to ask why the investing public are so carefully and unanimously avoid- ing the purchasing of house property and with one accord sinking their capital in Colonial and industrial securities. He knows the reason. Ten years ago the builder erected four-roomed cottages for a weekly rental of 4s. 6d. to 4s. Bd., and six-roomed houses for 6d. more, and these rents have obtained until recently, notwith- standing that the rates have increased by leaps and bounds. An exacting puklic is demanding additional schools of a costly and palatial' nature. Each ward is crying out for its recrea- tion grounds and bowling greens, and aspiring politicians are competing with each other in promising these amenities of a fuller life.

Increasing Rates. Strange to say, that all along the rent-payers have imagined that all these things were free. Because the rates were not paid directly, as such, they fondly thought they were not paid for at all. " Something for nothing" is still an attractive bait, but some day the fallacy of free education, free bowling greens and free this and free that will be ,finally exploded. Far be it from me to deny the public of these things, but the truth should be known .to the participants. If from no other reasons than those already quoted, the thoughtful , man can answer the question of the rising rents, but unfortunately there are others and weightier .ones. The cost of building materials has advanced at least 30 per cent, and wages are considerably higher. The Corporation have embarked upon ii expen- sive system of street paving and are rigorously pursuing it. The average cost 'of paving contiguous to a five shilling cottage is .218 per cottage, totalling for every acte of house property £B5O. Consider this in connection with the town-planning scheme, where the housA are to be limited to twenty-four to the acre; on this basis each cottage will cost about .240 for its paving. If under intensive cultitaron each foot of cottage garden produced a prize cabbage and a turnip of unheard-of proportions, it would not justify this extravagant outlay. The Corporation demand that these paving charges shall be paid by the landlord in seven yearly instalments, although they could obtain powers 'to spread the payments over twenty years. This is not in the interests of the ratepaying. tenant and landlord, for the Corporation can borrow on the security of the town's assets whatever money is required at 31 per cent., ,-While the builder finds the greatest difficulty in obtaining it at 5 per cent. This difference has to go on the rents. Again, in the building bylaws, the CorporatiOn, instead of encouraging, the builder by wise and well-devised 'conditions, impedes his progress at all points by insisting upon im- possible and expensive fads. You can build houses in a row On the warehouse style, but if you build them semi-detached they will not pass the plans with a sit-feet passage between each pair; it must have ten feet, because the bylaw for passages between cottages less than one hun- dred feet long is ten feet, and the passage be- tween the semi-detached cottages is termed a like passage. This is only one example out of a great number. But the real cause of the trouble is finan- cial. Mortgagees are as keen in their demands as the tenant is backward in paying the in- creased rent, for no sooner is the mortgage deed completed than the trouble begins. Less than five years ago money was readily obtained at n per cent. to 4 per cent., but to-day s.per cent. often fails to attract it. Consequently the pro- perty owner is compelled to accept the first offeh that presents itself, though knowing that he is courting disaster. - The "modus operandi" of the inconsiderate mortgagees of to-day is to advance more fully upon the property and any other that the builder may have than his competitors, and wait events. Should' the interest fall a day behind, at once the whole of the, mortgages are called in together. During the last three years .270,000 worth of property has been " grabbed " in Warrington, to my own personal knowledge. How has the Land Valuation Act affected Warrington? When the valuers came upon the scene they v.alued the property here at 20 per cent 'less than the cost of construction, and 30 per cent. less than in Manchester, Liverpool, and other adjacent towns, because houses here are rented at considerably below those in less prosperous boroughs. This was disastrous both to owner and tenant. The value of a house is determined by its rental, and the mortgagees immediately taking fright in many cases called in their principal, or asked for it to be reduced forthwith.' The owner has but one remedy—to raise the rents. to the price charged neigh- bouring towns.

--- - Decreasing Values. But is not the all-important question, Are wages too low? rather than, Are rents too high? Are the owners of property entitled to dividends equal to the industrial concerns in this town, who can 'afford to pay 6 per cent. on preference shares, and, who show an earning capacity of seven times that required to pay the dividend on the shares issued, and !enough to pay 10 per cent. on their ordinaries? Surely it will be granted that he is entitled to a 6 per cent. divi- dend. If the manufacturer could ensure a good re- turn for his expenditure it would be to his in- terest to build dwellings for his workmen. • But he cannot, and it has been left fot the specula- tive builder to house the manufacturers' ser- vants, and that at a much less remunerative return than the workmasfer has on the goods he produces and sells. Why should the builder sell his labour and employ his capital at the lower and more hazardous rate? It requires as much skill to build houses as to convert hides into leather, iron into wire, or fats into soap.

The resultant of this intense unrest iaust -be that the future dwellings of the workman must be built by the employer, or the employe go houseless, . The only possible alternatives are that the expansion of works must cease, Or the wages of the employed be raised to enable the increasing rents of th,e speculators' houses to be paid. Capital follows high dividends, and this ex- plains the althost complete cessation of house- building in Warrington during the last three years. Works have increased in a manner without precedent, whilst the Much libelled speculative builder has gone to the wall from lack of capital, credit and profit. Whatever may have been urged against the builder, this may, at any rate, be said in his defence, and counted to his righteousness—that he enabled the artisan to live in a better house and breathe a purer atmosphere than ever his employer sup 7 plied. Higher Wages Needed. Never in .the history of industrialism have such large profits been made; never has the cast of living been so high. And it is not difficult to 'find from whence the increased profits 'come. The Worker, though receiving More wages, can- not understand how it comes about that he is as poor now as he was years ago. Ile fails to recognise that labour is the standard of value and that coin is only a means of exchange. He fails to see that the medium of exchange, the sovereign of to-day, compared with the sovereign of five years ago, is only worth seventeen shil- lings. When he recognises this, and gets it fixed in his mind, he ;will find that he ha's either suffered a reduction or is living above his in- come, which in part accounts for the increased profits of industrialism. The property owners must keep in mind the fact that low wages mean rent in arrears and little chance of any increase. - In no way has the landlord reCouped himself like the industrial magnate. Every article that is sold to-day is dearer than it was five years ago. Education means increased efficiency, greater output, and a better standard, and again the master gains.

N.A. CENTRE.

Another week-end has passed and the sets still remain the same, and when members go through these notes the competition, as far as weighing- in goes, will be almost over.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0265.txt | included=true | included =====
A POOR SEASON.

There are many prominent anglers left oM this year, but they are only too pleased to sec some of the novices' names recorded. Members who follow up the Dee at Chester have ,had the worst season in their career, some of them-mak- ing about nine journeys without taking a fish. Angling has been; very poor this year almost everywhere, but no place has been as bad as the Dee at Chester. They say that some years ago it was almost as bad for sport, and the season that followed this was one of the best. Well, let us hope for a repetition. , According to reports, the Northwich clubs have.taken over the Weaver. Others say they cannot prevent angling from the towing path, but at present we must take this for what it is worth. We shall certainly know more about this when our delegate attends the next E.C. meeting, which is very shortly. Some of the reports go so far as to say they haVe got the river both sides up to Winsford from Northwich and anglers will have to take out a season ticket to fish it, and matches will be charged for at a small rate. If this is correct, and bank fees are charged at so much per head, clubs may fish their matches in their own waters, or travel a little further for free fishing. It is one of the things members won't stand, and if their re- spective clubs cannot cover the fees out of the subscriptions already charged, the members will vote against their matches being fished in waters where a charge is made. The subscription for the *Working Men's Angling Society is high nnongh already, and it will have to be a very serious affair before the officials will entertain any , such idea. With more members anglers will have the same sets and specials to fish for, and there would be every likelihood of subscrip- tions dropping off instead of increasing. Members who visited the Gowy had poor sport again, and we Cannot advise anyone to pay this fishery, a visit before the proper baits are in full go. Members will visit this place weekly, and they may just catch fish on the -go, but,' accord- ing to returns, we fail to find where big catches have been taken about this time of the season. • The best catch has again come from North- wich. Roach up to 12oz. were taken, and alto- gether the mgmber had a very fair catch of roach, perch and chub. The canals are 'doing very little everywhere, and members may as well try their waters near home at present. None of the waters are fish- ing as they should be. A. S.

HOCKEY.

LATCHFORD SENIORS DEFEATED.

Latchford Ist played Alderley Edge at Chel- ford and were beaten 4-2. Latchford were short of Travis, which means a lot in the forwards. Itowever, they were soon on the attack, but rather disjointed forward work led to their being driven back, and the home forwards, ably led by E. L. Platt, made 'a number of attacks on the Visitors' goal. Had it not been for sterling work by Fletcher, Isherwood and Knight, the visitors would have been more than two goals • down at, half-time. At outside-left Alderley have Armstrong, the Cheshire County forward, but on Saturday Isherwood and Flet- cher tackled and dispossessed him almost invar- iably, so that most of the danger came from the inside men. In the first half these n:ien put on two goals. Latchford's forwards had failed to get in any deadly shots. The second half was a constant attack by Latchford on the Alderley goal. In the first minute Dale got through, but his shot was just touched by the advancing goalkeeper and turned round the post. The attack was kept up, and only fine goalkeeping and hard hitting by the backs kept Latchford out. Alderley broke away twice and scored two goals. How- ever, Latchford kept on attacking, and Pearson scored twice for them, and the match ended with a score of 4-2 in favour of Alderley; Knight, Isherwood and W. Fletcher played finely for the losers.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0267.txt | included=true | included =====
Latchfilrd Second Win.

The second eleven beat Mid-Cheshire by 6-1. They were fortunate in having the assistance of Travis, who fairly made the forward line go. Two goals early on by Travis and Charlie New- bold put the home tea& in a good position, but Mid-Cheshire did plenty of attacking, and Myne- kyme wos called upon several times and brought off good saves. As the game wore on Latchford got the upper hand, and Marsden did the "hat trick," his third goal being very clever. The visitors, after nice passing, responded with their only goal. McCawley gOt about the best goal of the match just on time, with a rousing shot from the edge of the circle. Fox, Cutter 'and Cottingham played well for the Mid-Cheshire defence. Eccles, at right back, for Latchford, touched his best form, and his "interceptions were cute. F. C. Newbold was a grafter at centre-half, and the wing halves, Banks and Bradshaw, did a lot of neat work. Travis and Marsden were the best of a strong forward line, the former working some splendid positions, although closely shadowed all the game.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0284.txt | included=true | included =====
A POINTED CRITICISM.

Threequarter Line Needs Amending.

By " Lakelander."

"What do you think of the match?" Such was the question fold' to me by "Scrum," as we sat in the Press Box, watching the close of the encounter between those old-time rivals—War- rington and Widnes. My answer to this query was :1" Not much !" and I think I am right when I say my verdict coincided with that of "Scrum." In my opinion, the game must havt been very disappointing, as far as scientific fbot- ball goes, to the large number of spedtators who lined the arena—the largest " gate " so far this season, I should imagine.

It is not my intention to give a resume of the game itself, as that will have been done full justice to by "Serum," whose reports in ‘" The Examiner" week by week prove such interest- ing reading, but just to give my impression of some of the Warrington players as they ap- peared to me on Saturday. Taking the place of honsour first—held by Ben Jolley—no' fault could be found there. I was very pleised with his display, and it was grati- fying to see him play so well against his "next door neighbours," so to speak. His fielding of the ball was perfect, and the judgment he used in his kicking and touch-finding stamps him as one of the best of full-backs in the N.U. It was no fault of his that Widnes were allowed to score that lucky try!

Threequarters the Weak Spot. The thr_eequartel.• line again proved the weak spot in the side, and something will have to be done there before the "cup ties" come along, if we are to make the same progress as last season. George Dickenson undoubtedly was the best of the bunch, and I was delighted to see him play such a good game. 114 e gave us some splendid defensive play, and the only thing lacking is speed, but one cannot expect him to have a superabundance of that after so many years' service to the Warrington Club! The rest of the quartet were a very poor lot, as far as my opinion of -them ,goes. Bradshaw could do nothing right, and repeatedly dropped the ball when well placed. He most certainly had an " off " day, although he has played very consistently up to Saturday's match.

Brookes, on the other wing, is not the player he used to be, and I must say I do not like his style of play, as shown to us in his recent games. I am afraid he has had his day.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0285.txt | included=true | included =====
Ruddick as a Threequarter.

Renwick, too, was very disappointing, and I most certainly hold him responsible for Wid- nes's try. The Warrington Committee will have to persevere with the "young blood," and surely the position the "A" team holds at the head of the Combination table proves that we have some talent on the register! I uphold " Scrum " in his argument that Ruddick ought to operate in the threequarter line, and now that we have so many half-backs I maintain that Dainteth; too, ought to be persevered with in that position. It most certainly was in this department where the finer arts of the game were missing on Saturday. Ruddick again was in fine form and played a rattling good game. It is a treat to watch his style of play, and he is a worker from start to finish. I wish we had one or two more like him. Dainteth, too, played very well, and did some most useful work, and a better understanding now exists between Ruddick and him. I think it would be impossible to improve on the forwards, and they have given us some fine exhibitions of late, and if we could only get a threequarter line to put on the finishing touches to the fine work of this department, Warrington would soon be climbing the League ladder, and not be clinging to the same rung, as they. have for the past two or three seasons—about half- way.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0286.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON "A " TOP THE

TABLE.

A Fine. Win at Widnes.

~„ . Warrington " A " have set an example to the SeDiors how to win matches, and on account of the persistently fine form of the _" A" men they are now at the top of the Combination table: They have won nine out of the ten matches played, have scored 119 points against 38, and have a percentake of 90. The Warrington " A's " played another fine game on Saturday, when they defeated Widnes "A," on the Widnes ground, by 12 points to 3, The visitors put in a great deal of hard work in the first half, and it was surprising they were so long in scoring. Douglas, the home back, was playing a magnificent game, and this was responsible for the inability of Warrington to score until, 'near half-time. Waywell did the needful, but his try was not converted, and the teams turned round with Warrington leading by one try to nil. The second half was chiefly in favour of War- rington. Every Man played his best. Howard Davis scored twice and 'Waywell once. The Warrington goal-kicking, however, was . very poor, and. no goals were added. The game re- sulted: Warrington "A," 4 tries (12 points); Widnes "A," 1 try (3 points).

Pendleton Their Latest Victims.

In the Manchester and District League, First Division, Rylands' Recs. met Pendleton, at Pendleton. Both team's were well represented, Bramhall.taking the place of F. Horrocks, who was indisposed. Taylor kicked off for Pendleton, I Bran:than returning. After even play the "Wiremen " began to attack, Bramhall nearly scoring through a follow up. From the ensuing play Warren dropped a goal for Rylands'. Good play by the Pendleton backs saw F. Leah mak- ing tracks for the line, but he was grassed just in time. The ball was worked to the other end, where, from the pack, the ball was passed among the backs for Morris to score. Bramhall converted far out. From a penalty Bramhall kicked another goal. Half-time arrived with the score: Rylands', 3 goals 1 try (9 points); Pendleton, nil. Stretford commenced, the second, half. The Pendleton forwards played better now, and this helped the halfback to give his threequar- ters plenty of play, but Rylands' defence held out. Pendleton spoiled many good chances by obstruction. From a misunderstanding 'be- tween the Rylands' backs on the line, Flanagan scored. The goal-kick failed. The game thus ended in favour of Rylands' by 3, goals 1 try (9 points) to Pendleton, 1 try (3 points). It was a hard-fought game, both sides playing for all they knew. This deposes Pendleton from the top of the League.

Latchford Could Only Draw Their Tie With Lymm at Latchford.

STIRRIRIG WARRINGTON LEAGUE GAMES.

The followers of the Association code were well catered for on Saturday in having two Cheshire Amateur Cup, ties played within the district. In Latchford and Lymm two old op- posing forces faced each other on the Thelwall- lane enclosure. The tie was favoured by the largest attendance of spectators this season, and they were not disappointed, for a keen and ex- citing struggle was witnessed from start to _

The home club were minus the valuable services of J.C. Bardsley, for whom S. Caldwell played as substitute, and did credit to himself in his unusual position, while the visitors had their usual eleven. The game opened at a fine

pace, and after several atempts by the home team, the visitors were the first to score. The home 'side were successful in equalising just before half-time. The second portion of the game was just as strenuously contested, with Latchford doing most of the pressing, but the visitors' defence held out pluckily, and pre- vented any further score, although on one occa- sion Blade was lucky in diverting a grand shot from Webb. The game resulted in a draw of one goal each. The replay will take place at Lymm. Hinde, Clare, Woods and Barber, for the visitors, and Robinson, Downs, Webb, Smith and Lees, for the home team, were the most prominent Players.

By 4 4 Linesman."

Simcock ; Gosling and Woodward; Magill, Denny and Gifford; J. Waller, Smith, Jones, Lockhart and Tayll. Oughtrington United: Burrows; Thomasson and Kay; W. Lord, Owen and C. Lord; Gresly, Haddon, Rouse, Owen and A. Holt. Referee, S. Breeze. _

Denny, the home captain, was successful in the spin of the coin, and after the opening ex- changes the home team was the first to become dangerous, and good play by Jones and Lockhart forced Thomasson to concede a corner. The flag kick was well placed by Magill, and a good clearance by Kay enabled the visitors', forwards to make an advance on the home citadel. Rouse passed out to Holt, who tested Simcock with a good shot, which was easily got away. Denny „set the right wing going, and Willer, getting past the visitors' backsoshot high over the bar. End-to-end play followed, and infringements of the off-side rule put the visitors out of play on several occasions. From a free kick Taylor on the home left made a grand run, and lent in a long, dropping shot, which was only inches the wrong side of the cross-bar, with the goalkeeper completely beaten. A raid to the other pid by good combination of the visitors' forward line followed. Owen tested Simcock, who again saved well at the expense 'of a corner, which proved fruitless. A grand run for the home team between Taylor, Lockhart and Jones was brought to a successful issue by Smith, who hooked the ball into the net and opened the score. An accident to Lockhart caused a stop- page, and he was away abopt ten. minutes. A free kick against Magill looked dangerous for the Heathens, but the opportunity was lost by Gresty shooting behind. The visitors were now playing hard to get on equal terms, but they found the home defence at the top of their form, and they would not yield. Half-time arrived with the score one goal to nil in favour of the home team.

On resuming, the visitors entered upon their task in no half-hearted way, and 'They met a strong 'defence in Simcock, one of his saves being greeted with loud applause. From a breakaway the home forwards transferred play to the other end, and, after several attempts, were driven back by Thomasson and Kay. The home half-backs continually broke up the com- bination of the visitors' forwards. A splendid effort by C. Lord, Holt afid Haddon was neutra- lised by a capital save by Simcock. A spell of mid-field play was now indulged in, and both sets of half-backs played well. Kay, Gosling and Woodward, for their respective sides, cleared their lines effectively. From an attack on the acme goal an appeal for hands in the penalty area was not allowed, and when the filial whistle w‘ent, the home forwa,rds were at- 'tacking the visitors' goo, but no 'score took' place. Result: Stockton Heath, one goal; Oughtrington United, nil.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0297.txt | included=true | included =====
Stockton Heath's Win.

On the West Avenue ground,Stockton Heath, a very exciting struggle for supremacy was wit- nesSed.between the 'heathens ;and Ong'Arington: -United, when the Heathens -rose to the occasion and indicted defeat on their opponents by one goal to nil. As the reputation of the visitors was well known, and had been made more so by their recent achievement in defeating Latch- ford in the Cheshire Senior "Cup Competition only a fortnight previously, the victory of the home team speaks well of the abilities of the players the committee have under their charge. The Heathens have now avenged themselves for the defeat they sustained last season in the Altrincham Amateur Cup tie, which was played at Heatley. By their success, the supporters of the club are on very good terms with their favourites, and anticipate further success when the next round is played. The draw is anxiously awaited.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE

NOTES.

The most interesting encounter in the above competition .was played at Acton Bridge, when the Weaver Refining Co.'s team was opposed by the Great Sankey team. As it was well known the result would have a great bearing on the championship, the game was keenly and evenly. contetEited, and at the close of play the visitors gained the verdict by the odd goal in seven. The Daresbury team was pitted against the Orford Barracks team at Moore, where the Sol- diers gained the verdict by two goals to one. In Victoria Park the Adult School met Cros- field's Reserves in the return match. The visit- ors had their ranks strengthened by the inclu- sion of three of their first team players. The I home team secured the winning point, and thus they still retain their unbeaten record, a feat of which the club is justly proud. In the Second Division a very exciting en- counter took place between the Excelsior and Bank Quay Recs. on the Bewsey-road ground. The visitors only gained the verdict by means of a penalty kick, which Spencer converted at the second attempt. By this win the Excelsior are challenging the Orford St. John's Club very strongly for premier honours.. . -

The. -Fairfield team opposed the reorganised team of Emmanuel Old Boys at Orford-road, and suffered defeat to the tune of five goals to none, all of which were scored during the first half.

CHESHIRE AMATEUR CUP. Third Round.

Latchford v. Lymm. At Thelwall-lane. The following players turned out—Latchford': Kite; Robinson and Caldwell- Downs, Webb and Carter; Smith, Challino;, Lees, Siddall and Taylor. Lymm : Haddon; Hinde .and Wilson; Clare, Woods and Daniels; Guest, Haddon, Barber, Jones and Owen. Referee, J. Smallwood. The home cap- tain won the toss and defended the lane goal, with the sun behind his players, and Barber kicked off before the largest crowd of the season. It was soon evident .that the game would be keenly contested. The home forwards were the first to become aggressive, a centre from Smith being forced over the line. Chal- linor was next prominent with a grand indi- vidual run, beating several opponents, and when in shooting range he sent the ball over the bar, with only Haddon to beat. From the goal kick the visitors began to make their presence known, and made- several rushes on the home goal, but the defence proved 'equal to the calls on them, and drove the 'attackers back into their own lines. Taylor skimmed the bar with a rasping shot. Following a series of even ex- changes the visitors broke away on the right. Guest with a grand centre hit the upright, and Jones rushed in, met the ball on the rebound, and drew first blood for the visitors. From the centre kick the home team set Up a warm attack on the visitors' goal. Lees was only inches wide from a centre by Smith, and Downs headed wide of the mark from a corner. This brought a temporary relief to the visitors, but the home- sters would not be sent back, and Smith, who had been playing grandly, made another fine centre, which Siddall fastened upon and equal- ised the score. Shortly after the interval was called with the scores equal. After the usual breather the game was re- started, and both teams were playing strenu- •cfusly.to obtain the lead. The visitors were the first to attack, and 'Robinson and Caldwell had to work very hard to keep them out, without Kite being seriously tested. The defence on, both sides played a good game, and the goal- keepers had little to do. The quality of play deteriorated owing to the shady tricks of some of the visitors, several free kicks being given near to the penalty area, and on one occasion the visitors' goal had a very lucky escape from downfall. Hinde got his head to a fine shot from Webb, and conceded a corner, which was not improved upon. This brought relief to the visitors, but they could do little with the home defenders at this period, and only tested Kite with long. shots, with which The easily dealt. At the other end Lees was distinctly unfortu- nate with a fine effort, which hit the under- side of the cross-bar, and a confident appeal for a goal was made, but the referee did not grant it. As time was drawing near both ends were • visited in turn, but the downfall of either' goal was not brought about, and at the close of play the score was one goal each.

area with his hands. H. Ditchfield took the re- sultant kick, placed it well out of the reach of Beans, and thus registered the first point for the visitors. Daresbury V. Orford Barracks. Played on the ground of the former at Moore, and the following players opposed each other— Daresbury: S. Ravenscroft; S. de Prez and H. Antrobus; W. Forster, A. Cash and F. Ryder; H. Forster, H. de Prez, M. Cartwright, A. Ryder. Barracks: Morrison ;-Stevens and Cal- low; Staples, Trafford and Topping; Campbell., Birkenhead, Roberts, Tansley and Spicer. The visitors now came away on the left, and a good centre by Spicer caused trouble to Ravenscroft, who made a fine save. Roberts increased the lead for the visitors just before the interval, when the score was two goals against one. The home players very rarely got over the half-way line, and on one occasion, when they made a rush, H. de Prez missed a golden opportunity of equalising. The visitors failed to increase their lead, and when the whistle blew the score was: Orford Barracks, two goals; Daresbury, one goal.

Second Division.

Warrington _Excelsior-v. Bank Quay Recs. The meeting of the above clubs on the Bew- sey-road ground was witnessed by a great crowd of spectators, and the following players turned out, under the control of Referee G. The opening exchanges were very quiet, until Green and Davies brought the play into the home quarter by good combina- tion. Boyd eventually cleared the danger. The players now settled down to their game, and the spectators were treated to a grand exhibition of passing. The visitors put the ball through, but off-side deposed .them of success. Each end was visited in turn, but no score took place, and at half-time neither side could claim the advan- tage. On resuming the visitors made a determined onslaught on the home..goal, but Spencer was a host in himself and repeatedly broke up the attack, and the home half-backs gradually opened out the gamoband there was a spell of attack, at the other end. When the visitors' goal was in imminent danger of downfall a penalty was successfully claimed for. Spencer took the kick and steered the ball past Bushell at the second attempt. After this reverse the game became more exciting, and each team were striving hard for goals, but the defence always held out, and at the final whistle the Excelsior had won by one goal to nil.,

Fairfield v. Emmanuel Old Boys.

Played on the ground of the first-named at Orford-road, and the visitors losing the toss were set to defend the railway goal. Isherwood kicked off. The home team were the first to attack, and were met by a sound defence. The visitors began to assert themselves, and were well supported by their half-backs. Singleton opened the score from a good passing movement on the visitors' right wing. Again the visit- ors brought pressure to bear on the home de- fence, and the backs were weak in tackling and conceded a corner, from which Isherwood scored the second goal, and three minutes later Potter added a third point. The visitors were com- pletely the masters of the situation, and a mis- understanding in the defence let Isherwood score a very easy goal. The home team tried hard to make up the leeway, but their efforts I were spoiled by bad shooting. Cardwell sciired a fifth goal, and at half-time the visitors were leading by five clear goals. On turning round, the home players improved greatly, and made several good attempts to break through the visitors' defence, but met with no success, and the visitors had several openings spoiled by getting off-side. Several good shots were sent in by the home forwards, but none had the desired effect. Just before the call of time Knight was penalised for carry- ing the ball, but this was cleared, and the game ended: Emmanuel Old Boys, five goals; Fair- field, none. Players—Fairfield Dainteth; Hughes and Jackson; Day, Roberts and Hol- lows; Broadhurst, Hart, Millington, Osborne and Hart. Emmanuel Old Boys: Knight; Eccles and Harrison; Ashton, Hatton and Wain- wright; Carter, Potter, Isherwood, Caldwell and Singleton. Referee, W. Dennett.

FRIENDLY MATCHES.

Grappenhall St Wilfrid's v. Widnes St.

Ambrose's.

At Grappenhall. The home tea& soon pressed and forced a corner, which proved fruitless, the visitors getting the ball away smartly,' only to be pulled up by Hankey and Evans. Not to be denied Grappenhall repeatedly bombarded the visiting custodian, and eventually Richards suc- ceeded in finding the net with a good ground shot. This reverse stung the visitors, who got going nicely on the left wing, but T. Ford eased the pressure with a kick, which resulted in an- other goal. Half-time arrived with the score 2-0 in favour of Grappenhall. On resumption, the Widnes right wing forced matters, but were repulsed by Hankey. After repeated rushes, which were cleared in grand style 'by the Widnes goalkeeper, Stubbs placed a nice centre, which enabled Richards to score for Grappenhall, who thus ran out the winners by 3-0. For Grappenhall. T. Ford, Richards, Stubbs and Hankey played well.

Latchford Athletic v. Latchford Juniors.

The rivalry which exists between the junior teams'of the district was exhibited on Saturday, when Latchford Athletic played Latchford Juniors on Victoria Park. This match took the form of a local " Derb,i," and keen and in- teresting play was the drder throughout the game. Llewellyn started, and both sets of for- wards endeavoured to net the sphere, but the defence on either side proved sound. Ultimately, Llewellyn sectired the ball and opened the scor- ing for the visitors. This roused the homesters, and soon after, from good forward play, the scores were equalised. Just before half-time Llewellyn again gave the visitors the lead with a shot 30 yards from goal. half-time: Juniors, two goals; Athletic, one goal. The second half was of a ding-dong charac- ter, both sides playing in grim earnest nd forc- ing matters. Hankinson equalised for the home- sters. Each team now strove for the mastery, both defences having a very warm time. Even- tually the visitors' staying powers proved equal to the occasion, and from a forward rush, Howard getting possession of the ball, gave the Juniors the winning goal. Score: Latchford Juniors, three goals; Latchford Athletic, two goals.

Bewsey-road Wesleyans v. Fairfield United. Fairfield won the toss and the Wesleyans kicked off, and at once made tracks for the visit- ors' goal, but were sent back by Woodhouse. Alter a nice bit of play in mid-field, the home forwards broke away, and by fine passing were soon hovering round the visitors' goal. E. Ken- drick, with a fine drive, shook the cross-bar, but 'T. Kendrick gained possession and beat

Hill, thus scoring the first goal. Fairfield made towards the home goal, but were sent back by the backs. They came again, and this time with success, as Morrison, one of the home Backs, miskicked, and Jackson got the ball and beat the home goalkeeper. The game was level at half-time.

Fairfield now played a clever game, but the Wesleyan half-backs kept them well in hand. The Wesleyan forwards would not be denied, and tried hard for the winning goal, and at last were rewarded, after some passing between the half-backs and the forwards. A. Nurse gath- vied the ball and put it safely past Hill, who had played a remarkable game. Fairfield tried hard to equalise, but were held in check by the home backs until the whistle blew. Result: Bewsey-road Wesleyans, two goals; Fairfield, one goal.

Grappenhall St. Wilfrid's Reserves v. Lyrnm

Men's Institute.

At Lymm. Grappenhall at once pressed, only to be dpulsed by the Lymm backs. The home left got going, and were awarded a penalty, from which they opened their account. Play had not been long in progress when Lymm were awarded another penalty, from which they increased their lead. At this reverse the visitors attacked and Woodward, receiving from the right, gave the home custodian no chance. Again the visit- ing forwards got going, and Woodward scored, putting the teams level. Shortly afterwards Lymm, after good play on the left, obtained the lead, half-time arriving with the score 3-2 in favour of Lymm. Resuming, Grappenhall attacked, and Davies scored with a fine dropping shot. At this point both teams were playing hard, and after a smart, movement on the left Lymm obtained the lead. Not to he denied, Grappenhall pressed, and Woodcock, receiving from the left, made no mis- take. At this point the visitors monopolised the play, and Ravenscroft went through on his own and scored, time arriving with Grappenhall winning by 5-4. The home defence and left wing were good, whilst Barber, Woodward. Woodcock and Davies played well for Grappen- hall.

GOSSIP FROM THE CLUBS.

Warrington Association enthusiasts have now something of which they can be proud of. A iew weeks ago Cartwright, who hails from Lower Walton, was given a trial in the Manchester City First League team, where he performed very creditably. Now we have another player in Haywood, from the Lovely-lane district, play- ing with the Manchester United First League team. The last-named, only two seasons ago, played with the Latchford Christ Church Club, and then transferred himself to Hindley Central: Good luck to him in his new club! By the way, a few weeks ago there were five or six Warrington player's in the Hindley Cen- tral team, namely, Stubbs, Gifford, Shakeshaft, Hawthorn, Aluckley and Caldwell. Many of the public think that Warrington could raise a team to compete in the Lancashire Combination. There are many more who could be mentioned, who have the making of capable players if the opportunities came their way. Warrington would have to start in the Second Division before they could get into the First Division.

LABOUR TOPICS.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0325.txt | included=true | included =====
The Position of the Farm Servants and a Minimum Wage.

FARMERS' STARTLING ADMISSION.

BY "A WORKER."

Now the ironwork labourers are reaping the benefit of a minimum wage and shorter. hours others in Warrington are beginning to realise the value of trade unionism, and, from what 1 can learn, the winter • will not pass without agitations in other directions, which at present it is not Policy to enumerate. One of the worst paid sections in the town at Present is the brewery labourers. In 'other brewing centres the men are organising, with the view of better- ing their conditions, and one wonders how long those of Warrington will remain outside the fold. There are signs, however, that this class of underpaid worker is becoming restive, and I should not be surprised to learn that they decide to take action. Stockport recently haS set them a good example, to copy.

Wages on the Land. It would be interesting reading if we could secure statistics as to wages paid to farm ser- vants around Warrington. Doubtless these statistics would show many working long hours for a mere pittance. I notice one Cheshire far- mer admitted that wages of 13s. to 15s. per week were paid in that county, and Cheshire workers are no worse off than Lancashire farm servants. It came as a great surprise to learn further thatthe farmers of Cheshire had passed a reso- lution that every agricultural labourer should "secure a wage which will enable him to main- tain himself and family in comfort." This, coming from the Cheshire Chamber of Agricul, ture, which consists of "poor" landowners and farmers who cannot make "a living out of the soil," is surprising. The terms of the resolu- tion, however, do not indicate that they WILL pay a living wage to their employes, but still the confession which the resolution conveys that they do underpay their workers is of suffi- cient value in the meantime.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0326.txt | included=true | included =====
The Cloven Foot.

One was so startled at such a proposition. coming from the farming class that it required time to recover. Then re-reading the resolu- tion the cloven foot was alighted upon, and it was discovered in this phrase: "We do.not dis- agree in principle to the proposal to fix wages by some sort of wage tribunal. It must, •how- ever, be laid down that any proposals which may be made, shall provide that lower wages than the minimum may be paid to the partially efficient labourers." The members of the Che- shire Chamber of Agriculture do not say what tribunal should decide the lower wages. They themselves would insist upon that right, an we would find partially efficient labourers woul

be the title of all expert labourers and the agriculturists professing to be philan- thropists and ready to pay a. minimum "to really efficient men," but which class of men they would 'say they could not find; and, conse- qtwitly, if the farmers' scheme is adopted, their employes will not he in a position much better than they are at present. '

===== 0002601_19131129_art0327.txt | included=true | included =====
What Is Wanted.

Legislation may establish a minimum wage, as it has done in the tailoring and some other trades, but legislation alone cannot ensure that the farm labourers will be paid a wage of -20 s. per week, far less one that will keep the farm labourer ,and his family "in comfort." . It wants a driving force behind it to make the legislation operative, and the only motor power to supply the 'driving force is trade unionism. It might be said that farm servants, owing to their isolated positions, cannot be organised. They can. There are other trades, the members of which are quite as much isolated as farm servants, who are organised, and who have thereby materially improved their position, and the same could be done for the tillers of the soil. There is one thing which must be made clear, and it is if there is to be a minimum wage it must be a minimum for all, and it must not lie in the power of the landowners or the farmers to contract out of it upon the basis of what they deem to be efficiency.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0328.txt | included=true | included =====
The Cult of the Pianola.

The pianola recitals in the Parr Hall on Wed- nesday fifternoon and evening provided special pleasure to the music lovers of the district, who were fortunate enough to be present. The pro- grammes were arranged by Messrs. J. Smith and Son, Ltd., of Liverpool, and consisted of selections on the Steck Grand pianola piano and accompaniments on the Themodist pianola piano. It was evident\ that this system will become highly.popular, from the fact that those who are not acquainted with a single note of music can play by. the means of the pianola, the

most intricate and difficult pieces of-music with the style and expression which the composer intended; or it is possible for anybody to play a Nocturne of Chopin exactly as Paderewski has played it. The piano need not be a useless piece of furniture in a home where no piano player lives, but at the same time it can be used as an ordinary piano for the use of those who can play. The accompaniments to the singing of Miss Edina Thraves, and the con- certoS for violin and piano, were exceedingly effective. Miss Isabel McCullagh was the.' violinist.

Client,: "Lie called me a liar, a scoundrel, a coward and a thief." Lawyer: And which epithet is it you object to?"

It is a great human play,, full of life, pathos and humour. Mr. Sam Livesey brings a talented co tioiliy

THE CRAM AND BILLY."

===== 0002601_19131129_art0329.txt | included=true | included =====
Attractive Bill at the Hippodrome.

As tumblers, acrobats and equilibrists, the Craggs have earned an enviable reputation, and in their comedy sketch, "Billy," the new steward of the "Channel Queen," they have ample scope for their abilities. The antics of the new steward are most laughable, and the skilful way in which all the members of the company perform their evolutions is very commendable. "The Key" is an intensely human playlet, dealing with an unfaithful wife and the tor- turous way her husband kills her clandestine lover by locking him up in an iron safe, the wife imploring the while for the key. Scamp and Scamp are the limit in eccentric acrobatic feats, which they perform with astonishing skill and daring on the bars. Tom Child, a favourite with Hippodrome audiences, is as great a success as ever as the Yorkshire tenor, and his voice has lost none of its power or sweet- ness of tone. Yazo is a skilled violinist and plays the most intricate pieces with natural ease. He is assisted by Mdlle. Le Clere at the piano. Eric and Erne are patter comedians with many new gags, funny stories and puns. Annie Hornby makes full use of a rich con- tralto voice in pleasing numbers, and " Winki- pop " band Eddie are successful in a comedv, musical .and dancing act, and Will Andrews, as s Winkipop, is a clever banjoist.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0330.txt | included=true | included =====
COLONIAL BOYS.

An excellent bill has been arranged at the Hippodrome for next week, when the principal turn will be provided by Theo. Y. Johnson, in a brass band scena, entitled "Encamped" Other good turns will be: Mackinnon's Scottish Meisters, Charles Stephenson, Fred Ward, Hymar and Metford, Gerbola Trio, Irve Hay- man and Co., and Lily Young.

PATRIOTIC PLAY

Attracts at the Royal. "The British Flag," a stirring play, dealing with a series of events during the time of the Crimean War, has been successfully presented at the Theatre Royal this week. Mr. Harry Vail Vousden, as Charles Corton, is as successful as ever: Mr. C. g. Herobin is a capital Colonel Hazleton. The part of Florkne is in .the able hamfs of Miss Beatrice Malvern. Mr. Digby Hayes, as Monsieur de Montre, is the typical "froggy," whilst Mr. Robson has ample scope in the part of Francis Corton. The comedy is sup- plied by Mr. A. A. Roe, as ,James Brown, and Miss Lilian Murray. as Mrs. Brown.,

PARK PICTURE PALACE.

"PAULETTE'S NECKLACE."

Next week's programme at the Park Picture Palace contains for the first three days the ex- ceptionally good detective story Paulette's Necklace," the plot of which is taken from the recent jewel robbery. For the remainder of the week "The Banshee" is to form the attrac- tion.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0333.txt | included=true | included =====
"SIXTY YEARS A QUEEN."

The life and times of the late Queen ViC- toria abounds with so many appealing incidents to the Briton's heart that the much-discussed film dealing with her occupancy of England's throne, entitled "Sixty years a Queen," cannot help but preve of interest. This picture is to be exhibited at the Park Picture Palace shortly, where, in addition to the usual performances, special matinees are to be held for children. A. ,competition is being arranged for the scholars and money prizes will be presented.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0334.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PICTUREDROME.

Lord Lytton's masterpiece, "The Last Days of Pompeii," is to be shown at the Picturedrome next week, and is sure to prove interesting.

===== 0002601_19131129_art0335.txt | included=true | included =====
ANDREW'S GOOD PROGRAMME.

A very striking programme has been arranged for next week at this Bridge Foot Hall, where, for the first three days, popular attention will centre round the exciting film "Truth will out," which contains the incidents of a great explosion and the escape by the huge derrick. The latter half of the week will be similarly attractive, and the changed programme includes that most ab- sorbing of all picture plots—war and a mother's love embodied in the film "A War-time Mother's Sacrifice." Mirth will be introduced, and noth- ing but a pleasant night can be spent here

THE NEW PICTURE HOUSE.

A thrilling story of the sea, which will vividly recall the recent Volturno disaster will be shown at the New Picture House for the first part of next week, whilst for Thursday, Friday and Saturday "The Gangster," with its plot founded in the slums of New York, should- appeal to all.

COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. JUNIOR CLERKSHIPS IN CORPOE ATION DEPARTMENTS. An Examination for the purpose of forming a "Waiting List" for appointments to Junior Clerkships in Corporation Offices will shortly be held. Any vacancies arising before August Ist, 1914, will be filled from this list, Candidates passing the Examination will be furnished with a Certificate to that effect. Applicants must be the sons of ratepayers of the borough, and must be not less than 14, nor more than 15i years of age on December Ist, 1913. Forms of Application may be obtained from the 'Pown Clerk's Office, and no candidate will be admitted to the examination unless he has returned the form, duly completed, not later than noon on Monday, the Bth December, 1913. There is at present a vacancy for a Junior Clerk in the Museum Department. Dated this 28th day of November, 1913. J. LYON WHITTLE, Town Clerk. Town Hall, Warrington.

Constipation brings on headaches; when of long-standing it causts indigestion. Impure blood is another result of pro- longed constipation. Mother Seigel's Syrup is beyond all doubt the tried and proved remedy for constipatio,n. It has a gentle but very effective influence upon the. bowels, and soon restores them to healthy activity, without any violent purging or griping pains.
